● Voilà les trois fichiers. Rien de partagé n'a été touché, aucune commande git.

  Ce que la figure montre

  Sept régions au lieu de sept boîtes. L'entrepôt en bandeau tout en haut, parce
  que c'est la seule partie que le système ne choisit pas. Le véhicule à
  gauche, avec deux copies en ombre derrière pour dire qu'il y en a N. La carte
  partagée au centre, une seule instance. La décision à droite, une fois par
  cycle et par véhicule. Le guide sémantique au-dessus de la décision, marqué
  optionnel. La supervision en bas. Et une légende numérotée qui donne la boucle
  en cinq étapes.

  Les flux

  Dix flèches, chacune avec son étiquette sur une plaque blanche pour rester
  lisible aux croisements. La couleur porte le sens : vert pour ce qui est
  observé, ambre pour ce que la carte rend, orange pour un engagement, rouge
  pour une libération, violet pour l'avis optionnel, gris pour la supervision.

  ┌────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        Flux        │                     Étiquette                      │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ perception → carte │ observations : codes, étiquettes repérées, volume  │
  │                    │ libre et solide                                    │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ carte → génération │ ce qui est connu                                   │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ carte → notation   │ revendications, coéquipiers                        │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ guide → décision   │ avis, pondéré                                      │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ carte → guide      │ résumé de la carte, en pointillé                   │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ contrôleur → carte │ libéré à l'abandon                                 │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ sélection → carte  │ revendication, expire si non renouvelée            │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ sélection →        │ pose et chemin choisis                             │
  │ contrôleur         │                                                    │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ carte →            │ avancement                                         │
  │ supervision        │                                                    │
  └────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Les deux dernières passent par les couloirs du bas et remontent, donc la
  boucle se ferme visuellement. Les deux flèches du guide sont en pointillé ou à
  l'écart, pour qu'on voie sans lire la légende qu'on peut l'enlever.

  Les trois fichiers

  Le PNG fait 3120×2264, assez pour imprimer en pleine largeur A4. Le SVG est
  vectoriel, à mettre directement dans LaTeX avec includesvg ou à convertir en
  PDF. Le fichier prompt_architecture.md est le prompt textuel que tu m'as
  demandé : il décrit chaque bloc, chaque flèche et le style, assez précisément
  pour qu'un autre outil redessine la même chose. Le générateur est dans
  architecture.py si tu veux changer un libellé et relancer.

  Il reste 3.4 (conception détaillée des composants) et 3.5 (fonctionnement du
  système) à rédiger.

  complete inventory of a warehouse it has never seen. Several vehicles take    
  off inside a building whose layout has not been supplied, and from the first  
  seconds they build a common description of it from what their sensors         
  establish. As that description grows, it produces the work: labels spotted    
  from a distance become positions to be occupied and read, surfaces that have  
  been mapped but never seen from a readable angle become places to return to,  
  and the boundary of the known region becomes somewhere to extend. Each        
  vehicle prices these possibilities on a single scale, takes the best one it   
  can reach, announces it so that the others go elsewhere, flies there and      
  reads. What it establishes returns to the shared description, which produces  
  the next set of possibilities, and the loop turns until the inventory stops   
  growing, a condition the team evaluates for itself. Because every decision is 
  drawn from a description that is rewritten continuously rather than from a    
  plan prepared on the ground, the same loop also handles the situations a      
  fixed plan cannot: an obstacle appearing in an aisle is mapped and the paths  
  that crossed it are recomputed, a warehouse whose contents change is          
  corrected rather than merely extended, and a vehicle that stops flying        
  releases its announcements by expiry, so the work it was holding returns to   
  the others without anything having to detect the failure.                     
                                                                                
  Three properties hold together here, and no published work combines them.     
  Several vehicles cooperate, with the work redistributed during the flight     
  rather than fixed before take-off. They operate in a warehouse they do not    
  know, building their own representation of it as they go. And a pre-trained   
  vision-language model takes part in the decision itself, advising the team on 
  where to look next, a role so far confined to single ground robots searching  
  for one designated object. The system has seven elements, described below

● Tu as raison : ici c'est la carte d'identité du système, pas le déroulé. Le
  déroulé, c'est 3.5, et les composants, c'est 3.4.

  ---
  3.3 Overview of the Proposed Approach

  The system proposed in this work carries out a complete QR-code inventory of a
  warehouse whose layout is not given in advance. Four characteristics define
  it. It is a multi-vehicle system: several unmanned aerial vehicles fly at the
  same time in the same building, and cooperate rather than divide the space
  between them beforehand. It operates without pre-planning: no coverage path,
  no visiting order and no list of targets is prepared on the ground, since none
  of these can be written for a building that has not yet been described. Its
  division of work is dynamic: what each vehicle is responsible for is decided
  during the flight, from the state of the search at that moment, and is revised
  whenever that state changes. And its decisions are guided by meaning as well
  as by geometry: a pre-trained vision-language model takes part in choosing
  which part of the building the team examines next.

  These four characteristics rest on a single mechanism: one description of the
  building, written by the vehicles from what their sensors establish, and read
  by all of them. It is the only thing they share, and the only thing their
  decisions draw on. The work available at any moment is derived from it, the
  share of that work each vehicle has taken is recorded in it, and the advice of
  the language model is an opinion about it. Because it is rewritten
  continuously instead of being fixed once, it also determines the system's
  behaviour in the three situations a prepared plan cannot absorb. An obstacle
  that appears in an aisle is written into the description, and the routes that
  crossed it cease to exist. A warehouse whose contents have changed since a
  previous visit is corrected rather than merely extended. And a vehicle that
  stops flying no longer renews the share of work it had taken, which returns to
  the others without the failure having to be detected at all.

  Three of these properties exist separately in the literature, but not
  together. Cooperative aerial inventory has been demonstrated, with the work
  divided before take-off over a warehouse whose layout is already known. The
  online construction of a shared representation by a team has been
  demonstrated, for exploration and mapping, where the map is the objective
  rather than the support of a task to be carried out on the objects found. And
  decision-making driven by a pre-trained vision-language model has been
  demonstrated, so far on a single ground robot sent to find one object
  designated by a human, in an indoor scene of domestic scale. The system
  presented here brings the three together: several aerial vehicles, in a
  warehouse they do not know, with a language model taking part in their
  decisions.

  The system is made of seven elements. They are shown in Figure 3.1 and
  described one by one in Section 3.4; the way they run together over the course
  of a mission is treated in Section 3.5.

  ---
  Ce que j'ai changé. Le premier paragraphe ne raconte plus le décollage et les
  premières secondes ; il nomme les quatre caractéristiques du système —
  plusieurs drones, pas de préplanification, répartition dynamique, guidage par
  un modèle de langage. Le deuxième ne suit plus la boucle pas à pas ; il dit
  d'où viennent ces quatre caractéristiques (une description partagée et
  réécrite) et présente les trois cas difficiles comme des conséquences de ce
  choix, pas comme des scènes. J'ai enlevé « prices these possibilities », «
  flies there and reads », « the loop turns until » : tout ça appartient à 3.5.
  Le paragraphe de nouveauté est gardé, avec la précision que tu avais demandée
  sur le robot terrestre piloté par un humain. Et la dernière phrase annonce les
  deux sections suivantes au lieu de la seule 3.4.

  ---
  3.3 Overview of the Proposed Approach

  The system carries out a complete QR-code inventory of a warehouse whose
  layout is not supplied. It is flown by a team of identical aerial vehicles,
  each running the same software and deciding for itself; there is no leader, no
  central planner, and no ground station that hands out work. Nothing in the
  design depends on how many vehicles are present. The vehicles are flown by
  real autopilots rather than displaced by the simulator, so every decision the
  system produces has to be a decision that can physically be flown, at a speed
  and along a path the aircraft can hold.

  Nothing is prepared before take-off — no coverage path, no visiting order, no
  list of targets, not even a plan of the aisles. What takes the place of the
  plan is a description of the building that the vehicles write themselves as
  they fly and that all of them read. It is the only object they share and the
  only thing their decisions consult; there is no message protocol between them
  and no agreement to reach. It is volumetric rather than a floor plan, because
  racks have levels and a code sits at a height as much as at a position. It
  records which volumes are solid, free, or still unobserved; which surfaces
  have been observed and from which direction; every label the team has come
  across, with its position, its orientation, its identifier, and whether it has
  been read; and where the other vehicles are, together with the targets they
  have taken. Coverage in it is angular, not merely spatial: a rack mapped from
  the far end of an aisle has been observed, but not from anywhere a code on it
  could be read, and the system keeps that difference instead of declaring the
  rack finished.

  The mission can be driven at all because of an asymmetry in what the cameras
  can do. A label is visible from several metres away but legible only from
  close range and within a narrow band of angles. Detection and reading are
  therefore two separate functions, and between them lies the state that carries
  the whole mission: a label that has been spotted but not yet read. Such a
  label is placed in three dimensions before anything has been decoded, from the
  distance measured along the ray that reaches it, so it becomes a target with
  a position and a facing direction while its content is still unknown. Reading
  it later is close to a certainty rather than a gamble, and an identifier found
  twice on two faces of the same panel is counted once, since the inventory is
  a set of identifiers and not a count of surfaces.

  Three kinds of work exist at any moment: read a label already spotted, go and
  observe a surface never seen from a readable direction, and extend the
  boundary of the known region. The system writes all three in the same form, as
  a position and a viewing direction, and prices them on one scale — what each
  is expected to return, less what it costs to reach, less the penalties that
  stop two vehicles converging on the same place. Because they compete directly,
  the team is never made to choose between exploring and reading as separate
  phases: whichever is worth more at that instant wins, and the balance moves on
  its own as the building becomes known. A candidate survives only if a route
  to it exists through space already established as free, so the system never
  commits to something it cannot reach; unknown space is not forbidden but
  expensive, which still lets a vehicle push into an untouched aisle when
  nothing better remains.

  Vehicles keep out of each other's way by announcing, in the shared
  description, the target they have taken, and by avoiding what others have
  announced — and the announcement expires unless it is renewed. There is no
  negotiation, no division into sectors, and no mechanism whose job is to notice
  that a vehicle has failed. A commitment is revocable from the vehicle's side
  as well: when a route is cut or progress stops, the vehicle gives the target
  up and releases it immediately, and a target that defeats several attempts is
  set aside. These few rules are what produce the system's behaviour when
  conditions change. An obstacle that appears in an aisle becomes solid volume,
  and the routes that passed through it stop existing. A warehouse whose
  contents have moved since an earlier mission is corrected rather than merely
  added to. And a vehicle that stops flying simply stops renewing, so the work
  it was holding returns to the others.

  A pre-trained vision-language model takes part in the decision on top of this
  geometry. It is shown what the team currently knows and asked which area
  deserves attention next and from which side to approach the rack there; its
  answer enters the same scale as everything else, multiplied by a weight. It
  runs at its own pace and is never waited for. Setting that weight to zero
  removes it entirely and leaves a system that still works — the guidance is an
  addition to something complete, not a dependency of it.

  The three activities run at three different rates: flight control at the rate
  of the aircraft, the decision once per cycle, the advice whenever it happens
  to be ready, so no decision ever holds up the flight. The mission ends when
  the team judges from the shared description that it has ended — a share of the
  expected inventory read, or no new identifier for long enough — and not when
  a timer runs out.

  Each of these traits has precedent on its own. What has no precedent is the
  combination: several aerial vehicles cooperating inside a warehouse none of
  them has seen, writing their own description of it as they go, dividing the
  work among themselves in flight and revising that division continuously, and
  taking advice from a pre-trained vision-language model — a role so far
  reserved to a single ground robot sent by a person to find one designated
  object.

  The system is made of seven elements. They appear in Figure 3.1 and are
  described one by one in Section 3.4; the way they run together over a mission
  is the subject of Section 3.5.

  ---

● 3.3 Overview of the Proposed Approach

  The system carries out a complete QR-code inventory of a warehouse whose
  layout is not supplied. It is a multi-UAV system, made of identical vehicles
  with no leader and no central planner: each one decides for itself, and the
  number of vehicles is not fixed by the design. Its planning is online —
  nothing is prepared before take-off — and its allocation of work is dynamic,
  decided in flight and revised continuously. Its mapping is online too, and the
  map is shared: the vehicles build a single three-dimensional description of
  the building and all read it. Their coordination is implicit, through that
  shared description, with no communication protocol between them. The system is
  fault-tolerant, without any component whose job is to detect a failure, and
  adaptive to change, whether the warehouse has been modified since an earlier
  mission or an obstacle appears during this one. It decides for itself when the
  inventory is complete. Its decisions are semantically guided: a pre-trained
  vision-language model takes part in them, and can be removed without the
  system ceasing to work. And it is physically realistic, since the vehicles are
  flown by real autopilots.

  The system is made of seven elements. They appear in Figure 3.1 and are
  described one by one in Section 3.4; the way they run together over a mission
  is the subject of Section 3.5.

● 3.3 Overview of the Proposed Approach

  The proposed system performs warehouse inventory inspection in an environment
  whose layout is not known in advance. It is a multi-UAV system: the inspection
  is carried out by a team of identical aerial vehicles operating
  simultaneously in the same building. Its organisation is decentralised, with
  no leader and no central planner; every vehicle runs the same software and
  reaches its own decisions. The number of vehicles is not a parameter of the
  design, so the same system operates unchanged with a larger or a smaller team.

  The system performs online planning. No coverage path, visiting order or list
  of targets is prepared before take-off, since none of these can be established
  for a building that has not yet been described. Task allocation is dynamic:
  the share of the inspection each vehicle is responsible for is determined
  during the flight and revised continuously as the state of the search evolves.
  Mapping is likewise online, and the resulting map is shared: the vehicles
  jointly construct a single three-dimensional description of the building,
  which is volumetric rather than a floor plan, as racks have several levels and
  a code is defined by a height as much as by a position. Coordination between
  vehicles is implicit and passes entirely through this shared description; no
  communication protocol, negotiation or consensus mechanism is required.

  The system is fault-tolerant, and contains no component whose function is to
  detect the failure of a vehicle. It is adaptive to change, whether the
  contents of the warehouse have been modified since an earlier mission or an
  obstacle appears during the current one. It is self-terminating: the end of
  the mission is determined by the team from the state of the inspection, not by
  a predefined time budget. Its decisions are semantically guided, in that a
  pre-trained vision-language model participates in them, and this guidance is
  optional — its removal leaves a system that remains fully operational.
  Finally, the system is physically realistic: the vehicles are flown by real
  autopilots, so that every decision it produces is one that can be executed in
  flight.

  The system is made of seven elements. They appear in Figure 3.1 and are
  described one by one in Section 3.4; the way they run together over a mission
  is the subject of Section 3.5.

  Conception = quoi et pourquoi. Implémentation = avec quoi et quelles valeurs.

  quand tu changes de bibliothèque, de machine ou de version, elle va en
  Implémentation.

● Voilà 3.4.1 avec le découpage. Tout ce qui est bibliothèque, modèle, seuil ou
  milliseconde est parti au chapitre implémentation.

  ---
  3.4.1 Perception

  a. Role and formalisation

  Perception is the only channel through which the system acquires information
  about the building. It receives, at each cycle and for one vehicle, the images
  of its two lateral cameras, the pose of each camera at the instant of
  capture, and one LiDAR revolution. It produces three things, and writes them
  into the shared map. Its mapping is formalised as:

  $$f_{\mathcal{P}} : \mathcal{I} \times \mathcal{T} \times \mathcal{L}
  \longrightarrow \mathcal{R} \times \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{V}, \qquad
  f_{\mathcal{P}}(I, T, L) = (R, S, V) \tag{3.1}$$

  where $R$ is the set of labels whose identifier has been decoded, each with a
  position and a surface normal; $S$ is the set of labels detected but not
  decoded, each with a position and the direction from which it was seen; and
  $V$ is the volume established as free or solid.

  The component is subject to an asymmetric cost of error. An identifier read
  wrongly corrupts the inventory permanently, since nothing downstream ever
  re-examines a label declared read. A target reported where none exists costs
  one flight. A missed observation costs nothing but a later opportunity.
  Perception is therefore designed to be conservative on $R$, selective on $S$,
  and permissive on what it declines to report.

  b. Design decision 1 — detecting and decoding are two distinct functions

  A QR code stops being readable long before it stops being visible, and the two
  usual explanations for a failed read — the code is too far, or it is seen too
  obliquely — are the same phenomenon. A square inclined by an angle $\alpha$
  projects onto the image exactly as a square $1/\cos\alpha$ times more distant.
  Both limits therefore collapse into a single quantity, the apparent distance:

  $$d_{\text{app}} = \frac{d}{\cos \alpha} \tag{3.2}$$

  with $d$ the distance from the camera to the centre of the code and $\alpha$
  the angle between the viewing direction and the normal of the panel. Measured
  across the full range of poses, the reading rate depends on this single
  quantity and on nothing else, and it defines a reading envelope

  $$\mathcal{E} = {, (d, \alpha) ;:; 1.5\ \text{m} \le d_{\text{app}} \le 4.0\
  \text{m} ,}. \tag{3.3}$$

  The upper bound is optical. The lower bound is not: closer than about one
  metre, a small lateral error produces a large incidence angle, and a vehicle
  cannot hold its heading precisely enough. This envelope is the single most
  structural quantity of the whole system, because every reading pose the
  decision component later generates is placed inside it.

  Detection is under no such constraint, since it does not require the
  individual modules of the code to be resolved. This gap between the two ranges
  is what makes the mission tractable: a vehicle travelling along an aisle can
  register the existence of every label in that aisle while being able to read
  only those it passes close to. Perception is therefore built as two functions
  with a third state between them:

  ┌─────────┬───────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │  State  │    What is established    │          What is written          │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Read    │ the identifier            │ identifier, position, surface     │
  │         │                           │ normal                            │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Spotted │ a label exists at this    │ position, direction of approach   │
  │         │ place                     │                                   │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Nothing │ —                         │ —                                 │
  └─────────┴───────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  The intermediate state is what drives the mission. A spotted label is not a
  hypothesis to be investigated but a target whose reading is close to certain,
  since the only thing still missing is the approach. Converting spotted labels
  into read labels is, for the decision component, the cheapest work available,
  and this is a direct consequence of the design choice made here.

  c. Design decision 2 — the image gives direction and identity, never distance

  The apparent size of a label cannot be used to recover its distance, because
  the labels are not of a single size: each panel occupies a fixed fraction of
  the face of the carton that carries it, and cartons differ. Assuming a nominal
  size would produce distance errors of a factor of three, which would place
  observations inside neighbouring racks.

  The component is therefore built on a division of labour between its two
  sensors. The image supplies the direction and the identity; the LiDAR supplies
  the range. A pixel is converted into a ray in the world frame, and the range
  is read along that ray. The consequence is that the placement of an
  observation no longer depends on any assumption about the object observed,
  which is what allows labels of unknown size to be positioned correctly.

  For a label that has been read, the four corners of the code are available,
  and the orientation of the panel is recovered from them by the pose
  formulation for planar square markers. Orientation, unlike distance, is
  scale-invariant: it can be obtained from the corners without knowing the
  physical size. This is why the two quantities are obtained from two different
  sources rather than from one.

  An observation whose incidence exceeds 60° is discarded even when the code was
  successfully decoded, because beyond that angle the four corners no longer
  determine a reliable normal, and a wrong normal would send a later vehicle to
  the wrong side of a rack.

  d. Design decision 3 — two placement regimes, separated by the resolution of 
  the LiDAR

  A label that has only been spotted is delivered as a region of the image, not
  as four corners, so no orientation can be recovered and the range must come
  entirely from elsewhere. Two regimes are required, and the boundary between
  them is a property of the sensor rather than a tuning choice: the angular
  spacing of the LiDAR is constant, so the distance between neighbouring rays
  grows linearly with range. Past a certain distance the nearest ray to a given
  direction no longer lands on the intended object, but on the neighbouring
  carton or, through a gap in the rack, on the back wall.

  - Within the reliable range of the LiDAR, the position is the point where the
  ray meets the measured surface.
  - Beyond it, the position is taken from the shared map instead: the first cell
  already established as occupied along the direction of the detection. The map
  has accumulated many revolutions from many poses, so it is dense where a
  single revolution is sparse.

  This is the first place in the system where the map is used as a sensor rather
  than as a memory, and it is a deliberate design choice: rather than adding a
  second range sensor, the component exploits the fact that the information it
  needs has already been collected by the team.

  A plausibility test then guards both regimes. The size of the detected region
  gives a coarse distance, unreliable in value but reliable in order of
  magnitude, and a placement that disagrees with it by more than a wide factor
  is rejected. The purpose is not precision but the detection of one specific
  failure — a ray that has passed through a hole in the rack and returned a
  point far behind the label.

  Finally, a spotted label is written with a direction of approach pointing back
  towards the camera that observed it. This is the only part of the observation
  that survives the absence of an orientation, and it is sufficient: it tells a
  later vehicle from which side the rack must be addressed.

  e. Design decision 4 — the detector must be learned, not geometric

  Detecting a dark square pattern by classical image processing is inexpensive
  and works at long range. It fails on the one criterion that matters here: in a
  warehouse containing no codes at all, a geometric detector reports a panel on
  more than a quarter of the images, confusing cartons, uprights and shadows.
  Since every spotted label produces a flight, a component with that false-alarm
  rate would generate more wasted journeys than useful ones, and the system
  would be slower with it than without it.

  The requirement placed on the detector is therefore stated as a pair, not as a
  single figure: it must extend the detection range well beyond the reading 
  envelope while reporting a label in an empty scene at a rate low enough that a
  wasted flight remains rare. A learned detector is retained because it is the
  only construction that satisfies both. A second requirement follows from the
  setting: the detector is also asked to report cartons, which do not need to be
  read but establish that a region contains inventory, and this fills the
  semantic channel of the map used later by the guide.

  The detector is trained without manual annotation. In simulation the position
  of every carton and every panel is known, so each object can be projected into
  the image from the exact camera pose. Projection alone is insufficient, since
  an object hidden behind another would be labelled as visible; visibility is
  therefore established physically, by casting rays at sampled points of each
  surface and accepting the point only when the first object reached is the one
  that owns it. Partially hidden objects are neither positive examples nor
  counted as errors. The training warehouses and the evaluation warehouses are
  disjoint, so that the detector is never measured on a building it has been
  shown.

  f. The cycle

  Algorithm 3.1 — One perception cycle, for one vehicle
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  input   two images with their camera poses, one LiDAR revolution
  output  writes into the shared map

   1  integrate the LiDAR revolution into the occupancy layer
   2  publish the vehicle position into the teammate layer
   3  hold the current images and poses; use those of the previous
      cycle, since the renderer delivers one frame behind the physics
   4  for each camera:
   5      decode the image
   6      for each decoded identifier of the expected format:
   7          recover the panel orientation from the four corners
   8          discard if the incidence exceeds the limit
   9          take the range along the ray of the code centre
  10          discard if outside the reading envelope
  11          write (identifier, position, normal) as READ
  12      if this camera is the one scheduled for detection this cycle:
  13          for each detected region:
  14              place it by the LiDAR, or by the map beyond its
                  reliable range
  15              discard if it fails the plausibility test
  16              write it as SPOTTED, facing this camera
  17              mark the semantic channel for a detected carton
  18      integrate the camera cone into the oriented coverage layer
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Two design decisions are visible in this algorithm. Line 3 pairs each image
  with the pose of the previous cycle rather than the current one: the renderer
  is one frame behind the physics, and using the current pose would displace
  every observation by the distance travelled in a cycle. Line 12 runs detection
  on one camera per cycle, alternating between them, while decoding runs on
  both every cycle. When the two cannot both be afforded, reading is preserved
  and detection is delayed, because a label spotted one cycle later is still
  spotted, whereas a label not read while the vehicle was in position requires a
  second journey.

  g. Summary of the design decisions

  ┌────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          Decision          │               Justification                │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Separate detection from    │ reading and detection have ranges that     │
  │ decoding, with an          │ differ by a factor of two; the gap between │
  │ intermediate spotted state │  them is what the mission exploits         │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Express distance and angle │ they are the same phenomenon; one envelope │
  │  as one apparent distance  │  replaces two independent limits           │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Take the range from the    │ labels differ in physical size; a size     │
  │ LiDAR, never from the      │ assumption would misplace observations by  │
  │ apparent size              │ metres                                     │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Recover the orientation    │ orientation is scale-invariant, distance   │
  │ from the four corners      │ is not                                     │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Reject an observation      │ a wrong normal sends a vehicle to the      │
  │ beyond a limiting          │ wrong side of a rack                       │
  │ incidence                  │                                            │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Place distant detections   │ LiDAR rays diverge with range; the map is  │
  │ from the map rather than   │ dense where one revolution is sparse       │
  │ the LiDAR                  │                                            │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                            │ a geometric detector's false-alarm rate    │
  │ Use a learned detector     │ would produce more wasted flights than     │
  │                            │ useful ones                                │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Prefer decoding over       │ a delayed detection costs nothing; a       │
  │ detection when both cannot │ missed reading costs a second journey      │
  │  be afforded               │                                            │
  └────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  h. Figures

  $\mathcal{E}$ marked, which is the measurement that fixes every reading pose
  in the system. Figure 3.3 — the three output states and what each writes into
  the map, since this distinction governs everything in Section 3.4.3.

● Tu as raison, je me suis trompé : la taille du panneau est connue et est

  ---
  3.4.1 Perception

  a. What the component produces, and why its errors are not symmetric

  Perception is the only channel through which the system acquires information
  about the building. At each cycle, for one vehicle, it receives the images of
  the two lateral cameras, the pose each camera held at the instant of capture,
  and one LiDAR revolution. It produces three outputs and writes them into the
  shared map:

  $$f_{\mathcal{P}} : \mathcal{I} \times \mathcal{T} \times \mathcal{L}
  \longrightarrow \mathcal{R} \times \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{V}, \qquad
  f_{\mathcal{P}}(I, T, L) = (R, S, V) \tag{3.1}$$

  where $R$ holds the labels whose identifier has been decoded, each with a
  position and a surface normal; $S$ holds the labels detected without being
  decoded, each with a position and the direction from which it was seen; and
  $V$ is the volume established as free or as solid.

  These three outputs do not carry the same risk. A wrong identifier in $R$ is
  permanent: nothing downstream ever re-examines a label declared read, so the
  error survives to the end of the mission and enters the final inventory. A
  wrong entry in $S$ costs one flight, which is expensive but recoverable. An
  observation that perception declines to make costs nothing at all, since the
  vehicle will pass again. The whole component is shaped by this asymmetry: it
  is built to refuse rather than to guess, and each of the four filters
  described in Section g exists to convert a possible error into a missed
  observation.

  b. Seeing is not reading, and the gap between the two is what the mission 
  exploits

  The first difficulty is that a code stops being readable long before it stops
  being visible. The two explanations normally given for a failed read — the
  code is too far away, or it is seen too obliquely — turn out to be one and the
  same. A square inclined by an angle $\alpha$ projects onto the image exactly
  as a square $1/\cos\alpha$ times more distant would; its modules occupy
  proportionally fewer pixels in both cases. Distance and angle therefore
  collapse into a single quantity, the apparent distance:

  $$d_{\text{app}} = \frac{d}{\cos \alpha} \tag{3.2}$$

  Measured across the full range of poses, the reading rate depends on this
  quantity and on nothing else. It defines a reading envelope:

  $$\mathcal{E} = {, (d, \alpha) ;:; 1.5\ \text{m} \le d_{\text{app}} \le 4.0\
  \text{m} ,} \tag{3.3}$$

  whose two bounds have different natures. The upper one is optical: past four
  metres the modules no longer occupy enough pixels. The lower one is not
  optical at all but mechanical: closer than about a metre, a lateral error of a
  few centimetres produces a large incidence angle, and a vehicle in flight
  cannot hold its heading tightly enough to stay in front of the panel. The
  envelope is the most structural quantity in the entire system, because every
  reading pose that the decision component will later generate is placed inside
  it, and because it is what makes a rack readable from an aisle only 3.9 m wide
  — there, it is the angle rather than the distance that lets one position
  cover several cartons.

  Detection is under no such constraint, since it does not require the modules
  to be resolved: a label remains a recognisable object long after it has become
  an unreadable one. This gap between the two ranges is the opening the whole
  mission goes through. A vehicle travelling down an aisle can register the
  existence of every label in that aisle while being able to read only the few
  it passes close to. Perception is therefore not one function but two, with a
  third state lying between them:

  ┌─────────┬───────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │  State  │ What has been established │   What is written into the map    │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Read    │ the identifier itself     │ identifier, position, surface     │
  │         │                           │ normal                            │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Spotted │ a label exists at this    │ position, direction of approach   │
  │         │ place                     │                                   │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Nothing │ —                         │ —                                 │
  └─────────┴───────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  The intermediate state is what gives the mission its momentum. A spotted label
  is not a hypothesis to be checked but a target whose reading is close to
  certain, since the only thing still missing is the approach. It converts an
  act of exploration, whose return is uncertain, into an act of collection,
  whose return is nearly guaranteed — and this is what the decision component
  will later price higher than anything else.

  c. A detector that sees far is worthless if it also sees what is not there

  Making the detection range long is not difficult. Detecting a dark square by
  classical image processing — an adaptive threshold, contour extraction, a
  convexity and aspect test, a dark-fraction test — is inexpensive and works
  well past the reading envelope. It fails on the only criterion that matters
  here. In a warehouse emptied of all its codes, such a detector reports a panel
  on more than a quarter of the images, mistaking cartons, rack uprights and
  shadows for labels. Since every spotted label produces a journey, a component
  with that false-alarm rate would send vehicles to more empty places than real
  ones, and the system would finish the inventory faster with the detector
  switched off.

  The requirement is therefore stated as a pair rather than as a single figure.
  The detector must extend the detection range well beyond the reading envelope,
  while reporting a label in an empty scene rarely enough that a wasted flight 
  stays exceptional. No threshold applied to a geometric detector satisfies both
  at once: raising it to suppress the false alarms also removes the distant
  true detections, which are precisely what the component exists to provide. A
  learned detector is retained because it separates the two, having learned what
  a label looks like in this setting rather than what a dark quadrilateral
  looks like anywhere.

  The training set is built without a single manual annotation, and this is what
  makes the choice practical rather than merely desirable. In simulation the
  position of every carton and every panel is known, so each object can be
  projected into the image from the exact camera pose. Projection alone would be
  wrong, since an object hidden behind another would be annotated as visible;
  visibility is therefore established physically, by casting rays at sampled
  points of each surface and accepting a point only when the first object the
  ray reaches is the one that owns it. The annotation box encloses the visible
  points rather than the whole object, and an object too largely hidden is
  marked as ignored, counting neither as an example nor as an error. Training
  warehouses and evaluation warehouses are disjoint, so the detector is never
  measured on a building it has been shown.

  The detector is also asked to report a second class, cartons, which are never
  read. A detected carton establishes that a region holds inventory, which is
  exactly what the semantic channel of the map needs in order to tell a full
  rack from an empty one without anything having been decoded there.

  d. Knowing that a label exists is not knowing where it is

  An image gives a direction, never a position. Turning a detection into an
  entry in a three-dimensional map requires a range along that direction, and
  three sources are available.

  The apparent size of the code is the first. It is usable here, because the
  panels are not arbitrary: the generator places on each carton face a square
  whose side is a fixed fraction of that face, and in the warehouses used every
  panel measures 0.40 m. The size is known, and the component does use it. But
  it carries a trap that must be stated, because it silently biases everything
  downstream. A QR code is printed with a quiet margin around it, two modules on
  each side of a twenty-one module symbol, and every decoder returns the
  corners of the symbol, not of the panel. Treating the returned quadrilateral
  as the full panel therefore over-estimates every distance by

  $$\frac{25}{21} - 1 \approx 19\ %. \tag{3.4}$$

  The component consequently never works with the panel side but with the code
  side, $c = 0.40 \times 21/25 = 0.336$ m.

  The pose recovered from the four corners is the second source. When a code has
  been decoded, its four corners are available, and the pose formulation for
  planar square markers returns both a rotation and a translation. This
  decomposes into two quantities of very different robustness. The rotation is
  determined by the shape of the quadrilateral alone and does not depend on the
  assumed side length at all: a wrong size would scale the translation but leave
  the rotation untouched. The translation does depend on it, linearly.
  Orientation obtained this way is therefore trustworthy under any assumption
  about the panel format, while distance obtained this way is only as
  trustworthy as that assumption.

  The LiDAR range along the ray is the third. The pixel at the centre of the
  code is converted into a ray in the world frame, and the range is read from
  the LiDAR point that passes closest to it. This is a direct measurement: it
  assumes nothing about the object, its size, or its format.

  The design retains the third for distance and the second for orientation, and
  combines them: the image supplies the direction and the identity, the corners 
  supply the orientation, the LiDAR supplies the range. The reason is not
  accuracy — the two distance sources agree to within a centimetre on the
  measurements of Section 3.x — but independence. A system whose placement
  depends on a printed format is a system that breaks when the format changes,
  and the warehouse the vehicles are meant to handle is by definition one they
  have not been shown. Keeping the range free of that assumption costs nothing
  here and removes a hidden coupling between perception and the scene.

  For a label that has only been spotted, the question is harder, because the
  detector returns a region and not four corners: there is no orientation to
  recover, and the coarse distance implied by the size of the region cannot be
  trusted, since the region is loose around the code and may be clipped by an
  obstruction. The range must come from the LiDAR alone — and here the sensor
  imposes a boundary that is a property of its construction rather than a tuning
  choice. The LiDAR's angular spacing is constant, so the distance between
  neighbouring rays grows linearly with range; at eight metres two neighbouring
  rays are already separated by more than a map cell. Past that point, the ray
  nearest a given direction no longer lands on the intended object but on the
  carton beside it or, through a gap in the rack, on the wall behind. Two
  regimes are therefore needed:

  - within the LiDAR's reliable range, the position is the point where the ray
  meets the measured surface;
  - beyond it, the position is taken from the shared map instead — the first
  cell along that direction already established as occupied.

  The second regime is the first place in the system where the map is read as a
  sensor rather than as a memory, and it is a deliberate choice. A single
  revolution is sparse at eight metres, but the map has accumulated many
  revolutions from many poses and many vehicles, so it is dense exactly where
  one revolution is not. Rather than adding a second range sensor, the component
  uses information the team has already paid for.

  A plausibility test guards both regimes, and it is the one place where the
  nominal size earns its keep. The size of the detected region gives a distance
  that is unreliable in value but correct in order of magnitude, and a placement
  disagreeing with it by more than a wide factor is rejected. The window is
  deliberately loose, since the region is not the code; its purpose is not
  precision but the detection of one specific failure — a ray that has passed
  through a gap in a rack and returned a point far behind the label it was aimed
  at.

  Finally, a spotted label is written facing the camera that observed it. This
  is the only directional information that survives the absence of corners, and
  it is enough: it records the side of the rack from which the label will have
  to be approached, which is what a later vehicle needs in order to place itself
  inside the envelope on the first attempt.

  e. What the camera saw and where it was are not the same instant

  The renderer delivers a frame one step behind the physics. Reading an image
  together with the pose the vehicle holds at the moment the image arrives
  therefore attaches every observation to a position the vehicle has already
  left, displacing it by the distance travelled in one cycle. The component
  keeps the two most recent instants and always pairs an image with the pose and
  the LiDAR revolution of the previous one. The correction is small in metres
  and decisive in effect: without it, the same panel observed while approaching
  and while receding is written twice, in two different places, and the map
  records two labels where one exists.

  f. Two cameras, and a budget that does not stretch

  The vehicle carries two lateral cameras rather than one forward camera, and
  the reason is the geometry of the building. Labels sit on the faces of cartons
  on both sides of an aisle, so a vehicle flying down the middle has readable
  surfaces on its left and on its right and nothing of interest ahead. Two
  lateral cameras let one traversal of an aisle serve both racks; a forward
  camera would require the vehicle to turn towards each face in turn, and the
  reading envelope is narrow enough that this would double the number of poses.

  Decoding and detection do not cost the same, and when the graphics card is
  also rendering, the two together exceed what a cycle can afford. The rule
  adopted is asymmetric: decoding runs on both images every cycle; detection 
  runs on one camera per cycle, alternating. The justification lies in what each
  failure costs. A label spotted one cycle later is still spotted, and the
  vehicle is not yet going there anyway. A label not decoded while the vehicle
  was in position in front of it requires a second journey to the same place.
  Reading is therefore never sacrificed, and detection is.

  Decoding also runs on the whole image rather than on the regions the detector
  proposes. This keeps the two paths independent: a label the detector misses
  can still be read, and a failure of the learned component degrades the range
  of the system without ever costing it an identifier.

  g. The four things perception refuses to write

  Filter: Identifier format
  What it rejects: a decoded string that does not match the inventory format
  What it prevents: the warehouse carries other QR codes on its signage; without

  this filter they enter the inventory as cartons
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Filter: Limiting incidence
  What it rejects: an observation seen at more than 60° from the panel normal
  What it prevents: past that angle the four corners no longer determine a
  reliable normal, and a wrong normal sends a later vehicle to the wrong side
  of the rack
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Filter: Reading envelope
  What it rejects: a placement whose range falls outside $\mathcal{E}$
  What it prevents: a code decoded from an implausible distance is a
  corner-localisation error, not a reading
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Filter: Plausibility of the placement
  What it rejects: a spotted label whose position contradicts the coarse
  distance
  from its region
  What it prevents: a ray that has gone through a gap in a rack places a target
  inside a wall

  Each of these turns a possible error into a missed observation, which is the
  trade the asymmetry of Section a demands.

  h. The cycle

  Algorithm 3.1 — One perception cycle, for one vehicle
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  input   two images with their camera poses, one LiDAR revolution
  output  writes into the shared map

   1  integrate the LiDAR revolution into the occupancy layer
   2  publish the vehicle position into the teammate layer
   3  store the current images, poses and point cloud
   4  if fewer than two instants are stored: return
   5  take the images of now, with the poses and cloud of the
      previous instant                          ▹ one frame of render lag
   6  for each of the two cameras:
   7      decode the whole image
   8      for each identifier matching the inventory format:
   9          recover the panel orientation from the four corners
  10          discard if the incidence exceeds the limit
  11          take the range from the LiDAR along the ray of the centre
  12          discard if the range falls outside the envelope
  13          write (identifier, position, normal) as READ
  14      if this camera is the one scheduled for detection:
  15          for each detected region:
  16              place it by the LiDAR, or by the map beyond the
                  LiDAR's reliable range
  17              discard if it fails the plausibility test
  18              write it as SPOTTED, facing this camera
  19              mark the semantic channel for a detected carton
  20      integrate the camera cone into the oriented coverage layer
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  i. Figures

  Figure 3.2 — the reading rate against apparent distance, with the envelope
  $\mathcal{E}$ marked; it is the measurement that fixes every reading pose the
  system will ever generate. Figure 3.3 — the three sources of range for one
  detection, showing which quantity each one assumes and which the component
  retains. Figure 3.4 — the two placement regimes on a plan of an aisle, with
  the LiDAR rays diverging and the map taking over past the point where they
  separate by more than a cell.

  sources sont d'accord au centimètre — c'est de ne pas coupler le placement au

  ---
  3.4.1 Perception ($\mathcal{P}$)

  Opening paragraph — role in one sentence, inputs, outputs, and the mapping
  $$f_{\mathcal{P}} : \mathcal{I}^2 \times \mathcal{T} \times \mathcal{L} \to
  \mathcal{R} \times \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{V} \tag{3.1}$$

  Table 3.1 — Notation used in the perception component. $I$, $K$,
  $(\mathbf{p}_c, R_c)$, $\mathbf{u}$, $\rho(\mathbf{u})$, $L={(\mathbf{d}i, 
  r_i)}$, $\mathcal{C}$ (point cloud), $s$ (panel side), $c$ (code side), $Q$ 
  (four corners), $\ell$ (identifier), $B$ (detection box), $d{app}$, $\alpha$,
  $\mathcal{E}$, $\mathcal{M}$ (shared map).

  Figure 3.2 — Perception pipeline. Two parallel paths (decode / detect) from
  the same image, converging into the map; the LiDAR feeding both the range
  estimate and the occupancy layer.

  ---
  3.4.1.1 Sensor Model

  - Camera. Back-projection of a pixel into a world ray:
  $$\rho(\mathbf{u}) = R_c \cdot
  \iota!\left(\frac{K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}}{\lVert K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}
  \rVert}\right) \tag{3.2}$$
  with $\iota$ the change of axis convention between the camera and the world.
  - LiDAR. One revolution $L$, point cloud $\mathcal{C} = {\mathbf{p}c + r_i 
  \mathbf{d}i}$ (3.3); ray spacing $\delta(r) = r,\Delta\theta$ and reliable 
  range $r{\max}$ defined by $\delta(r{\max}) = $ one map cell (3.4).
  - Temporal alignment. Render latency: image $I_t$ paired with $(\mathbf{p}c, 
  R_c, \mathcal{C}){t-1}$ (3.5).
  - Figure 3.3 — geometry of a camera ray and the closest LiDAR point.

  3.4.1.2 Reading Envelope

  - Apparent distance $d_{app} = d / \cos\alpha$ (3.6).
  - Envelope $\mathcal{E} = {(d,\alpha) : d_{\min} \le d_{app} \le d_{\max}}$
  (3.7), with the nature of each bound (optical / mechanical).
  - Table 3.2 — reading rate per band of apparent distance (the measurement that
  fixes $d_{\min}, d_{\max}$).
  - Figure 3.4 — reading rate against $d_{app}$, envelope shaded.

  3.4.1.3 Three-State Output

  - State function $\sigma : \mathcal{I} \to {\textsc{Read}, \textsc{Spotted},
  \textsc{Nothing}}$ with its decision rule (3.8).
  - Table 3.3 — state → fact established → tuple written into $\mathcal{M}$.
  - Why the intermediate state exists (detection range ≫ reading range).

  3.4.1.4 Decoding Path

  - Decoding. $D(I) = {(\ell_k, Q_k)}$; identifier filter $\ell_k \in
  \mathcal{F}$ (3.9).
  - Code side. $c = s \cdot \dfrac{m}{m + 2b} = 0.84,s$ (3.10) — the corners
  returned are those of the symbol, not the panel.
  - Planar pose. $(R_k, \mathbf{t}k) = \mathrm{PnP}{\square}(Q_k, c, K)$; the
  two-solution ambiguity and its resolution (3.11).
  - Incidence. $\alpha_k = \arccos\left|\langle
  -\mathbf{t}_k/\lVert\mathbf{t}_k\rVert,\ R_k\mathbf{e}z\rangle\right|$, reject
  if $\alpha_k > \alpha{\max}$ (3.12).
  - Range along the ray. $r_k = \arg\min_{\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{C}} \lVert
  (\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}_c) \times \rho(\bar{\mathbf{u}}_k) \rVert$ under
  tolerance $\tau(r)$ (3.13).
  - World position and normal. $\mathbf{p}_k = \mathbf{p}_c +
  r_k,\rho(\bar{\mathbf{u}}_k)$, $\mathbf{n}_k = R_c,\iota(R_k\mathbf{e}_z)$
  oriented toward the camera (3.14).
  - Why orientation comes from the corners and range from the LiDAR
  (scale-invariance of $R_k$, dependence of $\mathbf{t}_k$ on $c$).
  - Algorithm 3.1 — DecodeAndPlace. Require: $I, K, \mathbf{p}_c, R_c,
  \mathcal{C}$. Ensure: set of $(\ell, \mathbf{p}, \mathbf{n})$ written as Read.

  3.4.1.5 Detection Path

  - Detector output. $\mathcal{D}(I) = {(\kappa_j, \gamma_j, B_j)}$, $\kappa_j
  \in {\text{qr}, \text{carton}}$.
  - Two-regime range.
  $$r_j = \begin{cases} r^{\text{lidar}}(\rho_j) & r^{\text{lidar}} \le r_{\max}
  \ r^{\mathcal{M}}(\rho_j) & \text{otherwise} \end{cases} \tag{3.15}$$
  with $r^{\mathcal{M}}$ the first occupied cell of the map along the ray.
  - Plausibility. $\hat d_j = f_x, c / w_j$; accept iff $\kappa_{lo},\hat d_j
  \le \lVert \mathbf{p}_j - \mathbf{p}c\rVert \le \kappa{hi},\hat d_j$ (3.16).
  - Direction of approach. $\mathbf{a}j = -\Pi{xy},\rho_j / \lVert
  \Pi_{xy},\rho_j\rVert$ (3.17).
  - Carton class → semantic channel of $\mathcal{M}$ at $\mathbf{p}_j$.
  - Algorithm 3.2 — DetectAndPlace. Require: $I, K, \mathbf{p}_c, R_c,
  \mathcal{C}, \mathcal{M}$. Ensure: set of $(\mathbf{p}, \mathbf{a})$ written
  as Spotted, semantic marks.
  - Figure 3.5 — the two regimes on a plan of an aisle; LiDAR rays diverging,
  the map taking over past $r_{\max}$.

  3.4.1.6 Learned Detector

  - Requirement pair. $R_{det} \gg d_{\max}$ and false-alarm rate $\varphi \le
  \varphi_{\max}$ (3.18); why no threshold on a geometric detector satisfies
  both.
  - Automatic annotation. Projection of known objects; visibility by ray casting
  with owner identity; visible fraction $v = |\text{visible points}| / N$; keep
  if $v \ge v_{keep}$, ignore if $v < v_{ign}$ (3.19).
  - Algorithm 3.3 — AutoAnnotate. Require: scene, camera pose, object set.
  Ensure: boxes with class and visibility status.
  - Disjoint training / evaluation warehouses.
  - Figure 3.6 — one annotated image: boxes on visible points, greyed boxes on
  hidden objects.

  3.4.1.7 Perception Cycle

  - Scheduling rule: decode both cameras each cycle; detect on camera $(k
  \bmod 2)$ (3.20); justification by cost of each missed output.
  - Why the two lateral cameras (labels on both sides of an aisle).
  - Independence of the two paths (decoding on the whole image, not on detector
  regions).
  - Algorithm 3.4 — PerceptionCycle. The top-level loop calling 3.1 and 3.2,
  integrating LiDAR and coverage.

  3.4.1.8 Rejection Rules

  - Table 3.4 — filter, condition, error prevented: identifier format, $\alpha >
  \alpha_{\max}$, range $\notin \mathcal{E}$, plausibility (3.16).
  - The cost asymmetry that justifies rejecting rather than guessing.

  3.4.1.9 Summary of Design Decisions

  - Table 3.5 — decision / justification, one line each.

  ---
  Ce qui est repoussé au chapitre implémentation : les noms des décodeurs et du
  détecteur, les valeurs de $\tau$, $\kappa_{lo}$, $\kappa_{hi}$,
  $\alpha_{\max}$, $v_{keep}$, le seuil de confiance, les millisecondes.

  Ce qui reste en conception avec sa valeur : $d_{\min}=1.5$, $d_{\max}=4.0$, $s
  = 0.40$ m, le facteur 21/25, $r_{\max} = 8$ m — parce qu'ils fixent la

● 3.4.1 Perception — outline

  Introduction — 2 paragraphes, aucune formule.
  Ce que le composant reçoit (2 images + poses + un tour de lidar), ce qu'il
  produit (codes lus, étiquettes repérées, volume libre/occupé). Puis une idée
  forte : ses trois sorties n'ont pas le même risque — un identifiant faux reste
  dans l'inventaire jusqu'à la fin, une étiquette inventée coûte un vol, une
  observation refusée ne coûte rien. C'est ce déséquilibre qui explique toute la
  suite.

  ---
  3.4.1.1 Lire et repérer ne sont pas la même chose

  ≈ 3 paragraphes + 1 formule + 1 tableau + 1 figure

  - Le problème : un code cesse d'être lisible bien avant de cesser d'être
  visible.
  - Formule (3.1) : distance et angle se ramènent à une seule grandeur, $d_{app}
  = d / \cos\alpha$.
  - Tableau 3.1 : taux de lecture par tranche de distance apparente →
  l'enveloppe $\mathcal{E} = [1{,}5,;,4{,}0]$ m. Dire que la borne haute est
  optique et la borne basse mécanique.
  - Figure 3.2 : la courbe de lecture, l'enveloppe hachurée.
  - Conclusion de la sous-section : le repérage porte deux fois plus loin, donc
  la perception a deux fonctions et trois états — lu / repéré / rien. Le petit
  tableau des trois états ferme la partie.

  3.4.1.2 D'une image à un point dans la carte

  ≈ 4 paragraphes + 2 formules + 1 figure + 1 algorithme

  - Une image donne une direction, jamais une position. Trois sources de
  distance possibles : la taille apparente, la pose par les quatre coins, le
  lidar.
  - Formule (3.2) : le côté du code et non du panneau, $c = s \cdot 21/25$ —
  sinon toutes les distances sont 19 % trop grandes.
  - Ce que le système retient et pourquoi : les coins donnent l'orientation
  (invariante à l'échelle), le lidar donne la distance (aucune hypothèse sur
  l'objet).
  - Formule (3.3) : la position monde $\mathbf{p} = \mathbf{p}_{cam} +
  r,\rho(\mathbf{u})$.
  - Le cas d'une étiquette seulement repérée : pas de coins, donc deux régimes —
  lidar de près, carte au-delà du point où deux rayons s'écartent de plus d'une
  cellule. C'est la première fois que la carte sert de capteur.
  - Figure 3.3 : une allée vue de dessus, les rayons du lidar qui divergent, la
  carte qui prend le relais.
  - Algorithme 3.1 — Placer une observation. Entrée : coins ou boîte, pose
  caméra, nuage lidar, carte. Sortie : position + normale, ou rejet.

  3.4.1.3 Pourquoi le détecteur doit être appris

  ≈ 3 paragraphes + 1 tableau

  - Porter loin est facile ; un détecteur géométrique le fait. Il échoue sur le
  seul critère qui compte : plus d'un quart des images sans aucun code en
  contiennent un pour lui. Chaque faux repérage coûte un vol.
  - L'exigence est une paire : porter bien au-delà de l'enveloppe et rester rare
  sur une scène vide. Aucun seuil sur un détecteur géométrique ne satisfait les
  deux : le monter enlève aussi les vraies détections lointaines.
  - L'annotation automatique : projection des objets connus + rayons physiques
  pour juger la visibilité par l'identité du premier objet touché. Entrepôts
  d'entraînement et de test disjoints.
  - La seconde classe, le carton, qui remplit le canal sémantique de la carte.
  - Tableau 3.2 : géométrique vs appris — portée tenue, fausses alertes sur
  scène vide, et ce que chaque ligne coûte en vols.

  3.4.1.4 Le cycle et ce qu'il refuse d'écrire

  ≈ 2 paragraphes + 1 tableau + 1 algorithme

  - Deux caméras latérales parce que les étiquettes sont des deux côtés d'une
  allée. Décodage sur les deux images à chaque cycle, détection sur une seule en
  alternance — justifié par le coût de chaque manque.
  - Le décalage d'une image : l'image de maintenant est appariée à la pose
  d'avant, sinon le même panneau est écrit deux fois à deux endroits.
  - Algorithme 3.2 — Un cycle de perception.
  - Tableau 3.3 : les quatre rejets — format d'identifiant, incidence trop
  grande, distance hors enveloppe, placement invraisemblable — et l'erreur que
  chacun évite.

  ---
  Bilan du dosage : 4 sous-sections, 3 formules, 3 tableaux, 2 figures, 2
  algorithmes. C'est le même équilibre que les sections composants de Hamadene.

  Valide ou corrige, et je rédige.
