● 3.4.1 Perception

  Perception is the only channel through which the system acquires information
  about the building. At each cycle, and for each vehicle, it receives the
  images of the two lateral cameras, the pose $(\mathbf{p}_c, R_c)$ each camera
  held at the instant of capture, and one LiDAR revolution. It produces three
  outputs, all of which are written into the shared map $\mathcal{M}$: the
  identifiers it has decoded, each with a position and a surface normal; the
  labels it has detected without decoding, each with a position and the
  direction from which it was seen; and the volume it has established as free or
  as solid.

  These three outputs do not carry the same risk, and the whole component is
  shaped by that imbalance. A wrong identifier is permanent, since nothing
  downstream ever re-examines a label declared read, and it survives into the
  final inventory. A label reported where none exists costs one flight. An
  observation that perception declines to make costs nothing, because the
  vehicle will pass that way again. Perception is therefore built to refuse
  rather than to guess, and every filter described below exists to convert a
  possible error into a missed observation.

  3.4.1.1 The Reading Envelope

  A code stops being readable long before it stops being visible, and the two
  explanations usually given for a failed read — the code is too far, or it is
  seen too obliquely — turn out to be the same phenomenon. A square inclined by
  an angle $\alpha$ projects onto the image exactly as a square $1/\cos\alpha$
  times more distant would; in both cases its modules occupy proportionally
  fewer pixels. Distance and angle therefore reduce to a single quantity, the
  apparent distance:

  $$d_{\text{app}} = \frac{d}{\cos \alpha} \tag{3.1}$$

  where $d$ is the distance from the camera to the centre of the code and
  $\alpha$ the angle between the viewing direction and the normal of the panel.
  Measured over the full range of poses, the reading rate depends on this
  quantity alone.

  Table 3.1 — Reading rate as a function of apparent distance.

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────┐
  │ $d_{\text{app}}$ │ Successful reads │ Images │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.00 – 1.25 m    │ 75 %             │ 84     │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.25 – 1.50 m    │ 89 %             │ 115    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.50 – 4.00 m    │ 91 – 99 %        │ 925    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 4.00 – 5.00 m    │ 74 %             │ 167    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 5.00 – 6.50 m    │ 31 %             │ 194    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ beyond 6.50 m    │ 3 %              │ 185    │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────┘

  The reading envelope of the system follows directly:

  $$\mathcal{E} = {, (d, \alpha) ;:; 1.5 \le d_{\text{app}} \le 4.0 ,}
  \tag{3.2}$$

  Its two bounds are of different natures. The upper one is optical: past four
  metres the modules no longer occupy enough pixels. The lower one is
  mechanical: closer than about a metre, a lateral error of a few centimetres
  produces a large incidence angle, and a vehicle in flight cannot hold its
  heading tightly enough to stay in front of the panel. Because an aisle between
  two racks is only 3.9 m wide, a vehicle cannot move further than 3.6 m from a
  panel; it is therefore the angle, not the distance, that allows one position
  to cover several cartons. $\mathcal{E}$ is the most structural quantity in the
  system, since every reading pose generated later by the decision component is
  placed inside it.

  Detection is under no such constraint, because it does not require the modules
  to be resolved: a label remains a recognisable object long after it has
  ceased to be a readable one. This gap between the two ranges is the opening
  the mission goes through — a vehicle travelling down an aisle can register the
  existence of every label in it while being able to read only those it passes
  close to. Perception is consequently not one function but two, with a third
  state lying between them.

  Table 3.2 — The three states of perception.

  ┌────────────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │       State        │     What is      │      What is written into       │
  │                    │   established    │          $\mathcal{M}$          │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $\textsc{Read}$    │ the identifier   │ $(\ell, \mathbf{p},             │
  │                    │ itself           │ \mathbf{n})$                    │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │                    │ a label exists   │ $(\mathbf{p}, \mathbf{a})$,     │
  │ $\textsc{Spotted}$ │ at this place    │ $\mathbf{a}$ the direction of   │
  │                    │                  │ approach                        │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $\textsc{Nothing}$ │ —                │ —                               │
  └────────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  The intermediate state is what gives the mission its momentum. A spotted label
  is not a hypothesis to be investigated but a target whose reading is nearly
  certain, the only missing element being the approach. It converts an act of
  exploration, whose return is uncertain, into an act of collection, whose
  return is almost guaranteed — and this is why the decision component will
  price it above everything else.

  3.4.1.2 Three-Dimensional Placement of an Observation

  An image supplies a direction, never a position. Turning a detection into an
  entry in a three-dimensional map requires a range along that direction, and
  three sources are available: the apparent size of the code, the translation of
  the pose recovered from its four corners, and the LiDAR range along the ray.

  The first two both require the physical size of the code, which is known here
  — the generator places on each carton face a square panel of side $s = 0.40$ m
  — but which carries a trap that silently biases everything downstream. A QR
  symbol is printed with a quiet margin of two modules on each side of a
  twenty-one module grid, and every decoder returns the corners of the symbol,
  not of the panel. Treating the returned quadrilateral as the full panel
  over-estimates every distance by $25/21 - 1 \approx 19\ %$. The component
  therefore never works with $s$ but with the code side:

  $$c = s \cdot \frac{21}{25} = 0.336 \ \text{m} \tag{3.3}$$

  The pose recovered from four coplanar corners decomposes into two quantities
  of very unequal robustness. The rotation $R_q$ is determined by the shape of
  the quadrilateral alone and does not depend on the assumed side length: a
  wrong value of $c$ would scale the translation and leave the rotation
  untouched. The translation $\mathbf{t}_q$ depends on it linearly. Orientation
  obtained this way is thus trustworthy under any assumption about the panel
  format, while distance obtained this way is only as trustworthy as that
  assumption.

  The design retains the corners for orientation and the LiDAR for range. A
  pixel $\mathbf{u}$ is back-projected into a world ray, and the range is read
  from the LiDAR point passing closest to it:

  $$\rho(\mathbf{u}) = R_c \cdot \iota!\left(
  \frac{K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}}{\lVert K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}} \rVert}
  \right), \qquad \mathbf{p} = \mathbf{p}_c + r,\rho(\mathbf{u}) \tag{3.4}$$

  where $\iota$ converts the camera axis convention into the world one. The
  motivation is not accuracy — the two distance sources agree to within a
  centimetre — but independence. A placement that depends on a printed format
  breaks when the format changes, and the warehouse the vehicles are meant to
  handle is by definition one they have not been shown. Keeping the range free
  of that assumption costs nothing here and removes a hidden coupling between
  perception and the scene.

  For a label that has only been detected the problem is harder, since the
  detector returns a region and not four corners: there is no orientation to
  recover, and the range must come from the LiDAR alone. Here the sensor imposes
  a boundary that is a property of its construction rather than a tuning
  choice. The angular spacing $\Delta\theta$ of the LiDAR is constant, so the
  separation between neighbouring rays grows as $r,\Delta\theta$; at eight
  metres two neighbouring rays are already further apart than one map cell, and
  the ray nearest a given direction no longer lands on the intended object but
  on the carton beside it or, through a gap in the rack, on the wall behind. Two
  regimes are therefore needed: within $r_{\max} = 8$ m the range is the LiDAR
  measurement, and beyond it the range is the first cell along that direction
  already established as occupied in $\mathcal{M}$. This is the first place in
  the system where the map is read as a sensor rather than as a memory, and the
  choice is deliberate — a single revolution is sparse at eight metres, but the
  map has accumulated many revolutions from many poses and several vehicles, so
  it is dense exactly where one revolution is not.

  A plausibility test guards the detection path, and it is the one place where
  the nominal size earns its keep. The width $w$ of the detected region gives a
  distance $\hat{d} = f_x,c/w$ that is unreliable in value but correct in order
  of magnitude, and a placement disagreeing with it by more than a wide factor
  is rejected. The window is deliberately loose, since the region is not the
  code; its purpose is not precision but the detection of one specific failure —
  a ray that has passed through a gap in a rack and returned a point far behind
  the label it was aimed at. Finally, a spotted label is written facing the
  camera that observed it: this is the only directional information that
  survives the absence of corners, and it is enough, since it records the side
  of the rack from which the label must later be approached.

  Algorithm 3.1 — PlaceObservation
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: K, (p_c, R_c), cloud C, map M, and either corners Q (decoded)
           or a region B (detected)
  Ensure:  (p, n) ∈ ℝ³ × ℝ³, or ⊥

   1:  if Q is given then                            ▷ decoded code
   2:      (R_q, t_q) ← PnP□(Q, c, K)
   3:      α ← ∠(−t_q , R_q e_z)
   4:      if α > α_max then return ⊥
   5:      u ← centroid(Q)
   6:  else
   7:      u ← centre(B)
   8:  end if
   9:  ρ ← R_c · ι( K⁻¹ũ / ‖K⁻¹ũ‖ )                  ▷ world ray, Eq. (3.4)
  10:  r ← RangeLidar(p_c, ρ, C)
  11:  if r = ⊥ or r > r_max then
  12:      if Q is given then return ⊥               ▷ a read code is always
  close
  13:      r ← RangeMap(p_c, ρ, M)                   ▷ first occupied cell
  14:  end if
  15:  if r = ⊥ then return ⊥
  16:  p ← p_c + r·ρ
  17:  if Q is given then
  18:      if r ∉ E then return ⊥                    ▷ reading envelope, Eq.
  (3.2)
  19:      n ← R_c · ι(R_q e_z), oriented towards p_c
  20:  else
  21:      d̂ ← f_x · c / w(B)
  22:      if ‖p − p_c‖ ∉ [κ_lo·d̂ , κ_hi·d̂] then return ⊥
  23:      n ← −Π_xy ρ / ‖Π_xy ρ‖                    ▷ direction of approach
  24:  end if
  25:  return (p, n)
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  3.4.1.3 Learned Detection

  Extending the detection range is not in itself difficult. A geometric detector
  — an adaptive threshold, contour extraction, a convexity and aspect test, a
  dark-fraction test — is inexpensive and works well past the reading envelope.
  It fails on the only criterion that matters here: in a warehouse emptied of
  all its codes, it reports a panel on more than a quarter of the images,
  mistaking cartons, rack uprights and shadows for labels. Since every spotted
  label produces a journey, a component with that false-alarm rate would send
  vehicles to more empty places than real ones, and the system would complete
  the inventory faster with the detector switched off.

  The requirement is therefore a pair rather than a single figure: the detection
  range must extend well beyond $\mathcal{E}$, and the rate of reports on a
  scene containing no code must stay low enough that a wasted flight remains
  exceptional. No threshold applied to a geometric detector satisfies both at
  once, because raising it to suppress false alarms also removes the distant
  true detections, which are precisely what the component exists to provide. A
  learned detector is retained because it separates the two: it has learned what
  a label looks like in this setting rather than what a dark quadrilateral
  looks like anywhere.

  Table 3.3 — The two detectors against the requirement pair.

  ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │                     │        Geometric        │         Learned         │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Range held at 90 %  │ 4.0 m                   │ 8.0 m                   │
  │ detection           │                         │                         │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Target panel        │ 47 %                    │ 98 %                    │
  │ detected at 6 – 8 m │                         │                         │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Images without any  │                         │                         │
  │ code where one is   │ 27.3 %                  │ 0.7 %                   │
  │ reported            │                         │                         │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │                     │ detection stops where   │ labels are registered   │
  │ Consequence for the │ reading stops, and one  │ across a whole aisle,   │
  │  mission            │ journey in four is      │ wasted journeys are     │
  │                     │ wasted                  │ rare                    │
  └─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  The training set is built without a single manual annotation, which is what
  makes the choice practical rather than merely desirable. In simulation the
  position of every carton and every panel is known, so each object can be
  projected into the image from the exact camera pose. Projection alone would be
  wrong, since an object hidden behind another would be annotated as visible;
  visibility is therefore established physically, by casting rays at sampled
  points of each surface and accepting a point only when the first object the
  ray reaches is the one that owns it. The annotation encloses the visible
  points rather than the whole object, and an object too largely hidden is
  marked as ignored, counting neither as an example nor as an error. Training
  warehouses and evaluation warehouses are disjoint, so the detector is never
  measured on a building it has been shown.

  The detector reports a second class, cartons, which are never read. A detected
  carton establishes that a region holds inventory, which is what the semantic
  channel of the map needs in order to distinguish a full rack from an empty one
  before anything has been decoded there.

  3.4.1.4 The Perception Cycle

  The vehicle carries two lateral cameras rather than one forward camera,
  because labels sit on the faces of cartons on both sides of an aisle: a
  vehicle flying down the middle has readable surfaces to its left and to its
  right and nothing of interest ahead. One traversal then serves both racks,
  whereas a forward camera would require the vehicle to turn towards each face
  in turn, and the envelope is narrow enough that this would double the number
  of poses.

  Decoding and detection do not cost the same, and when the graphics card is
  also rendering, the two together exceed what one cycle can afford. The rule
  adopted is asymmetric: decoding runs on both images every cycle, detection
  runs on one camera per cycle, alternating. The justification lies in what each
  failure costs — a label detected one cycle later is still detected, and the
  vehicle is not going there yet, whereas a label not decoded while the vehicle
  was in position in front of it requires a second journey to the same place.
  Decoding also runs on the whole image rather than on the regions the detector
  proposes, which keeps the two paths independent: a label the detector misses
  can still be read, and a failure of the learned component reduces the range of
  the system without ever costing an identifier.

  One last correction is temporal. The renderer delivers a frame one step behind
  the physics, so reading an image together with the pose held at the moment it
  arrives attaches every observation to a position the vehicle has already
  left. The component keeps the two most recent instants and always pairs the
  image of now with the pose and the point cloud of the previous one. Without
  it, the same panel observed while approaching and while receding is written
  twice, in two different places, and the map records two labels where one
  exists.

  Algorithm 3.2 — PerceptionCycle
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: vehicle i, cycle index k, map M, history H (length 2)
  Ensure:  M updated

   1:  (D, r) ← Lidar(i)
   2:  M.IntegrateOccupancy(p_i, D, r)
   3:  M.Announce(i, p_i)
   4:  push (poses of L and R, cloud C) onto H
   5:  if |H| < 2 then return                        ▷ one frame of render lag
   6:  (p_c, R_c, C) ← H[t−1]
   7:  for cam ∈ {L, R} do
   8:      I ← Image(cam)
   9:      for (ℓ, Q) ∈ Decode(I) do
  10:          if ℓ ∉ F then continue                ▷ foreign identifier
  11:          o ← PlaceObservation(Q, K, p_c, R_c, C, M)
  12:          if o ≠ ⊥ then M.WriteRead(ℓ, o)
  13:      end for
  14:      if cam ≡ k (mod 2) then                   ▷ detection alternates
  15:          for (κ, B) ∈ Detect(I) do
  16:              o ← PlaceObservation(B, K, p_c, R_c, C, M)
  17:              if o = ⊥ then continue
  18:              if κ = qr then M.WriteSpotted(o)
  19:              else M.MarkSemantic(o.p)
  20:          end for
  21:      end if
  22:      M.IntegrateCoverage(p_c, R_c e_x)
  23:  end for
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Four rejections appear across the two algorithms, and together they define
  what perception is willing to assert.

  Table 3.4 — Rejection rules and the error each one prevents.

  Rule: Identifier format
  Condition: $\ell \notin \mathcal{F}$
  Error prevented: the warehouse carries QR codes on its signage; without this
  filter they enter the inventory as cartons
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Limiting incidence
  Condition: $\alpha > \alpha_{\max}$
  Error prevented: past that angle the four corners no longer determine a
  reliable normal, and a wrong normal sends a later vehicle to the wrong side
  of a rack
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Reading envelope
  Condition: $r \notin \mathcal{E}$
  Error prevented: a code decoded from an implausible distance is a
  corner-localisation error, not a reading
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Plausibility of placement
  Condition: $\lVert \mathbf{p} - \mathbf{p}c \rVert \notin [\kappa{lo}\hat{d},\

  \kappa_{hi}\hat{d}]$
  Error prevented: a ray through a gap in a rack places a target inside a wall

  Each converts a possible error into a missed observation, which is the trade
  the cost asymmetry of the introduction demands.

  Figures. Figure 3.2 — reading rate against apparent distance, with
  $\mathcal{E}$ shaded, the measurement that fixes every reading pose in the
  system. Figure 3.3 — a plan view of an aisle showing the two placement
  regimes: LiDAR rays diverging with range, and the map taking over past
  $r_{\max}$.

  Légende à mettre sous la figure: Inside the perception component. The image 
  supplies direction and identity, the LiDAR  supplies range; the two paths 
  write three  different kinds of fact into the shared map.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fichier: fig_distance_apparente
  Où la placer: 3.4.1.1, avec l'éq. (3.1)
  Légende à mettre sous la figure: Distance and angle are one limit. Both panels
   
  fill the same cone, so they produce the same  image: a panel tilted by α is 
  equivalent to an  untilted one 1/cos α times further away.

  Légende à mettre sous la figure: Reading rate against apparent distance, 1 670

   by every  reading pose in the system.

  Légende à mettre sous la figure: Two placement regimes. Inside the LiDAR's 
  trusted range a ray lands on the label; beyond  it the rays are further apart
   than a map  cell, and the range is taken from the map  instead.

  images. The geometric one stops where reading  stops and reports a label on 
  27 % of empty  images; the learned one holds 98 % at 6–8 m  for 0.7 %.

  Où la placer: 3.4.1.3
  Légende à mettre sous la figure: Training examples built without manual

   is decided  by physics rays.

● Verdict titre par titre. Deux de mes figures sont effectivement à la
  frontière, je te le dis franchement.

  Titre: 3.4.1 intro
  Figure: fig_perception_chaine
  Statut: Conception pure
  Ce qu'il faut surveiller: Aucune techno nommée dans le schéma. Rien à
  déplacer.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Titre: 3.4.1.1 The Reading Envelope
  Figure: fig_distance_apparente + fig_enveloppe
  Statut: Conception, avec une réserve
  Ce qu'il faut surveiller: L'équation et l'enveloppe sont de la conception. Le
  tableau 3.1 et la courbe sont une mesure, donc du résultat.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Titre: 3.4.1.2 Placement of an Observation
  Figure: fig_regimes
  Statut: Conception pure
  Ce qu'il faut surveiller: Le facteur 21/25 et les 8 m sont des propriétés du
  code et du capteur, pas des réglages.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Titre: 3.4.1.3 Learned Detection
  Figure: fig_portees + fig_annotation
  Statut: Mixte — le point sensible
  Ce qu'il faut surveiller: fig_portees est un résultat d'évaluation.
  L'annotation automatique dépend de ta convention.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Titre: 3.4.1.4 The Perception Cycle
  Figure: l'algorithme 3.2
  Statut: Conception pure
  Ce qu'il faut surveiller: À condition de ne jamais écrire les millisecondes ni

  le nom du décodeur.

  Ce que je défends, et ce que je concède

  Je défends fig_enveloppe en conception. L'enveloppe n'est pas une performance,
  c'est une contrainte d'entrée : elle détermine où chaque pose de lecture sera
  placée, donc la géométrie de tout le système. Bouhadi fait exactement ça — il
  met la limite de 8 192 jetons dans le chapitre conception, parce qu'elle
  élimine des modèles candidats. Ta courbe joue le même rôle. Si tu la retires,
  la section 3.4.1.1 n'a plus rien pour justifier 1,5 et 4,0.

  Je concède fig_portees. C'est une comparaison de deux solutions, mesurée après
  coup, avec des pourcentages de rappel. C'est un résultat. Trois options :
  - la déplacer au chapitre évaluation et ne garder en conception que la phrase
  « aucun seuil sur un détecteur géométrique ne satisfait les deux exigences » ;
  - la garder mais réduite à deux lignes : portée tenue à 90 % et taux sur scène
  vide, sans courbe ;
  - la garder entière et assumer.

  Je recommande la deuxième : deux chiffres suffisent à fonder la décision, et
  la courbe complète va au chapitre évaluation où elle sera lue comme une
  preuve, pas comme une justification.

  fig_annotation : tes deux références sont en désaccord. Bouhadi met la
  construction du jeu de données dans le chapitre conception (sa section 4.4).
  Hamadene met la construction du corpus RAG dans le chapitre implémentation (sa
  5.9.2). Je recommande Bouhadi : chez toi, l'annotation sans clic est une idée
  de méthode, pas un détail d'outillage — c'est ce qui rend le détecteur appris
  possible dans ton cadre. Elle reste en conception.

  Le vrai danger de 3.4.1.3, c'est que le titre attire les détails. Écris-le
  comme une décision (« pourquoi le détecteur doit être appris ») et pas comme
  une description de modèle, sinon YOLO, la résolution et le seuil vont y entrer
  tout seuls.

  ---
  Description du schéma « Inside the perception component »

  À donner telle quelle pour le régénérer. Contenu uniquement, aucune consigne
  de style.

  Sujet. Le fonctionnement interne du composant de perception, sur un cycle,
  pour un drone. Le lecteur doit comprendre trois choses : il y a deux chemins
  parallèles et indépendants dans le composant, ils se rejoignent sur une étape
  commune de placement, et ils produisent des écritures de natures différentes
  dans la carte.

  Cinq blocs, de gauche à droite.

  1. Sensors — caméra gauche, caméra droite, un tour de lidar, la pose des
  caméras. Une note : la pose et le nuage utilisés sont ceux de l'instant
  précédent, parce que le rendu a une image de retard.
  2. Decoding — produit un identifiant et les quatre coins d'un code. S'applique
  aux deux caméras à chaque cycle.
  3. Detection — produit une région et une classe. S'applique à une seule caméra
  par cycle, en alternance.
  4. Placement in 3D — l'étape commune. Convertit un pixel en rayon monde,
  obtient la distance du lidar ou, au-delà de 8 m, de la carte. Contient aussi
  les motifs de rejet : incidence trop grande, distance hors de l'enveloppe,
  placement invraisemblable.
  5. Shared map — cinq entrées distinctes : READ (identifiant, position,
  normale), SPOTTED (position, direction d'approche), SEMANTIC (un carton est
  là), OCCUPANCY (volume libre et solide), COVERAGE (observé, et depuis quelle
  direction).

  Les liens.

  - Sensors → Decoding, et Sensors → Detection : deux flèches séparées, pour
  montrer que les deux chemins partent de la même image sans dépendre l'un de
  l'autre.
  - Decoding → Placement, étiquetée « corners ».
  - Detection → Placement, étiquetée « region ».
  - Sensors → Placement, en pointillé, étiquetée « LiDAR point cloud » : c'est
  la source de distance, elle ne passe par aucun des deux chemins.
  - Placement → Shared map, deux flèches distinctes : « read » et « spotted ».
  Elles doivent arriver à des endroits différents du bloc carte, parce qu'elles
  n'écrivent pas la même chose.
  - Sensors → Shared map, en pointillé, étiquetée « LiDAR and camera cone,
  written straight into the map » : l'occupation et la couverture ne passent pas
  par le placement.

  Ce que la figure doit rendre évident. Que le décodage et la détection ne
  communiquent jamais entre eux ; que la distance ne vient jamais de l'image ;
  et que deux des cinq écritures dans la carte court-circuitent complètement la
  chaîne de placement.

  Ce qu'elle ne doit pas contenir. Aucun nom de bibliothèque, de modèle ou de
  format, aucune durée, aucun seuil chiffré. Les seules valeurs autorisées sont
  les 8 m qui séparent les deux régimes de distance.

  Thought for 15s (ctrl+o to expand)

● Description du schéma — le sens seulement

  Ce que le schéma représente. Le fonctionnement interne de la perception, sur
  un cycle, pour un drone.

  Ce que le composant contient.

  - Les capteurs : deux caméras latérales, un tour de lidar, et la pose des
  caméras. La pose et le nuage employés sont ceux de l'instant précédent, le
  rendu ayant une image de retard.
  - Le décodage : d'une image il tire un identifiant et les quatre coins du
  code. Il s'applique aux deux caméras à chaque cycle.
  - La détection : de la même image elle tire une région et une classe. Elle
  s'applique à une seule caméra par cycle, en alternance.
  - Le placement en trois dimensions : il convertit un pixel en rayon dans le
  monde et lui donne une distance, prise sur le lidar, ou sur la carte au-delà
  de huit mètres. C'est là que se font les rejets : incidence trop grande,
  distance hors de l'enveloppe de lecture, placement invraisemblable.
  - La carte partagée, destinataire de tout ce qui est établi.

  Ce qui circule.

  - L'image va au décodage et à la détection.
  - Le décodage transmet des coins au placement, la détection lui transmet une
  région.
  - Le nuage lidar va directement au placement : c'est de là que vient toute
  distance.
  - Le placement écrit dans la carte deux choses de natures différentes : un
  code lu, avec son identifiant, sa position et sa normale ; et une étiquette
  repérée, avec sa position et la direction depuis laquelle il faudra
  l'approcher. Il marque aussi la présence d'un carton.
  - Les capteurs écrivent dans la carte sans passer par le placement : le tour
  de lidar y dépose le volume libre et solide, le cône de la caméra y dépose ce
  qui a été observé et depuis quelle direction.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Que le décodage et la détection ne se
  parlent jamais : l'échec de l'un ne prive pas l'autre. Que la distance ne
  vient jamais de l'image, seulement du lidar ou de la carte. Et que
  l'occupation et la couverture atteignent la carte sans passer par la chaîne de
  placement, donc sans pouvoir être rejetées par ses filtres.

  Ce qui ne doit pas y figurer. Aucun nom de bibliothèque, de modèle ou de
  format, aucune durée, aucun seuil. La seule valeur qui a sa place est les huit
  mètres qui séparent les deux sources de distance.

  ---
  3.4.1 Perception

  Perception is the only channel through which the system acquires information
  about the building. At each cycle, and for each vehicle, it receives the
  images of the two lateral cameras, the pose $(\mathbf{p}_c, R_c)$ each camera
  held at the instant of capture, and one LiDAR revolution. It produces three
  outputs, all of which are written into the shared map $\mathcal{M}$: the
  identifiers it has decoded, each with a position and a surface normal; the
  labels it has detected without decoding, each with a position and the
  direction from which it was seen; and the volume it has established as free or
  as solid.

  $$f_{\mathcal{P}} : \mathcal{I}^2 \times \mathcal{T} \times \mathcal{L}
  \longrightarrow \mathcal{R} \times \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{V} \tag{3.1}$$

  These three outputs do not carry the same risk, and the whole component is
  shaped by that imbalance. A wrong identifier is permanent, since nothing
  downstream ever re-examines a label declared read, and it survives into the
  final inventory. A label reported where none exists costs one flight. An
  observation that perception declines to make costs nothing, because the
  vehicle will pass that way again. Perception is therefore built to refuse
  rather than to guess, and every filter described below exists to convert a
  possible error into a missed observation.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.2 ici — fig_perception_chaine
  ▎ Inside the perception component. The image supplies direction and identity, 
  ▎ the LiDAR supplies range; the two paths write three different kinds of fact 
  ▎ into the shared map.

  3.4.1.1 The Reading Envelope

  A code stops being readable long before it stops being visible, and the two
  explanations usually given for a failed read — the code is too far, or it is
  seen too obliquely — turn out to be the same phenomenon. A square inclined by
  an angle $\alpha$ projects onto the image exactly as a square $1/\cos\alpha$
  times more distant would; in both cases its modules occupy proportionally
  fewer pixels. Distance and angle therefore reduce to a single quantity, the
  apparent distance:

  $$d_{\text{app}} = \frac{d}{\cos \alpha} \tag{3.2}$$

  where $d$ is the distance from the camera to the centre of the code and
  $\alpha$ the angle between the viewing direction and the normal of the panel.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.3 ici — fig_distance_apparente
  ▎ Distance and angle are a single limit. Both panels fill the same cone, so 
  ▎ they produce the same image: a panel tilted by α is equivalent to an 
  ▎ untilted one 1/cos α times further away.

  Measured across the full range of poses, the reading rate depends on this
  quantity and on nothing else.

  Table 3.1 — Reading rate as a function of apparent distance.

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────┐
  │ $d_{\text{app}}$ │ Successful reads │ Images │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.00 – 1.25 m    │ 75 %             │ 84     │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.25 – 1.50 m    │ 89 %             │ 115    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.50 – 4.00 m    │ 91 – 99 %        │ 925    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 4.00 – 5.00 m    │ 74 %             │ 167    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 5.00 – 6.50 m    │ 31 %             │ 194    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ beyond 6.50 m    │ 3 %              │ 185    │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────┘

  The reading envelope of the system follows directly:

  $$\mathcal{E} = {, (d, \alpha) ;:; 1.5 \le d_{\text{app}} \le 4.0 ,}
  \tag{3.3}$$

  Its two bounds are of different natures. The upper one is optical: past four
  metres the modules no longer occupy enough pixels. The lower one is
  mechanical: closer than about a metre, a lateral error of a few centimetres
  produces a large incidence angle, and a vehicle in flight cannot hold its
  heading tightly enough to stay in front of the panel. Because an aisle between
  two racks is only 3.9 m wide, a vehicle cannot move further than 3.6 m from a
  panel; it is therefore the angle, not the distance, that allows one position
  to cover several cartons. $\mathcal{E}$ is the most structural quantity in the
  system, since every reading pose generated later by the decision component is
  placed inside it.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.4 ici — fig_enveloppe
  ▎ Reading rate against apparent distance, 1 670 images at known poses. The 
  ▎ shaded band is the envelope ℰ = [1.5, 4.0] m used by every reading pose in 
  ▎ the system.

  Detection is under no such constraint, because it does not require the modules
  to be resolved: a label remains a recognisable object long after it has
  ceased to be a readable one. This gap between the two ranges is the opening
  the mission goes through — a vehicle travelling down an aisle can register the
  existence of every label in it while being able to read only those it passes
  close to. Perception is consequently not one function but two, with a third
  state lying between them.

  Table 3.2 — The three states of perception.

  ┌────────────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │       State        │     What is      │      What is written into       │
  │                    │   established    │          $\mathcal{M}$          │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $\textsc{Read}$    │ the identifier   │ $(\ell, \mathbf{p},             │
  │                    │ itself           │ \mathbf{n})$                    │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │                    │ a label exists   │ $(\mathbf{p}, \mathbf{a})$,     │
  │ $\textsc{Spotted}$ │ at this place    │ $\mathbf{a}$ the direction of   │
  │                    │                  │ approach                        │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $\textsc{Nothing}$ │ —                │ —                               │
  └────────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  The intermediate state is what gives the mission its momentum. A spotted label
  is not a hypothesis to be investigated but a target whose reading is nearly
  certain, the only missing element being the approach. It converts an act of
  exploration, whose return is uncertain, into an act of collection, whose
  return is almost guaranteed — and this is why the decision component will
  price it above everything else.

  3.4.1.2 Three-Dimensional Placement of an Observation

  An image supplies a direction, never a position. Turning a detection into an
  entry in a three-dimensional map requires a range along that direction, and
  three sources are available: the apparent size of the code, the translation of
  the pose recovered from its four corners, and the LiDAR range along the ray.

  The first two both require the physical size of the code, which is known here
  — the generator places on each carton face a square panel of side $s = 0.40$ m
  — but which carries a trap that silently biases everything downstream. A QR
  symbol is printed with a quiet margin of two modules on each side of a
  twenty-one module grid, and every decoder returns the corners of the symbol,
  not of the panel. Treating the returned quadrilateral as the full panel
  over-estimates every distance by $25/21 - 1 \approx 19\ %$. The component
  consequently never works with $s$ but with the code side:

  $$c = s \cdot \frac{21}{25} = 0.336 \ \text{m} \tag{3.4}$$

  The pose recovered from four coplanar corners decomposes into two quantities
  of very unequal robustness. The rotation $R_q$ is determined by the shape of
  the quadrilateral alone and does not depend on the assumed side length: a
  wrong value of $c$ would scale the translation and leave the rotation
  untouched. The translation $\mathbf{t}_q$ depends on it linearly. Orientation
  obtained this way is thus trustworthy under any assumption about the panel
  format, while distance obtained this way is only as trustworthy as that
  assumption.

  The design retains the corners for orientation and the LiDAR for range. A
  pixel $\mathbf{u}$ is back-projected into a world ray, and the range is read
  from the LiDAR point passing closest to it:

  $$\rho(\mathbf{u}) = R_c \cdot \iota!\left(
  \frac{K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}}{\lVert K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}} \rVert}
  \right), \qquad \mathbf{p} = \mathbf{p}_c + r,\rho(\mathbf{u}) \tag{3.5}$$

  where $\iota$ converts the camera axis convention into the world one. The
  motivation is not accuracy — the two distance sources agree to within a
  centimetre — but independence. A placement that depends on a printed format
  breaks when the format changes, and the warehouse the vehicles are meant to
  handle is by definition one they have not been shown. Keeping the range free
  of that assumption costs nothing here and removes a hidden coupling between
  perception and the scene.

  For a label that has only been detected the problem is harder, since the
  detector returns a region and not four corners: there is no orientation to
  recover, and the range must come from the LiDAR alone. Here the sensor imposes
  a boundary that is a property of its construction rather than a tuning
  choice. The angular spacing $\Delta\theta$ of the LiDAR is constant, so the
  separation between neighbouring rays grows as $r,\Delta\theta$; at eight
  metres two neighbouring rays are already further apart than one map cell, and
  the ray nearest a given direction no longer lands on the intended object but
  on the carton beside it or, through a gap in the rack, on the wall behind. Two
  regimes are therefore needed: within $r_{\max} = 8$ m the range is the LiDAR
  measurement, and beyond it the range is the first cell along that direction
  already established as occupied in $\mathcal{M}$. This is the first place in
  the system where the map is read as a sensor rather than as a memory, and the
  choice is deliberate — a single revolution is sparse at eight metres, but the
  map has accumulated many revolutions from many poses and several vehicles, so
  it is dense exactly where one revolution is not.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.5 ici — fig_regimes
  ▎ Two placement regimes. Inside the LiDAR's trusted range a ray lands on the 
  ▎ label; beyond it the rays are further apart than a map cell, and the range 
  ▎ is taken from the map instead.

  A plausibility test guards the detection path, and it is the one place where
  the nominal size earns its keep. The width $w$ of the detected region gives a
  distance $\hat{d} = f_x,c/w$ that is unreliable in value but correct in order
  of magnitude, and a placement disagreeing with it by more than a wide factor
  is rejected. The window is deliberately loose, since the region is not the
  code; its purpose is not precision but the detection of one specific failure —
  a ray that has passed through a gap in a rack and returned a point far behind
  the label it was aimed at. Finally, a spotted label is written facing the
  camera that observed it: this is the only directional information that
  survives the absence of corners, and it is enough, since it records the side
  of the rack from which the label must later be approached.

  Algorithm 3.1 — PlaceObservation
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: K, (p_c, R_c), cloud C, map M, and either corners Q (decoded)
           or a region B (detected)
  Ensure:  (p, n) ∈ ℝ³ × ℝ³, or ⊥

   1:  if Q is given then                            ▷ decoded code
   2:      (R_q, t_q) ← PnP□(Q, c, K)
   3:      α ← ∠(−t_q , R_q e_z)
   4:      if α > α_max then return ⊥
   5:      u ← centroid(Q)
   6:  else
   7:      u ← centre(B)
   8:  end if
   9:  ρ ← R_c · ι( K⁻¹ũ / ‖K⁻¹ũ‖ )                  ▷ world ray, Eq. (3.5)
  10:  r ← RangeLidar(p_c, ρ, C)
  11:  if r = ⊥ or r > r_max then
  12:      if Q is given then return ⊥               ▷ a read code is always
  close
  13:      r ← RangeMap(p_c, ρ, M)                   ▷ first occupied cell
  14:  end if
  15:  if r = ⊥ then return ⊥
  16:  p ← p_c + r·ρ
  17:  if Q is given then
  18:      if r ∉ E then return ⊥                    ▷ reading envelope, Eq.
  (3.3)
  19:      n ← R_c · ι(R_q e_z), oriented towards p_c
  20:  else
  21:      d̂ ← f_x · c / w(B)
  22:      if ‖p − p_c‖ ∉ [κ_lo·d̂ , κ_hi·d̂] then return ⊥
  23:      n ← −Π_xy ρ / ‖Π_xy ρ‖                    ▷ direction of approach
  24:  end if
  25:  return (p, n)
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  3.4.1.3 Why Detection Must Be Learned

  Extending the detection range is not in itself difficult. A geometric
  detector, built on a threshold and a shape test, is inexpensive and works well
  past the reading envelope. It fails on the only criterion that matters here:
  in a warehouse emptied of all its codes, it reports a panel on more than a
  quarter of the images, mistaking cartons, rack uprights and shadows for
  labels. Since every spotted label produces a journey, a component with that
  false-alarm rate would send vehicles to more empty places than real ones, and
  the system would complete the inventory faster with the detector switched off.

  The requirement is therefore a pair rather than a single figure, and it is the
  pair that decides the construction:

  Table 3.3 — The requirement placed on the detector.

  ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │                     │      Geometric      │          Required           │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Range held at 90 %  │ 4.0 m, where        │                             │
  │ detection           │ reading already     │ well beyond $\mathcal{E}$   │
  │                     │ stops               │                             │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Reports on a scene  │ 27 % of images      │ rare enough that a wasted   │
  │ holding no code     │                     │ flight is exceptional       │
  └─────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  No threshold applied to a geometric detector satisfies both at once, because
  raising it to suppress false alarms also removes the distant true detections,
  which are precisely what the component exists to provide. The two requirements
  are only separable by a detector that has learned what a label looks like in
  this setting, rather than what a dark quadrilateral looks like anywhere. This
  is the reason the component is learned; the model itself, and the margin it
  achieves on both requirements, belong to Chapters 4 and 5.

  The training examples are produced without any manual annotation, which is
  what makes the choice practical in this setting. The position of every carton
  and every panel is known, so each object can be projected into the image from
  the camera pose; visibility is then established physically, by casting rays
  and accepting a point only when the first object reached is the one that owns
  it, so that an object hidden behind another is never labelled as visible. The
  buildings used to produce the examples and those used to measure the result
  are disjoint.

  The detector reports a second class, cartons, which are never read. A detected
  carton establishes that a region holds inventory, which is what the semantic
  channel of the map needs in order to distinguish a full rack from an empty one
  before anything has been decoded there.

  3.4.1.4 The Perception Cycle

  The vehicle carries two lateral cameras rather than one forward camera,
  because labels sit on the faces of cartons on both sides of an aisle: a
  vehicle flying down the middle has readable surfaces to its left and to its
  right and nothing of interest ahead. One traversal then serves both racks,
  whereas a forward camera would require the vehicle to turn towards each face
  in turn, and the envelope is narrow enough that this would double the number
  of poses.

  Decoding and detection do not cost the same, and the two together exceed what
  one cycle can afford. The rule adopted is asymmetric: decoding runs on both
  images every cycle, detection runs on one camera per cycle, alternating. The
  justification lies in what each failure costs — a label detected one cycle
  later is still detected, and the vehicle is not going there yet, whereas a
  label not decoded while the vehicle was in position in front of it requires a
  second journey to the same place. Decoding also runs on the whole image rather
  than on the regions the detector proposes, which keeps the two paths
  independent: a label the detector misses can still be read, and a failure of
  the learned component reduces the range of the system without ever costing an
  identifier.

  One last correction is temporal. The renderer delivers a frame one step behind
  the physics, so reading an image together with the pose held at the moment it
  arrives attaches every observation to a position the vehicle has already
  left. The component keeps the two most recent instants and always pairs the
  image of now with the pose and the point cloud of the previous one. Without
  it, the same panel observed while approaching and while receding is written
  twice, in two different places, and the map records two labels where one
  exists.

  Algorithm 3.2 — PerceptionCycle
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: vehicle i, cycle index k, map M, history H (length 2)
  Ensure:  M updated

   1:  (D, r) ← Lidar(i)
   2:  M.IntegrateOccupancy(p_i, D, r)
   3:  M.Announce(i, p_i)
   4:  push (poses of L and R, cloud C) onto H
   5:  if |H| < 2 then return                        ▷ one frame of render lag
   6:  (p_c, R_c, C) ← H[t−1]
   7:  for cam ∈ {L, R} do
   8:      I ← Image(cam)
   9:      for (ℓ, Q) ∈ Decode(I) do
  10:          if ℓ ∉ F then continue                ▷ foreign identifier
  11:          o ← PlaceObservation(Q, K, p_c, R_c, C, M)
  12:          if o ≠ ⊥ then M.WriteRead(ℓ, o)
  13:      end for
  14:      if cam ≡ k (mod 2) then                   ▷ detection alternates
  15:          for (κ, B) ∈ Detect(I) do
  16:              o ← PlaceObservation(B, K, p_c, R_c, C, M)
  17:              if o = ⊥ then continue
  18:              if κ = qr then M.WriteSpotted(o)
  19:              else M.MarkSemantic(o.p)
  20:          end for
  21:      end if
  22:      M.IntegrateCoverage(p_c, R_c e_x)
  23:  end for
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Four rejections appear across the two algorithms, and together they define
  what perception is willing to assert.

  Table 3.4 — Rejection rules and the error each one prevents.

  Rule: Identifier format
  Condition: $\ell \notin \mathcal{F}$
  Error prevented: the warehouse carries QR codes on its signage; without this
  filter they enter the inventory as cartons
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Limiting incidence
  Condition: $\alpha > \alpha_{\max}$
  Error prevented: past that angle the four corners no longer determine a
  reliable normal, and a wrong normal sends a later vehicle to the wrong side
  of a rack
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Reading envelope
  Condition: $r \notin \mathcal{E}$
  Error prevented: a code decoded from an implausible distance is a
  corner-localisation error, not a reading
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Plausibility of placement
  Condition: $\lVert \mathbf{p} - \mathbf{p}c \rVert \notin [\kappa{lo}\hat{d},\

  \kappa_{hi}\hat{d}]$
  Error prevented: a ray through a gap in a rack places a target inside a wall

  Each converts a possible error into a missed observation, which is the trade
  the cost asymmetry of the introduction demands.

  ---
  3.4.1 Perception

  Perception is the only channel through which the system learns anything about
  the building. At each cycle it receives, for one vehicle, the two lateral
  images, the pose $(\mathbf{p}_c, R_c)$ each camera held at capture, and one
  LiDAR revolution; it writes three kinds of fact into the shared map
  $\mathcal{M}$ — decoded identifiers with a position and a normal, labels
  detected but not decoded with a position and an approach direction, and the
  volume established as free or solid.

  $$f_{\mathcal{P}} : \mathcal{I}^2 \times \mathcal{T} \times \mathcal{L}
  \longrightarrow \mathcal{R} \times \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{V} \tag{3.1}$$

  The three do not carry the same risk. A wrong identifier is permanent, since
  nothing downstream re-examines a label declared read. A label reported where
  none exists costs one flight. A refused observation costs nothing, the vehicle
  passing that way again. Perception is therefore built to refuse rather than
  to guess.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.2 — fig_perception_chaine
  ▎ Inside the perception component. The image supplies direction and identity, 
  ▎ the LiDAR supplies range; the two paths write three different kinds of fact 
  ▎ into the shared map.

  3.4.1.1 The Reading Envelope

  A code stops being readable long before it stops being visible. The two usual
  explanations for a failed read — too far, or too oblique — are the same
  phenomenon: a square inclined by $\alpha$ projects exactly as a square
  $1/\cos\alpha$ times more distant, its modules covering proportionally fewer
  pixels in both cases. Distance and angle thus reduce to one quantity, the
  apparent distance:

  $$d_{\text{app}} = \frac{d}{\cos \alpha} \tag{3.2}$$

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.3 — fig_distance_apparente
  ▎ Distance and angle are a single limit. Both panels fill the same cone, so 
  ▎ they produce the same image: a panel tilted by α is equivalent to an 
  ▎ untilted one 1/cos α times further away.

  Measured over the full range of poses, the reading rate depends on this
  quantity alone.

  Table 3.1 — Reading rate as a function of apparent distance.

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────┐
  │ $d_{\text{app}}$ │ Successful reads │ Images │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.00 – 1.25 m    │ 75 %             │ 84     │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.25 – 1.50 m    │ 89 %             │ 115    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.50 – 4.00 m    │ 91 – 99 %        │ 925    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 4.00 – 5.00 m    │ 74 %             │ 167    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 5.00 – 6.50 m    │ 31 %             │ 194    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ beyond 6.50 m    │ 3 %              │ 185    │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────┘

  The envelope follows:

  $$\mathcal{E} = {, (d, \alpha) ;:; 1.5 \le d_{\text{app}} \le 4.0 ,}
  \tag{3.3}$$

  Its two bounds differ in nature. The upper one is optical: past four metres
  the modules cover too few pixels. The lower one is mechanical: closer than a
  metre, a few centimetres of lateral error produce a large incidence, and a
  vehicle cannot hold its heading that tightly. Since an aisle is 3.9 m wide, a
  vehicle can never retreat beyond 3.6 m from a panel — it is therefore the
  angle, not the distance, that lets one position cover several cartons.
  $\mathcal{E}$ is the most structural quantity of the system: every reading
  pose the decision component generates is placed inside it.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.4 — fig_enveloppe
  ▎ Reading rate against apparent distance, 1 670 images at known poses. The 
  ▎ shaded band is the envelope ℰ = [1.5, 4.0] m used by every reading pose in 
  ▎ the system.

  Detection obeys no such limit, since it does not need the modules resolved.
  This gap between the two ranges is what the mission exploits: a vehicle
  travelling down an aisle registers every label in it while reading only those
  it passes close to. Perception is therefore two functions with a third state
  between them.

  Table 3.2 — The three states of perception.

  ┌────────────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │       State        │     What is      │      What is written into       │
  │                    │   established    │          $\mathcal{M}$          │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $\textsc{Read}$    │ the identifier   │ $(\ell, \mathbf{p},             │
  │                    │ itself           │ \mathbf{n})$                    │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │                    │ a label exists   │ $(\mathbf{p}, \mathbf{a})$,     │
  │ $\textsc{Spotted}$ │ at this place    │ $\mathbf{a}$ the direction of   │
  │                    │                  │ approach                        │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $\textsc{Nothing}$ │ —                │ —                               │
  └────────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  That intermediate state drives the mission. A spotted label is not a
  hypothesis to be investigated but a target whose reading is nearly certain,
  since only the approach is missing: it turns exploration, whose return is
  uncertain, into collection, whose return is almost guaranteed.

  3.4.1.2 Three-Dimensional Placement of an Observation

  An image gives a direction, never a position. A range along that direction
  must come from somewhere, and three sources exist: the apparent size of the
  code, the translation of the pose recovered from its four corners, and the
  LiDAR.

  The first two need the physical size, which is known — the generator places on
  each carton face a square panel of side $s = 0.40$ m — but which hides a
  trap. A QR symbol is printed with a quiet margin of two modules around a
  twenty-one module grid, and decoders return the corners of the symbol, not of
  the panel; taking one for the other over-estimates every distance by $25/21 -
  1 \approx 19\ %$. The component therefore works with the code side:

  $$c = s \cdot \frac{21}{25} = 0.336 \ \text{m} \tag{3.4}$$

  The pose recovered from four coplanar corners splits into two quantities of
  unequal robustness. The rotation $R_q$ follows from the shape of the
  quadrilateral alone, so a wrong side length would scale the translation and
  leave it untouched; the translation $\mathbf{t}_q$ depends on it linearly.
  Orientation from the corners therefore holds under any assumption about the
  format, distance from the corners does not.

  The design keeps the corners for orientation and the LiDAR for range. A pixel
  $\mathbf{u}$ is back-projected into a world ray and the range read from the
  LiDAR point passing closest to it:

  $$\rho(\mathbf{u}) = R_c \cdot \iota!\left(
  \frac{K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}}{\lVert K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}} \rVert}
  \right), \qquad \mathbf{p} = \mathbf{p}_c + r,\rho(\mathbf{u}) \tag{3.5}$$

  The motivation is independence, not accuracy — the two sources agree to within
  a centimetre. A placement that depends on a printed format breaks when the
  format changes, and the warehouse the vehicles must handle is by definition
  one they have not seen.

  A label only detected has no corners: no orientation to recover, and the range
  must come from the LiDAR alone. There the sensor sets its own boundary. Its
  angular spacing $\Delta\theta$ is constant, so neighbouring rays separate as
  $r,\Delta\theta$; at eight metres they are already further apart than a map
  cell, and the ray nearest a given direction lands on the neighbouring carton
  or, through a gap in the rack, on the wall behind. Two regimes follow: inside
  $r_{\max} = 8$ m the range is the LiDAR measurement, beyond it the first cell
  already occupied along that direction in $\mathcal{M}$. This is the first
  place where the map is read as a sensor rather than as a memory, and the
  choice is deliberate — one revolution is sparse at eight metres, but the map
  has accumulated many, from many poses and several vehicles.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.5 — fig_regimes
  ▎ Two placement regimes. Inside the LiDAR's trusted range a ray lands on the 
  ▎ label; beyond it the rays are further apart than a map cell, and the range 
  ▎ is taken from the map instead.

  A plausibility test guards that path, and is the one place where the nominal
  size earns its keep: the region width $w$ gives $\hat{d} = f_x,c/w$,
  unreliable in value but right in order of magnitude, and a placement
  disagreeing with it by more than a wide factor is rejected. The window is
  loose because the region is not the code; its purpose is to catch one failure,
  a ray gone through a gap and returned a point far behind the label. Finally,
  a spotted label is written facing the camera that saw it — the only
  directional information surviving the absence of corners, and enough, since it
  records the side from which the rack must later be approached.

  Algorithm 3.1 — PlaceObservation
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: K, (p_c, R_c), cloud C, map M, and either corners Q (decoded)
           or a region B (detected)
  Ensure:  (p, n) ∈ ℝ³ × ℝ³, or ⊥

   1:  if Q is given then                            ▷ decoded code
   2:      (R_q, t_q) ← PnP□(Q, c, K)
   3:      α ← ∠(−t_q , R_q e_z)
   4:      if α > α_max then return ⊥
   5:      u ← centroid(Q)
   6:  else
   7:      u ← centre(B)
   8:  end if
   9:  ρ ← R_c · ι( K⁻¹ũ / ‖K⁻¹ũ‖ )                  ▷ world ray, Eq. (3.5)
  10:  r ← RangeLidar(p_c, ρ, C)
  11:  if r = ⊥ or r > r_max then
  12:      if Q is given then return ⊥               ▷ a read code is always
  close
  13:      r ← RangeMap(p_c, ρ, M)                   ▷ first occupied cell
  14:  end if
  15:  if r = ⊥ then return ⊥
  16:  p ← p_c + r·ρ
  17:  if Q is given then
  18:      if r ∉ E then return ⊥                    ▷ reading envelope, Eq.
  (3.3)
  19:      n ← R_c · ι(R_q e_z), oriented towards p_c
  20:  else
  21:      d̂ ← f_x · c / w(B)
  22:      if ‖p − p_c‖ ∉ [κ_lo·d̂ , κ_hi·d̂] then return ⊥
  23:      n ← −Π_xy ρ / ‖Π_xy ρ‖                    ▷ direction of approach
  24:  end if
  25:  return (p, n)
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  3.4.1.3 Why Detection Must Be Learned

  Extending the detection range is not in itself difficult: a geometric
  detector, built on a threshold and a shape test, is inexpensive and works well
  past the reading envelope. It fails on the only criterion that matters here.
  In a warehouse emptied of all its codes it reports a panel on more than a
  quarter of the images, mistaking cartons, uprights and shadows for labels;
  since every spotted label produces a journey, such a detector would send
  vehicles to more empty places than real ones.

  The requirement is therefore a pair, and it is the pair that decides the
  construction.

  Table 3.3 — The requirement placed on the detector.

  ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │                     │      Geometric      │          Required           │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Range held at 90 %  │ 4.0 m, where        │                             │
  │ detection           │ reading already     │ well beyond $\mathcal{E}$   │
  │                     │ stops               │                             │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Reports on a scene  │ 27 % of images      │ rare enough that a wasted   │
  │ holding no code     │                     │ flight is exceptional       │
  └─────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  No threshold on a geometric detector satisfies both, since raising it to
  suppress false alarms also removes the distant true detections the component
  exists to provide. Only a detector that has learned what a label looks like in
  this setting, rather than what a dark quadrilateral looks like anywhere,
  separates them. The model itself, and the margin it achieves, belong to
  Chapters 4 and 5.

  The training examples are produced without manual annotation. The position of
  every carton and panel being known, each object is projected into the image
  from the camera pose; visibility is then established physically, by casting
  rays and accepting a point only when the first object reached is the one that
  owns it, so an object hidden behind another is never labelled as visible. The
  buildings used to produce the examples and those used to measure the result
  are disjoint.

  The detector reports a second class, cartons, which are never read. A detected
  carton establishes that a region holds inventory, which is what the semantic
  channel of the map needs to distinguish a full rack from an empty one before
  anything has been decoded there.

  3.4.1.4 The Perception Cycle

  The vehicle carries two lateral cameras rather than one forward camera because
  labels sit on both sides of an aisle: a vehicle down the middle has readable
  surfaces left and right and nothing of interest ahead, so one traversal serves
  both racks. A forward camera would require turning towards each face in turn,
  doubling the number of poses.

  Decoding and detection together exceed what one cycle affords, so the rule is
  asymmetric: decoding on both images every cycle, detection on one camera per
  cycle, alternating. A label detected one cycle later is still detected, and
  the vehicle is not going there yet; a label not decoded while the vehicle
  stood in front of it costs a second journey. Decoding also runs on the whole
  image rather than on the detector's regions, which keeps the two paths
  independent — a label the detector misses can still be read.

  One correction is temporal. The renderer is one step behind the physics, so
  pairing an image with the pose held when it arrives attaches every observation
  to a position the vehicle has already left. The component keeps the two most
  recent instants and pairs the image of now with the pose and cloud of the
  previous one. Without it, the same panel seen while approaching and while
  receding is written twice, and the map records two labels where one exists.

  Algorithm 3.2 — PerceptionCycle
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: vehicle i, cycle index k, map M, history H (length 2)
  Ensure:  M updated

   1:  (D, r) ← Lidar(i)
   2:  M.IntegrateOccupancy(p_i, D, r)
   3:  M.Announce(i, p_i)
   4:  push (poses of L and R, cloud C) onto H
   5:  if |H| < 2 then return                        ▷ one frame of render lag
   6:  (p_c, R_c, C) ← H[t−1]
   7:  for cam ∈ {L, R} do
   8:      I ← Image(cam)
   9:      for (ℓ, Q) ∈ Decode(I) do
  10:          if ℓ ∉ F then continue                ▷ foreign identifier
  11:          o ← PlaceObservation(Q, K, p_c, R_c, C, M)
  12:          if o ≠ ⊥ then M.WriteRead(ℓ, o)
  13:      end for
  14:      if cam ≡ k (mod 2) then                   ▷ detection alternates
  15:          for (κ, B) ∈ Detect(I) do
  16:              o ← PlaceObservation(B, K, p_c, R_c, C, M)
  17:              if o = ⊥ then continue
  18:              if κ = qr then M.WriteSpotted(o)
  19:              else M.MarkSemantic(o.p)
  20:          end for
  21:      end if
  22:      M.IntegrateCoverage(p_c, R_c e_x)
  23:  end for
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Four rejections appear across the two algorithms, and together they define
  what perception is willing to assert.

  Table 3.4 — Rejection rules and the error each one prevents.

  Rule: Identifier format
  Condition: $\ell \notin \mathcal{F}$
  Error prevented: the warehouse carries QR codes on its signage; without this
  filter they enter the inventory as cartons
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Limiting incidence
  Condition: $\alpha > \alpha_{\max}$
  Error prevented: past that angle the corners no longer determine a reliable
  normal, sending a later vehicle to the wrong side of a rack
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Reading envelope
  Condition: $r \notin \mathcal{E}$
  Error prevented: a code decoded from an implausible distance is a
  corner-localisation error, not a reading
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Plausibility of placement
  Condition: $\lVert \mathbf{p} - \mathbf{p}c \rVert \notin [\kappa{lo}\hat{d},\

  \kappa_{hi}\hat{d}]$
  Error prevented: a ray through a gap in a rack places a target inside a wall

  Each converts a possible error into a missed observation, which is the trade
  the cost asymmetry of the introduction demands.

● Six. Perception était le premier des sept.

  3.4.2 The Shared Map
  The single memory of the team: a volumetric grid recording what is solid, what
  has been observed and from which direction, every label with its state, and
  where the other vehicles are and what they have claimed.

  3.4.3 Candidate Generation
  Turns the current state of the map into the set of actions worth taking,
  writing three different kinds of work — read a spotted label, observe a face
  never seen from a readable angle, extend the known boundary — in one common
  form, a pose and a viewing direction.

  3.4.4 Target Selection
  Prices every candidate on a single scale, checks that a route to it exists
  through space already established as free, and commits to one.

  3.4.5 Coordination
  Keeps the vehicles apart without a protocol: each announces its target in the
  map, the announcement expires unless renewed, and this one rule also absorbs
  an abandoned target and a vehicle that stops flying.

  3.4.6 The Semantic Guide
  The optional layer: a pre-trained vision-language model is shown what the team
  knows and asked which area to examine next; its answer enters the same scale
  as the rest, weighted, and a weight of zero removes it without changing
  anything else.

  3.4.7 Flight Control
  Executes a chosen pose: transit along the route, slow approach, hold inside
  the reading envelope, and abandon when the route is cut or progress stops.

  Pour ton planning. 3.4.2 et 3.4.4 sont les deux lourdes — la carte porte
  quatre couches et le calcul des chemins, la sélection porte le score et tous
  ses termes. 3.4.3, 3.4.5 et 3.4.7 sont courtes. 3.4.6 est moyenne, et c'est
  celle où il faudra être le plus prudent sur la frontière conception /
  implémentation, puisque le modèle y est nommé.

● Figure 3.3 — fig_distance_apparente

  Sujet. Pourquoi la distance et l'angle ne forment qu'une seule limite de
  lecture.

  Ce qui est représenté. Une caméra, le cône qu'elle voit, et deux panneaux de
  même taille physique placés à l'intérieur de ce cône. L'un est perpendiculaire
  à la direction de visée et plus éloigné ; l'autre est incliné d'un angle α et
  plus proche. Les deux remplissent exactement le cône, donc ils occupent la
  même étendue dans l'image.

  Les deux distances sont mesurées depuis la caméra le long de l'axe : le
  panneau incliné est à $d$, le panneau de face est à $d/\cos\alpha$. L'angle α
  est marqué sur le panneau incliné. La relation $d_{app} = d/\cos\alpha$
  conclut.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Deux situations différentes produisent la
  même image, donc la même difficulté de lecture. Le système n'a pas besoin de
  deux bornes, une distance maximale et un angle maximal, mais d'une seule
  grandeur qui porte les deux.

  Ce qui n'y figure pas. Aucun taux mesuré, aucune bibliothèque, aucune valeur
  chiffrée en dehors de la relation elle-même.

  ---
  Figure 3.5 — fig_regimes

  Sujet. D'où vient la distance d'une détection, et pourquoi cette distance a
  deux régimes.

  Ce qui est représenté. Un drone le long d'une face de rack, vue de dessus. Les
  rayons du lidar partent du drone avec un écart angulaire constant et
  atteignent la face ; leurs points d'arrivée s'écartent de plus en plus à
  mesure que la distance grandit.

  Deux étiquettes sur cette face. Sur la première, proche, un rayon tombe
  directement : sa distance est la mesure du lidar. Sur la seconde, lointaine,
  aucun rayon ne tombe ; le plus proche atteint le carton voisin ou passe par un
  trou du rack. Sa distance vient alors de la carte, premier cube déjà occupé
  le long de la direction — les cubes parcourus le long de cette direction sont
  visibles.

  Une frontière à $r = 8$ m sépare les deux régimes, accompagnée du fait qui la
  justifie : au-delà, deux rayons voisins sont plus écartés qu'un cube de la
  carte. La relation $\delta(r) = r,\Delta\theta$ l'explique.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. La frontière n'est pas un réglage, c'est
  une propriété du capteur : l'écart entre deux rayons croît avec la distance.
  Et au-delà de cette frontière, c'est la carte qui remplace le capteur, parce
  qu'elle a accumulé de nombreux tours là où un seul tour est trop clairsemé.

  Ce qui n'y figure pas. Aucune bibliothèque, aucun modèle, aucune valeur
  chiffrée en dehors des 8 m et de la relation.

● Voilà la même section, mais écrite en phrases complètes. J'ai supprimé les
