  ---
  3.4.1 Perception

  Perception is the part of the system that looks at the building, and it is the
  only part that does. Every other component works from what perception has
  written down. At each cycle, and for one vehicle, it receives four things: the
  image of the left camera, the image of the right camera, the pose that each
  camera had at the moment its image was taken, and one full revolution of the
  LiDAR. From these it produces three kinds of fact, and writes all of them into
  the shared map $\mathcal{M}$ — the identifiers it has managed to decode, each
  with a position and the direction the panel faces; the labels it has found
  without being able to decode them, each with a position and the direction from
  which it will have to be approached; and the volume it has established as
  free or as solid.

  $$f_{\mathcal{P}} : \mathcal{I}^2 \times \mathcal{T} \times \mathcal{L}
  \longrightarrow \mathcal{R} \times \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{V} \tag{3.1}$$

  These three kinds of fact are not equally dangerous when they are wrong. If
  perception reports a wrong identifier, the error is permanent, because no part
  of the system ever returns to check a label that has been declared read; the
  wrong identifier stays in the inventory until the end of the mission. If
  perception reports a label in a place where there is none, the error costs one
  flight, which is expensive but not final. And if perception decides not to
  report something it was unsure about, this costs nothing at all, because a
  vehicle will fly past the same place again later. The component is built
  around this difference: it is designed to stay silent when it is not sure,
  rather than to make a guess.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.2 — fig_perception_chaine
  ▎ Inside the perception component. The image supplies direction and identity, 
  ▎ the LiDAR supplies range; the two paths write three different kinds of fact 
  ▎ into the shared map.

  3.4.1.1 The Reading Envelope

  A QR code becomes unreadable long before it becomes invisible. When a reading
  fails, two explanations are usually given: the code was too far away, or the
  camera was looking at it from too sharp an angle. In reality these two
  explanations describe the same thing. When a square is turned by an angle
  $\alpha$, it appears narrower in the image — exactly as narrow as it would
  appear if it stayed facing the camera but moved $1/\cos\alpha$ times further
  away. In both cases the small black squares that make up the code cover fewer
  pixels, and it is the number of pixels per square that decides whether the
  code can be decoded. Distance and angle can therefore be replaced by a single
  quantity, which we call the apparent distance:

  $$d_{\text{app}} = \frac{d}{\cos \alpha} \tag{3.2}$$

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.3 — fig_distance_apparente
  ▎ Distance and angle are a single limit. Both panels fill the same cone, so 
  ▎ they produce the same image: a panel tilted by α is equivalent to an 
  ▎ untilted one 1/cos α times further away.

  The rate at which codes are successfully read was measured over the whole
  range of positions, and it depends on this single quantity and on nothing
  else.

  Table 3.1 — Reading rate as a function of apparent distance.

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────┐
  │ $d_{\text{app}}$ │ Successful reads │ Images │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.00 – 1.25 m    │ 75 %             │ 84     │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.25 – 1.50 m    │ 89 %             │ 115    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.50 – 4.00 m    │ 91 – 99 %        │ 925    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 4.00 – 5.00 m    │ 74 %             │ 167    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 5.00 – 6.50 m    │ 31 %             │ 194    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ beyond 6.50 m    │ 3 %              │ 185    │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────┘

  From these measurements we define the reading envelope of the system:

  $$\mathcal{E} = {, (d, \alpha) ;:; 1.5 \le d_{\text{app}} \le 4.0 ,}
  \tag{3.3}$$

  The two limits of this envelope do not come from the same place. The upper
  limit is optical: beyond four metres the squares of the code no longer cover
  enough pixels, and no part of the system can do anything about it. The lower
  limit is mechanical: when a vehicle is closer than about one metre, a sideways
  error of only a few centimetres already produces a large angle, and a drone
  in flight cannot hold its heading precisely enough to stay in front of the
  panel. The shape of the building adds a third fact. An aisle between two racks
  is 3.9 metres wide, so a vehicle can never move further than about 3.6 metres
  away from a panel. This means that the way to cover several cartons from one
  position is to use the angle, and not to step back. The envelope is the most
  important quantity in the whole system, because every reading position that
  the decision component produces later is chosen inside it.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.4 — fig_enveloppe
  ▎ Reading rate against apparent distance, 1 670 images at known poses. The 
  ▎ shaded band is the envelope ℰ = [1.5, 4.0] m used by every reading pose in 
  ▎ the system.

  Detecting a label does not have the same limit, because recognising that an
  object is a label does not require reading the small squares inside it. A
  label can still be recognised long after it has stopped being readable. This
  difference between the two ranges is what makes the mission possible: a
  vehicle flying along an aisle can record that a label exists at every point of
  that aisle, even though it can only read the few labels it passes close to.
  For this reason perception is not one function but two, with a third state
  sitting between them.

  Table 3.2 — The three states of perception.

  ┌────────────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │       State        │     What is      │      What is written into       │
  │                    │   established    │          $\mathcal{M}$          │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $\textsc{Read}$    │ the identifier   │ $(\ell, \mathbf{p},             │
  │                    │ itself           │ \mathbf{n})$                    │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │                    │ a label exists   │ $(\mathbf{p}, \mathbf{a})$,     │
  │ $\textsc{Spotted}$ │ at this place    │ with $\mathbf{a}$ the direction │
  │                    │                  │  of approach                    │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $\textsc{Nothing}$ │ —                │ —                               │
  └────────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  This middle state is what keeps the mission moving. Once a label has been
  spotted, it is no longer a guess that needs to be checked; it is a target that
  will almost certainly be read, because the only thing still missing is for a
  vehicle to come close enough to it. Spotting therefore turns exploration,
  where the result is uncertain, into collection, where the result is nearly
  guaranteed.

  3.4.1.2 Three-Dimensional Placement of an Observation

  An image tells the system in which direction something lies, but never how far
  away it is. To place an observation in a three-dimensional map, a distance
  along that direction is needed, and there are three ways to obtain one: from
  the apparent size of the code in the image, from the translation returned when
  the pose of its four corners is solved, or from the LiDAR.

  The first two ways both need the real size of the code. That size is known in
  our case, because the generator places on each face of a carton a square panel
  whose side measures $s = 0.40$ m. Using it, however, is not as simple as it
  appears. A QR code is always printed with an empty margin around it, two
  modules on each side of a grid of twenty-one modules, and every decoder
  returns the corners of the code itself rather than the corners of the panel
  that carries it. If the two are confused, every distance computed from them is
  too large by $25/21 - 1$, that is by about 19 %. The component therefore
  never works with the side of the panel but with the side of the code:

  $$c = s \cdot \frac{21}{25} = 0.336 \ \text{m} \tag{3.4}$$

  When the four corners of a code are available, solving for its pose gives two
  results at once, a rotation and a translation, and these two results are not
  equally reliable. The rotation depends only on the shape of the quadrilateral
  seen in the image, so an error on the assumed size would leave it unchanged.
  The translation, on the contrary, is directly proportional to that size. This
  is why the orientation obtained from the corners can be trusted whatever the
  panel format happens to be, while the distance obtained from the corners can
  only be trusted as far as the size assumption can.

  The design therefore uses the corners for the orientation and the LiDAR for
  the distance. A pixel is turned into a ray in the world frame, and the
  distance is read from the LiDAR point that passes closest to that ray:

  $$\rho(\mathbf{u}) = R_c \cdot \iota!\left(
  \frac{K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}}{\lVert K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}} \rVert}
  \right), \qquad \mathbf{p} = \mathbf{p}_c + r,\rho(\mathbf{u}) \tag{3.5}$$

  The reason for this choice is not precision, since the two sources of distance
  agree to within a centimetre. The reason is independence. If the position of
  a label depended on a printing format, the system would break on the day that
  format changed, and the warehouse these vehicles have to inventory is by
  definition one they have never seen. Keeping the distance free of that
  assumption costs nothing here, and it removes a hidden link between perception
  and the particular building it is working in.

  A label that has only been spotted is a harder case, because the detector
  returns a region of the image and not four corners. There is no orientation to
  recover, and the distance can only come from the LiDAR. Here the sensor
  itself creates a boundary. Its rays leave at a constant angular spacing, so
  the gap between two neighbouring rays grows with range, following $\delta(r) =
  r,\Delta\theta$. At eight metres, two neighbouring rays are already further
  apart than one cell of the map. Past that point, the ray closest to a given
  direction no longer touches the object that was detected; it touches the
  carton beside it, or it passes through a gap in the rack and reaches the wall
  behind. The component therefore works in two regimes. Within eight metres the
  distance is the LiDAR measurement. Beyond eight metres the distance is taken
  from the map instead, as the first cell along that direction that is already
  known to be occupied. This is the first place in the system where the map is
  used as a sensor rather than as a memory, and the choice is deliberate: a
  single revolution of the LiDAR is sparse at eight metres, but the map has
  collected many revolutions, from many positions and from several vehicles, so
  it is dense exactly where one revolution is not.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.5 — fig_regimes
  ▎ Two placement regimes. Inside the LiDAR's trusted range a ray lands on the 
  ▎ label; beyond it the rays are further apart than a map cell, and the range 
  ▎ is taken from the map instead.

  One last test protects this path, and it is the only place where the nominal
  size of the code is genuinely useful. The width $w$ of the detected region
  gives an approximate distance $\hat{d} = f_x,c/w$. This value cannot be
  trusted precisely, because the region drawn around a code is wider than the
  code itself and may be cut by an obstacle, but it is correct in order of
  magnitude. Any placement that disagrees with it by more than a wide factor is
  rejected. The window is kept deliberately wide, since its only job is to catch
  one specific failure: a ray that has gone through a gap in a rack and
  returned a point far behind the label it was aimed at.

  Finally, a spotted label is recorded as facing the camera that saw it. This is
  the only piece of directional information that survives when the corners are
  missing, and it is enough for what follows, because it says from which side of
  the rack the label will later have to be approached.

  Algorithm 3.1 — PlaceObservation
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: K, (p_c, R_c), cloud C, map M, and either corners Q (decoded)
           or a region B (detected)
  Ensure:  (p, n) ∈ ℝ³ × ℝ³, or ⊥

   1:  if Q is given then                            ▷ decoded code
   2:      (R_q, t_q) ← PnP□(Q, c, K)
   3:      α ← ∠(−t_q , R_q e_z)
   4:      if α > α_max then return ⊥
   5:      u ← centroid(Q)
   6:  else
   7:      u ← centre(B)
   8:  end if
   9:  ρ ← R_c · ι( K⁻¹ũ / ‖K⁻¹ũ‖ )                  ▷ world ray, Eq. (3.5)
  10:  r ← RangeLidar(p_c, ρ, C)
  11:  if r = ⊥ or r > r_max then
  12:      if Q is given then return ⊥               ▷ a read code is always
  close
  13:      r ← RangeMap(p_c, ρ, M)                   ▷ first occupied cell
  14:  end if
  15:  if r = ⊥ then return ⊥
  16:  p ← p_c + r·ρ
  17:  if Q is given then
  18:      if r ∉ E then return ⊥                    ▷ reading envelope, Eq.
  (3.3)
  19:      n ← R_c · ι(R_q e_z), oriented towards p_c
  20:  else
  21:      d̂ ← f_x · c / w(B)
  22:      if ‖p − p_c‖ ∉ [κ_lo·d̂ , κ_hi·d̂] then return ⊥
  23:      n ← −Π_xy ρ / ‖Π_xy ρ‖                    ▷ direction of approach
  24:  end if
  25:  return (p, n)
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  3.4.1.3 Why Detection Must Be Learned

  Making the detection range long is not difficult in itself. A geometric
  detector, built from a threshold and a few shape tests, is cheap to run and
  works well beyond the reading envelope. It fails on the only criterion that
  really matters here. In a warehouse from which every code has been removed,
  such a detector still reports a panel on more than a quarter of the images,
  because it mistakes cartons, rack uprights and shadows for labels. Since every
  spotted label produces a flight, a detector with that rate would send
  vehicles to more empty places than real ones, and the system would finish the
  inventory faster with the detector switched off.

  The requirement placed on this element is therefore a pair of conditions
  rather than a single number, and it is the pair that decides how the element
  has to be built.

  Table 3.3 — The requirement placed on the detector.

  ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │                     │      Geometric      │          Required           │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Range held at 90 %  │ 4.0 m, where        │                             │
  │ detection           │ reading already     │ well beyond $\mathcal{E}$   │
  │                     │ stops               │                             │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Reports on a scene  │ 27 % of images      │ rare enough that a wasted   │
  │ holding no code     │                     │ flight is exceptional       │
  └─────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  No threshold applied to a geometric detector can satisfy both conditions at
  once. Raising the threshold does remove the false alarms, but it also removes
  the distant true detections, which are exactly what the element exists to
  provide. The two conditions can only be separated by a detector that has
  learned what a label looks like in this particular setting, instead of
  recognising what a dark quadrilateral looks like anywhere. This is the reason
  the detector is learned. The model itself, and the margin it achieves on both
  conditions, belong to Chapters 4 and 5.

  The training examples are produced without any manual annotation, and this is
  what makes the choice practical here. Since the position of every carton and
  every panel is known, each object can be projected into the image using the
  exact pose of the camera. Projection alone would not be enough, because an
  object hidden behind another would then be labelled as visible; visibility is
  therefore established physically, by casting rays towards points on each
  surface and accepting a point only when the first object the ray reaches is
  the one that owns it. The buildings used to produce the examples and the
  buildings used to measure the result are kept separate.

  The detector also reports a second class, cartons, which are never read. A
  detected carton tells the system that a region holds inventory, and this is
  what the semantic layer of the map needs in order to tell a full rack from an
  empty one before anything has been decoded there.

  3.4.1.4 The Perception Cycle

  The vehicle carries two lateral cameras instead of one forward camera, and the
  reason comes from the shape of the building. Labels sit on the faces of
  cartons on both sides of an aisle, so a vehicle flying down the middle has
  readable surfaces on its left and on its right, and nothing of interest
  straight ahead. With two lateral cameras, one pass along an aisle serves both
  racks. With a forward camera, the vehicle would have to turn towards each face
  in turn, and since the reading envelope is narrow, this would roughly double
  the number of positions the mission has to visit.

  Decoding an image and running the detector on it do not cost the same, and the
  two together are more than one cycle can afford. The rule adopted is
  therefore asymmetric: decoding runs on both images at every cycle, while
  detection runs on only one camera per cycle, alternating between them. The
  justification lies in what each missed opportunity costs. A label detected one
  cycle later is still detected, and the vehicle was not going there yet in any
  case. A label that is not decoded while the vehicle is standing in front of
  it, on the other hand, requires a second journey to the same place. Decoding
  is also applied to the whole image rather than to the regions proposed by the
  detector, which keeps the two paths independent of each other: a label the
  detector misses can still be read, so a weakness of the learned element
  reduces the range of the system without ever costing it an identifier.

  One correction remains, and it is a question of timing. The renderer delivers
  each frame one step behind the physics. If an image were paired with the pose
  the vehicle holds at the moment the image arrives, every observation would be
  attached to a position the vehicle has already left. The component therefore
  keeps the two most recent instants and always pairs the image of the current
  instant with the pose and the point cloud of the previous one. Without this
  correction, the same panel seen once while approaching and once while moving
  away is written into the map twice, in two different places, and the map ends
  up holding two labels where there is only one.

  Algorithm 3.2 — PerceptionCycle
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: vehicle i, cycle index k, map M, history H (length 2)
  Ensure:  M updated

   1:  (D, r) ← Lidar(i)
   2:  M.IntegrateOccupancy(p_i, D, r)
   3:  M.Announce(i, p_i)
   4:  push (poses of L and R, cloud C) onto H
   5:  if |H| < 2 then return                        ▷ one frame of render lag
   6:  (p_c, R_c, C) ← H[t−1]
   7:  for cam ∈ {L, R} do
   8:      I ← Image(cam)
   9:      for (ℓ, Q) ∈ Decode(I) do
  10:          if ℓ ∉ F then continue                ▷ foreign identifier
  11:          o ← PlaceObservation(Q, K, p_c, R_c, C, M)
  12:          if o ≠ ⊥ then M.WriteRead(ℓ, o)
  13:      end for
  14:      if cam ≡ k (mod 2) then                   ▷ detection alternates
  15:          for (κ, B) ∈ Detect(I) do
  16:              o ← PlaceObservation(B, K, p_c, R_c, C, M)
  17:              if o = ⊥ then continue
  18:              if κ = qr then M.WriteSpotted(o)
  19:              else M.MarkSemantic(o.p)
  20:          end for
  21:      end if
  22:      M.IntegrateCoverage(p_c, R_c e_x)
  23:  end for
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Four rejection rules appear across the two algorithms, and together they
  define what perception is willing to state.

  Table 3.4 — Rejection rules and the error each one prevents.

  Rule: Identifier format
  Condition: $\ell \notin \mathcal{F}$
  Error prevented: the warehouse carries QR codes on its own signage, and
  without
  this filter they would be entered into the inventory as cartons
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Limiting incidence
  Condition: $\alpha > \alpha_{\max}$
  Error prevented: beyond that angle the four corners no longer give a reliable
  normal, and a wrong normal would send a later vehicle to the wrong side of
  the rack
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Reading envelope
  Condition: $r \notin \mathcal{E}$
  Error prevented: a code decoded from an impossible distance is a
  corner-localisation error, not a reading
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Plausibility of placement
  Condition: $\lVert \mathbf{p} - \mathbf{p}c \rVert \notin [\kappa{lo}\hat{d},\

  \kappa_{hi}\hat{d}]$
  Error prevented: a ray that has passed through a gap in a rack would otherwise

  place a target inside a wall

  Each of these rules turns a possible error into a missed observation, which is
  exactly the trade that the difference in cost described at the beginning of
  this section requires.

  3.4.2 The Shared Map

  The shared map is the memory of the team. It is a single object: the vehicles
  do not each keep their own version and then try to agree, they all write into
  the same structure and all read from it. Everything a vehicle establishes goes
  there, and everything a vehicle decides is based on what it finds there. This
  is also the only place where the vehicles meet, since they never exchange
  messages directly.

  The map answers four questions, and the rest of the system asks nothing else
  of it. Where is it solid and where is it free? Which surfaces have already
  been looked at from a position where a code on them would have been readable?
  Which labels exist, where are they, and which of them have been read? And
  where are the other vehicles, and what have they claimed?

  The whole structure holds in about one and a half megabytes, which matters
  more than it seems. A memory of that size can be updated many times per second
  and read by every vehicle at every decision, so nothing in the design has to
  be sacrificed to keep the sharing affordable.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.6 — fig_carte_couches
  ▎ The four layers of the shared map, what is written into each of them, and 
  ▎ which component reads them.

  3.4.2.1 Why Two Structures Rather Than One

  Space and labels are not remembered in the same way, and trying to store both
  in the same structure loses something in each case.

  Space is stored in a grid. The building is cut into cubes of 25 cm on a side,
  and each cube records what is known about the volume it covers. This is the
  right shape for space, because a question such as "can a vehicle pass here" is
  a question about a region, not about a point, and because a grid can be
  written into from thousands of LiDAR rays per second without any search.

  Labels are stored in a table instead. A label that has been read has an
  identity, its code, and a position that perception has established to within
  about a centimetre. Putting it into a cube of 25 cm would throw that precision
  away, and it would also make it impossible to distinguish two labels lying in
  the same cube. A table keeps each panel as its own entry, with its code, its
  position, the direction it faces, and how many times it has been read.

  Table 3.5 — The four layers and their role.

  ┌─────────────┬─────────────────────────┬─────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │    Layer    │     What it stores      │ Written by  │     Read by      │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │             │ solid, free, or not yet │             │ route planning,  │
  │ Occupancy   │  observed               │ the LiDAR   │ candidate        │
  │             │                         │             │ generation       │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │             │ which cells have been   │             │                  │
  │ Oriented    │ looked at, and from     │ the reading │ candidate        │
  │ coverage    │ which of the four       │  cameras    │ generation       │
  │             │ horizontal sides        │             │                  │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │             │                         │             │ candidate        │
  │ Label table │ every panel and every   │ perception  │ generation,      │
  │             │ unidentified track      │             │ mission          │
  │             │                         │             │ supervision      │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Teammates   │ vehicle positions and   │ each        │ target selection │
  │ and claims  │ the targets they hold   │ vehicle     │                  │
  └─────────────┴─────────────────────────┴─────────────┴──────────────────┘

  3.4.2.2 Occupancy: Adding Evidence Instead of Replacing It

  Each revolution of the LiDAR gives a set of directions and, for each
  direction, a distance. Two facts can be drawn from a single ray: the space it
  crossed before it stopped is free, and the point where it stopped is solid.
  Both are written into the grid.

  The important design decision is that the grid does not store the last thing
  observed, but the sum of everything observed. Each cell holds a value that
  increases when the cell is measured as solid and decreases when it is crossed
  as free, and the state of the cell is read from that value:

  $$L(c) ;\leftarrow; \mathrm{clip}\big(L(c) + \ell\big), \qquad
  \text{state}(c) = \begin{cases}
  \text{solid} & L(c) > \tau^{+}\
  \text{free} & L(c) < \tau^{-}\
  \text{unknown} & \text{otherwise}
  \end{cases} \tag{3.6}$$

  If the grid simply stored the last measurement, then a single wrong return — a
  reflection, a ray that grazed a corner, a moving vehicle passing through —
  would turn an aisle into a wall, and a path would disappear. Because evidence
  accumulates, a cell that has been crossed as free ten times and then measured
  as solid once stays free. Wrong measurements are absorbed rather than
  believed, and a real obstacle still appears quickly, because it is measured
  again at every revolution for as long as the vehicle can see it.

  Two details make this work in practice. A ray deposits its free evidence at
  several points inside each cell it crosses, so that a ray passing near the
  corner of a cell still marks it, instead of leaving an unknown hole in the
  middle of free space. And the free evidence stops one full cell before the
  impact, so that a ray does not erase the very surface it has just measured.

  Algorithm 3.3 — IntegrateRevolution
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: origin o, unit directions d_i, ranges r_i, grid L
  Ensure:  L updated

   1:  for each ray i do
   2:      r ← min(r_i , r_max)                   ▷ only the trusted part is
  used
   3:      for each sample point p along d_i up to r − one cell do
   4:          L(cell(p)) ← clip( L(cell(p)) + ℓ_free )
   5:      end for
   6:      if r_i is finite and r_i ≤ r_max then
   7:          L(cell(o + r_i·d_i)) ← clip( L(cell(o + r_i·d_i)) + ℓ_solid )
   8:      end if
   9:  end for
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  3.4.2.3 Coverage Is Oriented, Not Just Spatial

  A vehicle that has flown past a rack has certainly seen it. That does not mean
  it could have read anything on it. The map therefore does not record that a
  cell has been seen; it records something stricter and more useful, namely that
  a code placed in this cell, facing this particular direction, would have been
  readable.

  The difference matters because a panel is only legible from the side it faces.
  A cell observed from the far end of an aisle, or from behind the rack through
  a gap, has been seen, but no code on the face of the carton it contains could
  have been decoded from there. If the map treated these observations as
  coverage, the team would declare a rack finished after flying past it once,
  and the mission would end with most of the inventory unread.

  Each cell therefore carries four bits, one for each horizontal side. A bit is
  set only when the cell has been looked at from that side and when the apparent
  distance at that moment fell inside the reading envelope $\mathcal{E}$ of
  Section 3.4.1.1, with no known obstacle in between. Asking whether a panel of
  normal $\mathbf{n}$ would have been covered is then a test on a single bit:

  $$\text{covered}(c, \mathbf{n}) ;=; \big( C(c);&;2^{,\kappa(-\mathbf{n})}
  \big) \neq 0,
  \qquad \kappa : \text{direction} \rightarrow {0,1,2,3} \tag{3.7}$$

  where $\kappa$ returns the nearest of the four horizontal sides. This
  definition is what allows candidate generation, in Section 3.4.3, to speak of
  a surface that still has to be visited without ever needing to know whether a
  label is actually there.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.7 — fig_couverture_orientee
  ▎ Why coverage is oriented. The same cell seen from two different sides: only 
  ▎ one of the two observations would have allowed a code on the carton face to 
  ▎ be read.

  3.4.2.4 The Label Table: Identity, and the Absence of Identity

  Two kinds of entry live in the table, and they behave differently because one
  has a name and the other does not.

  A panel is a label whose code has been decoded. It carries that code, a
  position, the direction it faces, and a count of how many times it has been
  read; each new reading of the same panel is averaged into the position rather
  than replacing it, so the estimate improves as the vehicle approaches.

  A track is a label that has been detected without being decoded. It has no
  identity, only a position that is a good guess and a direction from which it
  was seen. Several sightings of the same place are merged into one track and
  averaged, and the direction is averaged too: a vehicle flying along an aisle
  sees the same code first at a sharp angle and then face-on, and the average of
  those directions moves towards the true normal of the panel.

  One rule governs the relation between the two kinds of entry, and it comes
  from the shape of the objects being inventoried. Each carton carries the same
  code on two opposite faces. Two readings of the same code that are far apart
  are therefore not a contradiction: they are the two faces of one carton, and
  both are recorded. But a carton has only two faces, so a third reading of the
  same code, far from the two already known, cannot be a new panel and is
  rejected as a decoding error. The rule is written as a distance test:

  $$\text{if } \min_{q \in \text{faces}(\ell)} \lVert \mathbf{p}_q - \mathbf{p}
  \rVert \le \varepsilon
  ;\Rightarrow; \text{same face, average it};
  \quad \text{else if } |\text{faces}(\ell)| < 2 ;\Rightarrow; \text{second
  face};
  \quad \text{else reject} \tag{3.8}$$

  A reading also settles anything uncertain near it. When a code is decoded,
  every track within the same distance is deleted, because a track sitting where
  a panel has just been identified is that same panel, placed approximately by
  a detection made from far away. This is how uncertain information is removed
  from the map as soon as certain information replaces it, and it is the reason
  the number of tracks does not grow without limit over a long mission.

  3.4.2.5 Routes, and the Price of the Unknown

  The map is the only structure that knows where the obstacles are, so it is
  also the map that computes routes. Planning is done on a horizontal view of a
  slice of the building around the flight altitude, and every cell of that view
  is given a cost:

  $$\text{cost}(c) = \begin{cases}
  \infty & \text{a known obstacle, widened by the radius of the vehicle}\
  1 & \text{known free}\
  K_u & \text{not yet observed}
  \end{cases} \tag{3.9}$$

  Two decisions are contained in this small definition, and both matter more
  than their size suggests.

  The first is that obstacles are widened by the radius of the vehicle before
  planning, rather than the vehicle checking its clearance while flying. A route
  that exists on this grid can be flown as a line between waypoints, and the
  question of whether the aircraft fits has already been answered. The widening
  uses the true radius without rounding up to a whole number of cells, because
  rounding 0.6 m up to three cells would give 0.75 m and would close the narrow
  aisles of the building entirely.

  The second is that unknown space is expensive but not forbidden, with $K_u =
  20$. If the unknown were forbidden, a vehicle could never leave the region it
  had already explored, and the mission would stop after the first few metres.
  If the unknown were free, a vehicle would cut straight through a rack it had
  never observed, because on the map that rack is simply not there yet. Making
  it cost twenty times a known free cell produces the behaviour that is wanted:
  the planner goes around through known space whenever a reasonable detour
  exists, and commits to the unknown only when there is no alternative.

  The altitude slice is defined asymmetrically, and this too is a design
  decision rather than a parameter. The planner looks at everything from two
  metres below the vehicle up to a little under one metre above it. Flying over
  the top of a rack, between its uprights, is possible in a simulator and would
  be a collision in a real building; taking a deep slice below the vehicle makes
  the planner refuse it and go around instead.

  Finally, teammates in flight are written into the same cost grid as moving
  obstacles. Two vehicles therefore avoid each other when their routes are
  computed, well before they are close, rather than through a last-second
  manoeuvre. A route that has already been computed is re-checked as the vehicle
  follows it, and recomputed as soon as a newly discovered obstacle cuts it —
  which is exactly what happens when an obstacle appears in an aisle during the
  mission.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.8 — fig_cout_inconnu
  ▎ The cost grid and the two routes it produces: the detour through known free 
  ▎ space when one exists, and the crossing of unknown space when nothing else 
  ▎ remains.

  Algorithm 3.4 — Route
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: start a, goal b, altitude z, occupancy grid
  Ensure:  a list of waypoints, or ⊥ if no route exists

   1:  cost ← CostView(z − below, z + above)         ▷ Eq. (3.9)
   2:  add every teammate in flight as an obstacle in cost
   3:  if b is not reachable in cost then return ⊥
   4:  if the straight line a → b crosses only known free cells then
   5:      return [ ]                                 ▷ no waypoint needed
   6:  end if
   7:  path ← A*(cost, a, b)
   8:  if path = ⊥ then return ⊥
   9:  return Simplify(path)                          ▷ keep only the turns
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  3.4.2.6 The Team Layer

  The last layer of the map holds the position of every vehicle and the target
  each one currently claims. The map only stores this information and lets it
  expire; the rules that decide who claims what, and what happens when a claim
  is not renewed, belong to the coordination component and are described in
  Section 3.4.5. What matters here is that this information lives in the same
  structure as everything else. A vehicle that wants to know what its teammates
  are doing does not ask them — it reads the map, exactly as it reads the
  occupancy or the label table, and it does so at the same moment and at the
  same cost.

  ---
  Description des trois figures à générer

  Figure 3.6 — fig_carte_couches

  Sujet. Ce que la carte partagée contient, et qui l'écrit et la lit.

  Ce qui est représenté. Les quatre couches : l'occupation, la couverture
  orientée, la table des panneaux, et la couche des coéquipiers. Pour chacune,
  ce qu'elle retient et sous quelle forme — l'occupation et la couverture sont
  des grilles de cubes, la table des panneaux et la couche des coéquipiers sont
  des listes d'entrées nommées. À gauche, qui écrit dans chaque couche : le
  lidar écrit l'occupation, les caméras de lecture écrivent la couverture, la
  perception écrit la table, chaque drone écrit sa propre position et sa
  revendication. À droite, qui les lit : la génération de candidats lit les
  trois premières, la sélection lit la dernière, le calcul des chemins lit
  l'occupation, la supervision lit la table.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Qu'il n'existe qu'une seule instance de
  cette structure pour toute l'équipe, et que les composants ne communiquent
  jamais entre eux : ils écrivent et lisent ici.

  Ce qui n'y figure pas. Aucune dimension de grille, aucun nom de bibliothèque,
  aucun format de fichier.

  ---
  Figure 3.7 — fig_couverture_orientee

  Sujet. Pourquoi être vu ne suffit pas, et pourquoi la couverture retient une
  direction.

  Ce qui est représenté. Un carton portant une étiquette sur une de ses faces,
  dans un rack, vu de dessus. Deux positions de drone observent le même cube de
  la carte. La première regarde la face depuis le côté vers lequel elle est
  tournée, à une distance apparente comprise dans l'enveloppe de lecture :
  l'étiquette aurait été lisible, le bit correspondant à ce côté est posé. La
  seconde regarde le même cube depuis l'autre côté du rack, ou depuis le bout de
  l'allée sous un angle trop rasant : le cube a bien été vu, mais aucune
  étiquette tournée de cette façon n'aurait pu y être lue, et aucun bit n'est
  posé pour ce côté.

  Les quatre côtés horizontaux d'un cube apparaissent, avec l'état de leur bit,
  pour montrer que la couverture est une information par direction et non une
  information unique.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Qu'un rack longé une fois n'est pas un rack
  terminé, et que c'est cette distinction qui empêche l'équipe de déclarer
  close une zone qu'elle n'a jamais regardée du bon côté.

  Ce qui n'y figure pas. Aucun chiffre en dehors des bornes de l'enveloppe de
  lecture.

  ---
  Figure 3.8 — fig_cout_inconnu

  Sujet. Comment la carte calcule un chemin, et ce que coûte l'inconnu.

  Ce qui est représenté. Une vue de dessus d'une tranche de l'entrepôt, où
  chaque cellule porte l'un des trois coûts : infini sur un obstacle connu
  élargi du rayon du drone, un sur du libre connu, vingt sur de l'inconnu.
  L'élargissement des obstacles est visible comme une bordure autour de chaque
  rack, distincte du rack lui-même.

  Deux trajets partant du même point. Le premier a une alternative par de
  l'espace libre connu : il fait le détour, même long, parce que traverser
  l'inconnu coûterait davantage. Le second n'en a pas : sa destination n'est
  atteignable qu'en traversant une zone jamais observée, et le chemin y entre.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Que l'inconnu n'est ni interdit ni gratuit,
  et que ces deux extrêmes produiraient chacun un échec — un drone qui ne
  quitte jamais sa zone explorée, ou un drone qui coupe à travers un rack qu'il
  n'a pas encore vu.

  Ce qui n'y figure pas. Aucun algorithme de recherche nommé, aucune structure
  de données, aucune durée.

● 3.4 Detailed Design of the System Components

  The seven elements introduced in Figure 3.1 are described in turn: perception,
  the shared map, candidate generation, target selection, coordination, the
  semantic guide, and flight control. Each is presented in the same order — what
  the element must produce, why the obvious construction fails, the design that
  was retained, and the measurements that fixed its parameters. The symbols
  used across these sections are collected once, below, so that they do not have
  to be reintroduced each time.

  ---
  3.4.1 Perception

  Perception is the part of the system that looks at the building, and it is the
  only part that does. Every other component works from what perception has
  written down. At each cycle, and for one vehicle, it receives four things: the
  image of the left camera, the image of the right camera, the pose
  $(\mathbf{p}_c, R_c)$ that each camera had at the moment its image was taken,
  and one full revolution of the LiDAR. From these it produces three kinds of
  fact, and writes all of them into the shared map $\mathcal{M}$:

  $$f_{\mathcal{P}} : \mathcal{I}^2 \times \mathcal{T} \times \mathcal{L}
  \longrightarrow \mathcal{R} \times \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{V} \tag{3.1}$$

  In this expression $\mathcal{I}$ is the space of camera images, $\mathcal{T}$
  that of camera poses and $\mathcal{L}$ that of LiDAR revolutions. On the other
  side, $\mathcal{R}$ holds the panels whose identifier has been decoded, each
  with a position and the direction the panel faces; $\mathcal{S}$ holds the
  labels that were found without being decoded, each with a position and the
  direction from which they will have to be approached; and $\mathcal{V}$ is the
  volume that has been established as free or as solid.

  These three kinds of fact are not equally dangerous when they are wrong. If
  perception reports a wrong identifier, the error is permanent, because no part
  of the system ever returns to check a label that has been declared read; the
  wrong identifier stays in the inventory until the end of the mission. If
  perception reports a label in a place where there is none, the error costs one
  flight, which is expensive but not final. And if perception decides not to
  report something it was unsure about, this costs nothing at all, because a
  vehicle will fly past the same place again later. The component is built
  around this difference: it is designed to stay silent when it is not sure,
  rather than to make a guess.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.2 — fig_perception_chaine
  ▎ Inside the perception component. The image supplies direction and identity, 
  ▎ the LiDAR supplies range; the two paths write three different kinds of fact 
  ▎ into the shared map.

  3.4.1.1 The Reading Envelope

  A QR code becomes unreadable long before it becomes invisible. When a reading
  fails, two explanations are usually given: the code was too far away, or the
  camera was looking at it from too sharp an angle. In reality these two
  explanations describe the same thing. When a square is turned by an angle
  $\alpha$, it appears narrower in the image — exactly as narrow as it would
  appear if it stayed facing the camera but moved $1/\cos\alpha$ times further
  away. In both cases the small black squares that make up the code cover fewer
  pixels, and it is the number of pixels per square that decides whether the
  code can be decoded. Distance and angle can therefore be replaced by a single
  quantity, which we call the apparent distance:

  $$d_{\text{app}} = \frac{d}{\cos \alpha} \tag{3.2}$$

  Here $d$ is the distance from the camera to the centre of the code, and
  $\alpha$ is the angle between the direction in which the camera sees the panel
  and the direction the panel faces. When the panel is seen straight on,
  $\alpha$ is zero and the apparent distance is simply the true distance; as the
  angle grows, the apparent distance grows with it, and the reading becomes as
  difficult as if the vehicle had moved away.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.3 — fig_distance_apparente
  ▎ Distance and angle are a single limit. Both panels fill the same cone, so 
  ▎ they produce the same image: a panel tilted by α is equivalent to an 
  ▎ untilted one 1/cos α times further away.

  The rate at which codes are successfully read was measured over the whole
  range of positions, and it depends on this single quantity and on nothing
  else.

  Table 3.1 — Reading rate as a function of apparent distance.

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────┐
  │ $d_{\text{app}}$ │ Successful reads │ Images │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.00 – 1.25 m    │ 75 %             │ 84     │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.25 – 1.50 m    │ 89 %             │ 115    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 1.50 – 4.00 m    │ 91 – 99 %        │ 925    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 4.00 – 5.00 m    │ 74 %             │ 167    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ 5.00 – 6.50 m    │ 31 %             │ 194    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┤
  │ beyond 6.50 m    │ 3 %              │ 185    │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────┘

  From these measurements we define the reading envelope of the system, written
  $\mathcal{E}$, as the set of positions and angles from which a code can be
  expected to be read:

  $$\mathcal{E} = {, (d, \alpha) ;:; 1.5 \le d_{\text{app}} \le 4.0 ,}
  \tag{3.3}$$

  The two limits of this envelope do not come from the same place. The upper
  limit is optical: beyond four metres the squares of the code no longer cover
  enough pixels, and no part of the system can do anything about it. The lower
  limit is mechanical: when a vehicle is closer than about one metre, a sideways
  error of only a few centimetres already produces a large angle, and a drone
  in flight cannot hold its heading precisely enough to stay in front of the
  panel. The shape of the building adds a third fact. An aisle between two racks
  is 3.9 metres wide, so a vehicle can never move further than about 3.6 metres
  away from a panel. This means that the way to cover several cartons from one
  position is to use the angle, and not to step back. The envelope is the most
  important quantity in the whole system, because every reading position that
  the decision component produces later is chosen inside it.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.4 — fig_enveloppe
  ▎ Reading rate against apparent distance, 1 670 images at known poses. The 
  ▎ shaded band is the envelope ℰ = [1.5, 4.0] m used by every reading pose in 
  ▎ the system.

  Detecting a label does not have the same limit, because recognising that an
  object is a label does not require reading the small squares inside it. A
  label can still be recognised long after it has stopped being readable. This
  difference between the two ranges is what makes the mission possible: a
  vehicle flying along an aisle can record that a label exists at every point of
  that aisle, even though it can only read the few labels it passes close to.
  For this reason perception is not one function but two, with a third state
  sitting between them.

  Table 3.2 — The three states of perception.

  ┌────────────────────┬─────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │       State        │    What is      │      What is written into        │
  │                    │   established   │          $\mathcal{M}$           │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │                    │ the identifier  │ the identifier $\ell$, a         │
  │ $\textsc{Read}$    │ itself          │ position $\mathbf{p}$, and the   │
  │                    │                 │ normal $\mathbf{n}$ of the panel │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │                    │ a label exists  │ a position $\mathbf{p}$ and a    │
  │ $\textsc{Spotted}$ │ at this place   │ direction of approach            │
  │                    │                 │ $\mathbf{a}$                     │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $\textsc{Nothing}$ │ —               │ —                                │
  └────────────────────┴─────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  This middle state is what keeps the mission moving. Once a label has been
  spotted, it is no longer a guess that needs to be checked; it is a target that
  will almost certainly be read, because the only thing still missing is for a
  vehicle to come close enough to it. Spotting therefore turns exploration,
  where the result is uncertain, into collection, where the result is nearly
  guaranteed.

  3.4.1.2 Three-Dimensional Placement of an Observation

  An image tells the system in which direction something lies, but never how far
  away it is. To place an observation in a three-dimensional map, a distance
  along that direction is needed, and there are three ways to obtain one: from
  the apparent size of the code in the image, from the translation returned when
  the pose of its four corners is solved, or from the LiDAR.

  The first two ways both need the real size of the code. That size is known in
  our case, because the generator places on each face of a carton a square panel
  whose side $s$ measures 0.40 m. Using it, however, is not as simple as it
  appears. A QR code is always printed with an empty margin around it, and every
  decoder returns the corners of the code itself rather than the corners of the
  panel that carries it. If the two are confused, every distance computed from
  them is too large by about 19 %. The component therefore never works with the
  side of the panel but with the side of the code, written $c$:

  $$c = s \cdot \frac{21}{25} = 0.336 \ \text{m} \tag{3.4}$$

  The numbers in this ratio come from the format of the symbol itself. A
  version-1 QR code is a grid of twenty-one modules across, and the empty margin
  printed around it is two modules wide on each side, which makes twenty-five
  modules in total for the panel. The code alone therefore occupies twenty-one
  twenty-fifths of the panel, and it is this smaller square that a decoder
  outlines.

  When the four corners $Q$ of a code are available, solving for its pose gives
  two results at once, a rotation $R_q$ and a translation $\mathbf{t}_q$, and
  these two results are not equally reliable. The rotation depends only on the
  shape of the quadrilateral seen in the image, so an error on the assumed size
  would leave it unchanged. The translation, on the contrary, is directly
  proportional to that size. This is why the orientation obtained from the
  corners can be trusted whatever the panel format happens to be, while the
  distance obtained from the corners can only be trusted as far as the size
  assumption can.

  The design therefore uses the corners for the orientation and the LiDAR for
  the distance. A pixel $\mathbf{u}$ is turned into a ray in the world frame,
  and the position of the observation is obtained by travelling along that ray
  for the range read from the LiDAR:

  $$\rho(\mathbf{u}) = R_c \cdot \iota!\left(
  \frac{K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}}{\lVert K^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{u}} \rVert}
  \right), \qquad \mathbf{p} = \mathbf{p}_c + r,\rho(\mathbf{u}) \tag{3.5}$$

  The first expression should be read from the inside out. Writing the pixel in
  homogeneous coordinates as $\tilde{\mathbf{u}}$ and multiplying it by the
  inverse of the intrinsic matrix $K$ undoes the projection that produced the
  image, and gives the direction of that pixel in the frame of the camera.
  Dividing by its own length turns it into a unit vector, since only the
  direction carries information. The function $\iota$ then converts the axis
  convention of the camera into the one used by the world, and the rotation
  $R_c$ expresses the result in world coordinates. What comes out is the ray
  $\rho(\mathbf{u})$, which starts at the camera and passes through whatever the
  pixel was looking at. The second expression follows immediately: starting
  from the camera position $\mathbf{p}_c$ and advancing along that ray by the
  range $r$ gives the point in space.

  The reason for splitting the two sources this way is not precision, since they
  agree to within a centimetre. The reason is independence. If the position of
  a label depended on a printing format, the system would break on the day that
  format changed, and the warehouse these vehicles have to inventory is by
  definition one they have never seen. Keeping the distance free of that
  assumption costs nothing here, and it removes a hidden link between perception
  and the particular building it is working in.

  A label that has only been spotted is a harder case, because the detector
  returns a region of the image and not four corners. There is no orientation to
  recover, and the distance can only come from the LiDAR. Here the sensor
  itself creates a boundary. Its rays leave at a constant angular spacing
  $\Delta\theta$, which is fixed by the sensor and does not change with
  distance, so the gap between two neighbouring rays on a surface grows in
  proportion to the range, following $\delta(r) = r,\Delta\theta$. At eight
  metres, two neighbouring rays are already further apart than one cell of the
  map. Past that point, the ray closest to a given direction no longer touches
  the object that was detected; it touches the carton beside it, or it passes
  through a gap in the rack and reaches the wall behind. The component therefore
  works in two regimes. Within $r_{\max} = 8$ m the distance is the LiDAR
  measurement. Beyond that the distance is taken from the map instead, as the
  first cell along that direction which is already known to be occupied. This is
  the first place in the system where the map is used as a sensor rather than
  as a memory, and the choice is deliberate: a single revolution of the LiDAR is
  sparse at eight metres, but the map has collected many revolutions, from many
  positions and from several vehicles, so it is dense exactly where one
  revolution is not.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.5 — fig_regimes
  ▎ Two placement regimes. Inside the LiDAR's trusted range a ray lands on the 
  ▎ label; beyond it the rays are further apart than a map cell, and the range 
  ▎ is taken from the map instead.

  One last test protects this path, and it is the only place where the nominal
  size of the code is genuinely useful. If a region $B$ of width $w$ pixels
  contains a code of physical side $c$, then its distance is roughly $\hat{d} =
  f_x,c/w$, where $f_x$ is the focal length of the camera expressed in pixels.
  This value cannot be trusted precisely, because the region drawn around a code
  is wider than the code itself and may be cut by an obstacle, but it is
  correct in order of magnitude. Any placement that disagrees with it by more
  than a wide factor is rejected. The window is kept deliberately wide, since
  its only job is to catch one specific failure: a ray that has gone through a
  gap in a rack and returned a point far behind the label it was aimed at.

  Finally, a spotted label is recorded as facing the camera that saw it, which
  gives the direction of approach $\mathbf{a}$. This is the only piece of
  directional information that survives when the corners are missing, and it is
  enough for what follows, because it says from which side of the rack the label
  will later have to be approached.

  Algorithm 3.1 — PlaceObservation
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: K, (p_c, R_c), cloud C, map M, and either corners Q (decoded)
           or a region B (detected)
  Ensure:  (p, n) ∈ ℝ³ × ℝ³, or ⊥

   1:  if Q is given then                            ▷ decoded code
   2:      (R_q, t_q) ← PnP□(Q, c, K)                ▷ pose of a planar square
   3:      α ← ∠(−t_q , R_q e_z)                     ▷ incidence, e_z = panel
  normal
   4:      if α > α_max then return ⊥
   5:      u ← centroid(Q)
   6:  else
   7:      u ← centre(B)
   8:  end if
   9:  ρ ← R_c · ι( K⁻¹ũ / ‖K⁻¹ũ‖ )                  ▷ world ray, Eq. (3.5)
  10:  r ← RangeLidar(p_c, ρ, C)
  11:  if r = ⊥ or r > r_max then
  12:      if Q is given then return ⊥               ▷ a read code is always
  close
  13:      r ← RangeMap(p_c, ρ, M)                   ▷ first occupied cell
  14:  end if
  15:  if r = ⊥ then return ⊥
  16:  p ← p_c + r·ρ
  17:  if Q is given then
  18:      if r ∉ E then return ⊥                    ▷ reading envelope, Eq.
  (3.3)
  19:      n ← R_c · ι(R_q e_z), oriented towards p_c
  20:  else
  21:      d̂ ← f_x · c / w(B)                        ▷ coarse distance from the
  region
  22:      if ‖p − p_c‖ ∉ [κ_lo·d̂ , κ_hi·d̂] then return ⊥
  23:      n ← −Π_xy ρ / ‖Π_xy ρ‖                    ▷ approach direction,
  horizontal
  24:  end if
  25:  return (p, n)
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  3.4.1.3 Why Detection Must Be Learned

  Making the detection range long is not difficult in itself. A geometric
  detector, built from a threshold and a few shape tests, is cheap to run and
  works well beyond the reading envelope. It fails on the only criterion that
  really matters here. In a warehouse from which every code has been removed,
  such a detector still reports a panel on more than a quarter of the images,
  because it mistakes cartons, rack uprights and shadows for labels. Since every
  spotted label produces a flight, a detector with that rate would send
  vehicles to more empty places than real ones, and the system would finish the
  inventory faster with the detector switched off.

  The requirement placed on this element is therefore a pair of conditions
  rather than a single number, and it is the pair that decides how the element
  has to be built.

  Table 3.3 — The requirement placed on the detector.

  ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │                     │      Geometric      │          Required           │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Range held at 90 %  │ 4.0 m, where        │                             │
  │ detection           │ reading already     │ well beyond $\mathcal{E}$   │
  │                     │ stops               │                             │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Reports on a scene  │ 27 % of images      │ rare enough that a wasted   │
  │ holding no code     │                     │ flight is exceptional       │
  └─────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  No threshold applied to a geometric detector can satisfy both conditions at
  once. Raising the threshold does remove the false alarms, but it also removes
  the distant true detections, which are exactly what the element exists to
  provide. The two conditions can only be separated by a detector that has
  learned what a label looks like in this particular setting, instead of
  recognising what a dark quadrilateral looks like anywhere. This is the reason
  the detector is learned. The model itself, and the margin it achieves on both
  conditions, belong to Chapters 4 and 5.

  The training examples are produced without any manual annotation, and this is
  what makes the choice practical here. Since the position of every carton and
  every panel is known, each object can be projected into the image using the
  exact pose of the camera. Projection alone would not be enough, because an
  object hidden behind another would then be labelled as visible; visibility is
  therefore established physically, by casting rays towards points on each
  surface and accepting a point only when the first object the ray reaches is
  the one that owns it. The buildings used to produce the examples and the
  buildings used to measure the result are kept separate.

  The detector also reports a second class, cartons, which are never read. A
  detected carton tells the system that a region holds inventory, and this is
  what the semantic layer of the map needs in order to tell a full rack from an
  empty one before anything has been decoded there.

  3.4.1.4 The Perception Cycle

  The vehicle carries two lateral cameras instead of one forward camera, and the
  reason comes from the shape of the building. Labels sit on the faces of
  cartons on both sides of an aisle, so a vehicle flying down the middle has
  readable surfaces on its left and on its right, and nothing of interest
  straight ahead. With two lateral cameras, one pass along an aisle serves both
  racks. With a forward camera, the vehicle would have to turn towards each face
  in turn, and since the reading envelope is narrow, this would roughly double
  the number of positions the mission has to visit.

  Decoding an image and running the detector on it do not cost the same, and the
  two together are more than one cycle can afford. The rule adopted is
  therefore asymmetric: decoding runs on both images at every cycle, while
  detection runs on only one camera per cycle, alternating between them. The
  justification lies in what each missed opportunity costs. A label detected one
  cycle later is still detected, and the vehicle was not going there yet in any
  case. A label that is not decoded while the vehicle is standing in front of
  it, on the other hand, requires a second journey to the same place. Decoding
  is also applied to the whole image rather than to the regions proposed by the
  detector, which keeps the two paths independent of each other: a label the
  detector misses can still be read, so a weakness of the learned element
  reduces the range of the system without ever costing it an identifier.

  One correction remains, and it is a question of timing. The renderer delivers
  each frame one step behind the physics. If an image were paired with the pose
  the vehicle holds at the moment the image arrives, every observation would be
  attached to a position the vehicle has already left. The component therefore
  keeps the two most recent instants and always pairs the image of the current
  instant with the pose and the point cloud of the previous one. Without this
  correction, the same panel seen once while approaching and once while moving
  away is written into the map twice, in two different places, and the map ends
  up holding two labels where there is only one.

  Algorithm 3.2 — PerceptionCycle
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: vehicle i, cycle index k, map M, history H (length 2)
  Ensure:  M updated

   1:  (D, r) ← Lidar(i)
   2:  M.IntegrateOccupancy(p_i, D, r)
   3:  M.Announce(i, p_i)
   4:  push (poses of L and R, cloud C) onto H
   5:  if |H| < 2 then return                        ▷ one frame of render lag
   6:  (p_c, R_c, C) ← H[t−1]
   7:  for cam ∈ {L, R} do
   8:      I ← Image(cam)
   9:      for (ℓ, Q) ∈ Decode(I) do
  10:          if ℓ ∉ F then continue                ▷ identifier not in the
  inventory
  11:          o ← PlaceObservation(Q, K, p_c, R_c, C, M)
  12:          if o ≠ ⊥ then M.WriteRead(ℓ, o)
  13:      end for
  14:      if cam ≡ k (mod 2) then                   ▷ detection alternates
  15:          for (κ, B) ∈ Detect(I) do
  16:              o ← PlaceObservation(B, K, p_c, R_c, C, M)
  17:              if o = ⊥ then continue
  18:              if κ = qr then M.WriteSpotted(o)
  19:              else M.MarkSemantic(o.p)
  20:          end for
  21:      end if
  22:      M.IntegrateCoverage(p_c, R_c e_x)          ▷ e_x = camera axis
  23:  end for
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Four rejection rules appear across the two algorithms, and together they
  define what perception is willing to state.

  Table 3.4 — Rejection rules and the error each one prevents.

  Rule: Identifier format
  Condition: $\ell \notin \mathcal{F}$
  Error prevented: the warehouse carries QR codes on its own signage, and
  without
  this filter they would be entered into the inventory as cartons
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Limiting incidence
  Condition: $\alpha > \alpha_{\max}$
  Error prevented: beyond that angle the four corners no longer give a reliable
  normal, and a wrong normal would send a later vehicle to the wrong side of
  the rack
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Reading envelope
  Condition: $r \notin \mathcal{E}$
  Error prevented: a code decoded from an impossible distance is a
  corner-localisation error, not a reading
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: Plausibility of placement
  Condition: $\lVert \mathbf{p} - \mathbf{p}c \rVert \notin [\kappa{lo}\hat{d},\

  \kappa_{hi}\hat{d}]$
  Error prevented: a ray that has passed through a gap in a rack would otherwise

  place a target inside a wall

  Each of these rules turns a possible error into a missed observation, which is
  exactly the trade that the difference in cost described at the beginning of
  this section requires.

  ca et dis moi ce que tu as appris ## 3.4.1 Perception                         
                                                                                
  Perception is the part of the system that observes the warehouse and provides 
  the information used by all other components. At each cycle, for each         
  vehicle, it receives the images from the left and right cameras, their        
  corresponding poses, and one full LiDAR revolution. It produces three types   
  of information and writes them into the shared map $\mathcal{M}$: decoded     
  identifiers with their position and panel orientation, detected but undecoded 
  labels with their position and approach direction, and regions identified as  
  free or occupied.                                                             
                                                                                
  The perception function can therefore be written as                           
                                                                                
  $$                                                                            
  f_{\mathcal{P}} :                                                             
  \mathcal{I}^2 \times \mathcal{T} \times \mathcal{L}                           
  \longrightarrow                                                               
  \mathcal{R} \times \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{V}                             
  \tag{3.1}                                                                     
  $$                                                                            
                                                                                
  where $\mathcal{I}$ denotes the camera images, $\mathcal{T}$ the              
  corresponding camera poses, $\mathcal{L}$ the LiDAR measurements, and         
  $\mathcal{R}$, $\mathcal{S}$, and $\mathcal{V}$ the decoded labels, spotted   
  labels, and spatial information respectively.                                 
                                                                                
  These three types of information do not have the same cost when they are      
  wrong. A wrong identifier is permanent because the system does not return to  
  a label once it has been declared read. A false detection instead causes one  
  unnecessary flight, while a missed uncertain detection costs nothing because  
  the area will be observed again later. The perception component therefore     
  favours rejection over guessing: when an observation is uncertain, it remains 
  silent.                                                                       
                                                                                
  > **▸ FIGURE 3.2** — `fig_perception_chaine`                                  
  >                                                                             
  > *Inside the perception component. The image provides direction and          
  identity, while the LiDAR provides range; the resulting information is        
  written into the shared map.*                                                 
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.1.1 The Reading Envelope                                              
                                                                                
  A QR code becomes unreadable before it becomes invisible. Reading mainly      
  depends on two factors: distance and viewing angle. These factors can be      
  combined into a single quantity. If a square panel is

   7:  for cam ∈ {L, R} do

  Four rejection rules are used across the two algorithms. Together, they       
  define which observations are considered reliable enough to enter the map.    
                                                                                
  **Table 3.4 — Rejection rules and the errors they prevent.**                  
                                                                                
  | Rule                   | Condition                                          
                      | Error prevented                                         
                                 |                                              
  | ---------------------- |                                                    
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------- |      
  ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- 
  -------- |                                                                    
  | Identifier format      | $\ell \notin \mathcal{F}$                          
                      | prevents warehouse signage or foreign QR identifiers    
  from being entered as inventory  |                                            
  | Limiting incidence     | $\alpha > \alpha_{\max}$                           
                      | prevents unreliable orientation estimates from          
  producing a wrong approach side        |                                      
  | Reading envelope       | $r \notin \mathcal{E}$                             
                      | rejects a decoded code whose estimated position is      
  outside the valid reading range    |                                          
  | Placement plausibility | $|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{p}*c|\notin[\kappa*{lo}\hat   
  d,\kappa_{hi}\hat d]$ | prevents a LiDAR ray passing through a rack gap from  
  placing a target behind the rack |                                            
                                                                                
  Each rule converts a potentially harmful false observation into a missed      
  observation. This is intentional: in this system, missing an uncertain        
  observation is preferable to writing incorrect information into the shared    
  map.
