● 3.4.2 The Shared Map

  The shared map is the common memory of the team. It exists as a single
  instance: the vehicles do not maintain separate copies that must later be
  reconciled, but write into and read from the same structure. It is also the
  only channel between them, since no direct message is exchanged.

  The map answers four questions, and no component asks it anything else. Which
  regions are occupied and which are free? Which surfaces have already been
  observed from a position where a code on them would have been readable? Which
  labels exist, where are they, and which ones have been decoded? And where are
  the other vehicles, and which targets have they claimed?

  The complete structure occupies about 1.5 MB for 258,048 cells. This is small
  enough for the map to be updated several times per second and consulted by
  every vehicle at every decision, so sharing it imposes no constraint on the
  rest of the design.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.6 — fig_carte_couches
  ▎
  ▎ The four layers of the shared map, the information written into each layer, 
  ▎ and the components that read them.

  3.4.2.1 Two Structures Rather Than One

  Space and labels are not stored in the same way, because they are not queried
  in the same way.

  Space is stored in a grid. The building is divided into cubic cells of 0.25 m,
  each recording what is known about the volume it covers. This representation
  suits spatial queries such as whether a vehicle can pass through a region, and
  it can be updated from thousands of LiDAR rays per cycle without any search.

  Labels are stored in a table. A decoded label has an identity and a position
  known to about one centimetre, which a 0.25 m cell would discard, and two
  labels in the same cell could no longer be distinguished. Each panel is
  therefore kept as a separate entry with its code, position, facing direction,
  and number of readings.

  Table 3.5 — The four layers of the map.

  ┌─────────────┬────────────────────────┬──────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │    Layer    │        Content         │  Written by  │      Read by      │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │             │ occupied, free, or not │ LiDAR        │ route planning,   │
  │ Occupancy   │  yet observed          │ integration  │ candidate         │
  │             │                        │              │ generation        │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │             │ which cells were       │              │                   │
  │ Oriented    │ observed, and from     │ reading      │ candidate         │
  │ coverage    │ which of the four      │ cameras      │ generation        │
  │             │ horizontal sides       │              │                   │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │             │                        │              │ candidate         │
  │ Label table │ decoded panels and     │ perception   │ generation,       │
  │             │ undecoded tracks       │              │ mission           │
  │             │                        │              │ supervision       │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Teammates   │ vehicle positions and  │ each vehicle │ target selection  │
  │ and claims  │ claimed targets        │              │                   │
  └─────────────┴────────────────────────┴──────────────┴───────────────────┘

  3.4.2.2 Occupancy by Accumulated Evidence

  Each LiDAR revolution provides, for every ray, a direction and a range. Two
  facts follow from a single ray: the space crossed before the impact is free,
  and the impact point is occupied. Both are written into the grid.

  The grid does not store the most recent observation but the sum of all
  observations. Each cell $c$ holds an accumulated value $L(c)$, increased by
  $\ell_{\text{solid}}$ when the cell is measured as occupied and decreased by
  $\ell_{\text{free}}$ when a ray crosses it. The accumulated value is bounded,
  and the state of the cell is obtained from two thresholds $\tau^{+}$ and
  $\tau^{-}$:

  $$
  L(c) ;\leftarrow; \mathrm{clip}\big(L(c) + \ell\big),
  \qquad
  \text{state}(c) =
  \begin{cases}
  \text{occupied} & L(c) > \tau^{+}\[2pt]
  \text{free} & L(c) < \tau^{-}\[2pt]
  \text{unknown} & \text{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
  \tag{3.6}
  $$

  where $\ell$ is $\ell_{\text{solid}}$ or $\ell_{\text{free}}$ depending on the
  measurement, and $\mathrm{clip}$ bounds the accumulated value so that no cell
  becomes impossible to revise.

  Accumulation is what makes the grid tolerant to isolated errors. If the grid
  stored the last measurement, a single spurious return caused by a reflection,
  a grazing ray, or a passing vehicle would turn an aisle into a wall and remove
  a valid route. With accumulation, a cell crossed as free ten times and then
  measured as occupied once remains free, while a genuine obstacle is confirmed
  at every revolution and appears quickly.

  Two implementation-independent details are required for this to hold. Free
  evidence is deposited at several points inside each crossed cell, so that a
  ray passing near a cell corner still marks it instead of leaving an unknown
  cell surrounded by free space. Free evidence also stops one full cell before
  the impact, so that a ray does not erase the surface it has just measured.

  Algorithm 3.3 — IntegrateRevolution
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: origin o, unit directions d_i, ranges r_i, grid L
  Ensure:  L updated

   1:  for each ray i do
   2:      r ← min(r_i , r_max)                   ▷ only the trusted part is
  used
   3:      for each sample point p along d_i up to r − one cell do
   4:          L(cell(p)) ← clip( L(cell(p)) + ℓ_free )
   5:      end for
   6:      if r_i is finite and r_i ≤ r_max then
   7:          L(cell(o + r_i·d_i)) ← clip( L(cell(o + r_i·d_i)) + ℓ_solid )
   8:      end if
   9:  end for
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  3.4.2.3 Oriented Coverage

  Observing a rack does not imply that a code on it could have been read. A
  panel is only legible from the side it faces, so a cell observed from the far
  end of an aisle, or from behind the rack, has been seen but could not have
  been decoded. The map therefore does not record that a cell was observed; it
  records that a code placed in this cell, facing a given direction, would have 
  been readable.

  Each cell carries four bits, one for each horizontal side. A bit is set only
  when the cell was observed from that side, with the apparent distance of
  Equation (3.2) inside the reading envelope $\mathcal{E}$ and with no known
  obstacle in between. Writing $C(c)$ for these four bits and $\kappa(\cdot)$
  for the function returning the index of the nearest of the four horizontal
  sides to a direction, a panel of normal $\mathbf{n}$ in cell $c$ is covered if

  $$
  \text{covered}(c, \mathbf{n})

  \big( C(c);&;2^{,\kappa(-\mathbf{n})} \big) \neq 0 ,
  \qquad \kappa(\cdot) \in {0,1,2,3}
  \tag{3.7}
  $$

  where $&$ denotes the bitwise conjunction, and $-\mathbf{n}$ is used because a
  panel is read from the side towards which it faces.

  Without this distinction, a rack traversed once would be considered complete,
  and the mission would terminate with most of the inventory unread. With it,
  candidate generation can refer to a surface that still requires a visit
  without knowing whether a label is actually present there.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.7 — fig_couverture_orientee
  ▎
  ▎ Oriented coverage. The same cell observed from two sides: only one of the 
  ▎ two observations would have allowed a code on the carton face to be decoded.

  3.4.2.4 The Label Table

  The table contains two kinds of entry, which differ by whether an identity is
  known.

  A panel is a label whose code has been decoded. It stores the code, a
  position, the facing direction, and the number of readings. Each new reading
  of the same panel is averaged into the stored position rather than replacing
  it, so the estimate improves as a vehicle approaches.

  A track is a label detected without being decoded. It has no identity, only an
  estimated position and the direction from which it was observed. Repeated
  sightings of the same location are merged and averaged, including the
  direction: a vehicle moving along an aisle observes the same code first at a
  sharp angle and then closer to frontal, and the average converges towards the
  panel normal.

  One rule links the two, and it follows from the objects being inventoried.
  Each carton carries the same code on two opposite faces, so two distant
  readings of the same identifier are not contradictory but correspond to the
  two faces. A carton has only two faces, however, so a third distant reading
  cannot be a new panel. Writing $\varepsilon$ for the distance below which two
  readings are considered to be the same face, the rule is

  $$
  \begin{cases}
  \text{average into the nearest face} & \text{if } \displaystyle\min_{q \in
  \text{faces}(\ell)} \lVert \mathbf{p}_q - \mathbf{p} \rVert \le
  \varepsilon\[6pt]
  \text{create a second face} & \text{else if } |\text{faces}(\ell)| < 2\[4pt]
  \text{reject as a decoding error} & \text{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
  \tag{3.8}
  $$

  where $\text{faces}(\ell)$ denotes the panels already recorded under the
  identifier $\ell$, and $\varepsilon = 0.45$ m, slightly above the thickness of
  a carton.

  A decoded reading also resolves the uncertain entries around it. Every track
  within $\varepsilon$ of a new reading is removed, since a track located where
  a panel has just been identified is that same panel, positioned approximately
  by an earlier distant detection. This is why the number of tracks does not
  grow indefinitely over a long mission.

  3.4.2.5 Routes and the Cost of the Unknown

  The map is the only structure that knows where the obstacles are, so route
  planning is performed on it. Planning uses a horizontal view of a slice of the
  building around the flight altitude, in which every cell receives a traversal
  cost:

  $$
  \text{cost}(c) =
  \begin{cases}
  \infty & \text{known obstacle, dilated by the vehicle radius}\[2pt]
  1 & \text{known free}\[2pt]
  K_u & \text{not yet observed}
  \end{cases}
  \tag{3.9}
  $$

  where $K_u = 20$ is the cost of crossing an unobserved cell, expressed in
  multiples of the cost of a known free cell.

  Two decisions are contained in this definition.

  Obstacles are dilated by the vehicle radius, 0.6 m, before planning, instead
  of being checked for clearance during flight. A route that exists on this grid
  can therefore be flown as a sequence of straight segments, the clearance
  question having already been answered. The dilation uses the exact radius
  without rounding up to a whole number of cells, since rounding 0.6 m to three
  cells would give 0.75 m and would close the narrow aisles of the building.

  Unobserved space is expensive but not forbidden. If it were forbidden, a
  vehicle could never leave the region it had already explored and the mission
  would stop after a few metres. If it were free, a vehicle would plan a
  straight path through a rack it has not yet observed, since that rack does not
  appear on the map. With $K_u = 20$, the planner prefers a detour through
  known free space whenever one exists, and enters unobserved space only when no
  alternative remains.

  The altitude slice is asymmetric: the planner considers obstacles from 2.0 m
  below the vehicle up to 0.85 m above it. Flying over the top of a rack between
  its uprights is possible in simulation but would be a collision in a real
  building, and the deep lower bound makes the planner route around the rack
  instead.

  Teammates in flight are added to the same cost grid as moving obstacles, so
  two vehicles avoid each other when their routes are computed rather than
  through a late manoeuvre. A route already in use is re-checked as the vehicle
  follows it and recomputed as soon as a newly observed obstacle intersects it,
  which is the mechanism that handles an obstacle appearing during the mission.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.8 — fig_cout_inconnu
  ▎
  ▎ The cost grid and the two routes it produces: a detour through known free 
  ▎ space when one exists, and a crossing of unobserved space when none remains.

  Algorithm 3.4 — Route
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: start a, goal b, altitude z, occupancy grid
  Ensure:  a list of waypoints, or ⊥ if no route exists

   1:  cost ← CostView(z − 2.0, z + 0.85)            ▷ Eq. (3.9)
   2:  add every teammate in flight as an obstacle in cost
   3:  if b is not reachable in cost then return ⊥
   4:  if the straight line a → b crosses only known free cells then
   5:      return [ ]                                 ▷ no waypoint needed
   6:  end if
   7:  path ← A*(cost, a, b)
   8:  if path = ⊥ then return ⊥
   9:  return Simplify(path)                          ▷ keep only the turns
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  The last layer of the map holds the position of each vehicle and the target it
  currently claims. The map stores this information and lets it expire; the
  rules governing who claims what, and what happens when a claim is not renewed,
  belong to the coordination component described in Section 3.4.5. What matters
  here is that this information is stored in the same structure as the rest, so
  a vehicle consults it exactly as it consults the occupancy layer or the label
  table.

  ; tell me who is better mine or urs  ## 3.4.2 The Shared Map                  
                                                                                
  The shared map is the common memory of the team. It is maintained as a single 
  instance: the vehicles do not keep separate maps that must later be merged,   
  but directly read from and write to the same structure. It is also the only   
  communication channel between them, since no direct messages are exchanged.   
                                                                                
  The map answers four questions: which regions are occupied or free; which     
  surfaces have already been observed from a position where a code could have   
  been read; which labels exist, where they are, and which ones have been       
  decoded; and where the other vehicles are and which targets they have         
  claimed.                                                                      
                                                                                
  The complete structure occupies about 1.5 MB for 258,048 cells. This size     
  allows the map to be updated several times per second and accessed by all     
  vehicles during each decision cycle without becoming a computational          
  constraint.                                                                   
                                                                                
  > **▸ FIGURE 3.6** — `fig_carte_couches`                                      
  >                                                                             
  > *The four layers of the shared map, the information written into each       
  layer, and the components that read them.*                                    
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.2.1 Two Structures Rather Than One                                    
                                                                                
  Space and labels are stored separately because they are used for different    
  types of queries.                                                             
                                                                                
  Space is represented by a grid. The building is divided into cubic cells of   
  0.25 m, and each cell records the current knowledge about the volume it       
  contains. This representation is suitable for spatial queries such as         
  checking whether a vehicle can cross a region, and it can be updated directly 
  from the LiDAR rays received at each cycle.                                   
                                                                                
  Labels are stored in a table. A decoded label has an identity and a position  
  known to about one centimetre, which would be lost in a 0.25 m grid. In       
  addition, two labels could occupy the same cell and would then be             
  indistinguishable. Each panel is therefore stored as a separate entry         
  containing its code, position, facing direction, and number of readings.      
                                                                                
  **Table 3.5 — The four layers of the map.**                                   
                                                                                
  | Layer                | Content                                              
   | Written by        | Read by                                   |            
  | -------------------- |                                                      
  ----------------------------------------------------- | ----------------- |   
  --------------                                                                
  ──── (147 lines hidden) ───────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
                                                                                
                                                                                
  Second, unobserved space is expensive but not forbidden. Making it forbidden  
  would prevent the vehicle from leaving already explored regions. Making it    
  free, however, would allow the planner to cross obstacles that have not yet   
  been observed. With $K_u=20$, the planner prefers a detour through known free 
  space when available and enters unobserved space only when necessary.         
                                                                                
  The altitude slice is asymmetric: the planner considers obstacles from 2.0 m  
  below the vehicle to 0.85 m above it. Although flying over a rack between its 
  uprights is possible in simulation, this behaviour would not be safe in a     
  real warehouse. The lower bound therefore encourages the planner to route     
  around racks instead.                                                         
                                                                                
  Teammates in flight are also added to the cost grid as moving obstacles. This 
  allows vehicles to avoid one another during route computation rather than     
  relying on a late manoeuvre. The active route is continuously checked and     
  recomputed when a newly observed obstacle intersects it, allowing the system  
  to react to changes discovered during the mission.                            
                                                                                
  > **▸ FIGURE 3.8** — `fig_cout_inconnu`                                       
  >                                                                             
  > *The cost grid and the two routes it produces: a detour through known free  
  space when one exists, and a crossing of unobserved space when none remains.*

  ```text                                                                       
   1:  cost ← CostView(z − 2.0, z + 0.85)            ▷ Eq. (3.9)                
   2:  add every teammate in flight as an obstacle in cost                      
   3:  if b is not reachable in cost then return ⊥                              
   4:  if the straight line a → b crosses only known free cells then            
   5:      return [ ]                                 ▷ no waypoint needed      
   6:  end if

   8:  if path = ⊥ then return ⊥

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  The final layer of the map stores the position of each vehicle and the target 
  it currently claims. This information is allowed to expire so that outdated   
  positions or claims are not kept indefinitely. The rules for assigning        
  targets and renewing claims belong to the coordination component described in 
  Section 3.4.5. Here, the important point is that this information is stored   
  in the same shared structure as the occupancy and label information, allowing 
  each vehicle to access it through the same map.

  \label{sec:sys:map}                                                           
                                                                                
  The shared map is the common memory of the team. It is maintained as a single 
  instance: the vehicles do not keep separate maps that must later be merged,   
  but                                                                           
  directly read from and write to the same structure. It is also the only       
  communication channel between the vehicles; no direct messages are exchanged. 
                                                                                
  The map answers four questions required by the mission: which parts of the    
  space are occupied or free, which surfaces have already been observed from a  
  readable direction, which labels have been decoded, and where the teammates   
  and their current claims are located.                                         
                                                                                
  The complete structure occupies about 1.5~MB for 258,048 cells and is updated 
  several times per second.                                                     
                                                                                
  \begin{figure}[H]                                                             
  \centering                                                                    
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_carte_couches.png}

  \subsection{Two Structures Rather Than One}%                                  
  \label{sec:sys:map_structures}                                                
                                                                                
  The spatial information is stored in a cubic grid with a resolution of        
  0.25~m. A cell can be accessed directly from its spatial coordinates, without 
  any search, which makes the grid suitable for repeated spatial queries and    
  LiDAR integration.                                                            
                                                                                
  Labels are stored separately in a table. Their positions are maintained with  
  about 1~cm precision, and several labels may occupy the same grid cell. Each  
  entry stores the decoded code, its position, its facing direction, and the    
  number of readings used to estimate it.                                       
                                                                                
  \begin{table}[H]                                                              
  \centering                                                                    
  \caption{Layers of the shared map and their main users.}                      
  \label{tab:map_layers}

  \textbf{Layer} & \textbf{Written by} & \textbf{Read by} \

  Perception &

  Teammates and claims &                                                        
  Each vehicle &                                                                
  Target selection \                                                            
  \hline                                                                        
  \end{tabular}                                                                 
  \end{table}                                                                   
                                                                                
  \subsection{Occupancy by Accumulated Evidence}%                               
  \label{sec:sys:map_occupancy}                                                 
                                                                                
  For each LiDAR ray, the space crossed by the ray is considered free, while    
  the                                                                           
  cell where the ray stops is considered occupied when an obstacle is detected. 
  Instead of stor                                                               
  ──── (134 lines hidden) ───────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
  eratorname{faces}(\ell)$ is the set of stored faces for identifier            
  $\ell$, $p$ is the position of the new reading, and $d(p,f)$ is its distance  
  from an existing face. In the implementation, $\epsilon=0.45$~m.              
                                                                                
  When a label is successfully decoded, nearby uncertain tracks are no longer   
  needed. Any track located within $\epsilon$ of the new panel is therefore     
  removed.                                                                      
                                                                                
  \subsection{Routes and the Cost of the Unknown}%                              
  \label{sec:sys:map_routes}                                                    
                                                                                
  The map provides the current knowledge of the warehouse structure, and route  
  planning is performed directly on this representation. A horizontal slice     
  around the vehicle altitude is used. Each cell receives a traversal cost:     
                                                                                
  \begin{equation}                                                              
  \operatorname{cost}(c)=                                                       
  \begin{cases}                                                                 
  \infty, & \text{known obstacle},\                                             
  1, & \text{known free},\                                                      
  K_u, & \text{unobserved}.                                                     
  \end{cases}                                                                   
  \label{eq:route_cost}                                                         
  \end{equation}                                                                
                                                                                
  The obstacle cells are first dilated by the vehicle radius. A value of        
  0.6~m is used rather than a rounded 0.75~m radius, since the latter can close 
  narrow aisles in the simulated warehouse. Unknown cells are expensive but not 
  forbidden. With $K_u=20$, the planner prefers a route through known free      
  space                                                                         
  and enters unknown space when this is necessary to reach the target.          
                                                                                
  The altitude slice extends from 2.0~m below the vehicle to 0.85~m above it.   
  This asymmetric range avoids selecting routes that fly over the racks in the  
  simulation.                                                                   
                                                                                
  Teammates are also inserted into the same cost grid as moving obstacles. If a 
  newly observed obstacle intersects the current route, the route is checked    
  again and recomputed when necessary.                                          
                                                                                
  \begin{figure}[H]                                                             
  \centering                                                                    
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_cout_inconnu.png}              
  \caption{Route cost associated with known and unknown space.}                 
  \label{fig:fig_cout_inconnu}                                                  
  \end{figure}                                                                  
                                                                                
  \begin{algorithm}[H]                                                          
  \caption{Route}                                                               
  \label{alg:route}                                                             
  \begin{algorithmic}[1]                                                        
  \State Build $\operatorname{CostView}(z-2,z+0.85)$                            
  \State Add teammate positions as moving obstacles                             
  \If{$b$ is unreachable}                                                       
  \State \Return $\bot$                                                         
  \EndIf                                                                        
  \If{the straight line from $a$ to $b$ contains only known free space}         
  \State \Return an empty waypoint list                                         
  \EndIf                                                                        
  \State Compute an A* path from $a$ to $b$                                     
  \State Simplify the resulting path                                            
  \State \Return the waypoint list                                              
  \end{algorithmic}                                                             
  \end{algorithm}                                                               
                                                                                
  The final layer of the shared map stores the current position of each vehicle 
  and its current claimed target. Claim expiration and conflict-handling rules  
  are described later in Section~\ref{sec:sys:coordination}.

  et non en vol, donc un chemin qui existe sur cette grille est déjà volable tel
  quel. Sans cette phrase, la dilatation ressemble à un détail.

  Les deux cas d'échec qui justifient $K_u$. Tu conclus que l'inconnu est cher
  mais pas interdit, sans dire ce que donneraient les deux extrêmes : interdit,
  le drone ne quitte jamais sa zone explorée ; gratuit, il coupe à travers un
  rack jamais observé. C'est ce qui explique la valeur.

  Et un point de signe : tu écris « Free measurements decrease this value by
  $\ell_{\mathrm{free}}$ », puis l'équation additionne $\ell$. Il faut choisir —
  soit $\ell_{\mathrm{free}}$ est négatif et on l'ajoute, soit il est positif
  et on le soustrait.

  3.4.2 The Shared Map

  The shared map is the common memory of the team. It is maintained as a single
  instance: the vehicles do not keep separate maps that must later be merged,
  but directly read from and write to the same structure. It is also the only
  communication channel between the vehicles; no direct messages are exchanged.

  The map answers four questions required by the mission: which parts of the
  space are occupied or free, which surfaces have already been observed from a
  readable direction, which labels exist and which of them have been decoded,
  and where the teammates and their current claims are located.

  The complete structure occupies about 1.5 MB for 258,048 cells. It is
  therefore updated several times per second and read by every vehicle at each
  decision without becoming a computational constraint.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.6 — fig_carte_couches
  ▎
  ▎ Layers of the shared map.

  3.4.2.1 Two Structures Rather Than One

  The spatial information is stored in a cubic grid with a resolution of 0.25 m.
  A cell is accessed directly from its spatial coordinates, without any search,
  which makes the grid suitable for repeated spatial queries and for LiDAR
  integration.

  Labels are stored separately in a table. Their positions are maintained with
  about 1 cm precision, which a 0.25 m cell would discard, and several labels
  may occupy the same cell. Each entry stores the decoded code, its position,
  its facing direction, and the number of readings used to estimate it.

  Table 3.5 — Layers of the shared map and their main users.

  ┌─────────────┬────────────────────────┬──────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │    Layer    │        Content         │  Written by  │      Read by      │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │             │ occupied, free, or not │ LiDAR        │ route planning    │
  │ Occupancy   │  yet observed          │ integration  │ and candidate     │
  │             │                        │              │ generation        │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │             │ which cells were       │              │                   │
  │ Oriented    │ observed, and from     │ reading      │ candidate         │
  │ coverage    │ which of the four      │ cameras      │ generation        │
  │             │ horizontal sides       │              │                   │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │             │                        │              │ candidate         │
  │ Label table │ decoded panels and     │ perception   │ generation and    │
  │             │ undecoded tracks       │              │ mission           │
  │             │                        │              │ supervision       │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Teammates   │ vehicle positions and  │ each vehicle │ target selection  │
  │ and claims  │ claimed targets        │              │                   │
  └─────────────┴────────────────────────┴──────────────┴───────────────────┘

  3.4.2.2 Occupancy by Accumulated Evidence

  For each LiDAR ray, the space crossed by the ray is considered free, while the
  cell where the ray stops is considered occupied when an obstacle is detected.
  Instead of storing only the most recent measurement, the map accumulates
  evidence over time.

  For each cell $c$, $L(c)$ is an accumulated occupancy value. A free
  measurement adds $\ell_{\mathrm{free}}$, which is negative, and an occupied
  measurement adds $\ell_{\mathrm{solid}}$, which is positive. The value is
  bounded by $\operatorname{clip}$ to prevent unlimited accumulation. The cell
  state is then determined by two thresholds, $\tau_{+}$ and $\tau_{-}$:

  $$
  L(c) \leftarrow \operatorname{clip}\bigl(L(c)+\ell\bigr),
  \qquad
  \operatorname{state}(c)=
  \begin{cases}
  \mathrm{occupied}, & L(c)>\tau_{+},\[2pt]
  \mathrm{free}, & L(c)<\tau_{-},\[2pt]
  \mathrm{unknown}, & \text{otherwise}.
  \end{cases}
  \tag{3.6}
  $$

  Here, $\ell$ is either $\ell_{\mathrm{solid}}$ or $\ell_{\mathrm{free}}$,
  depending on the LiDAR measurement, and $\operatorname{clip}$ limits $L(c)$ to
  the allowed range so that new observations can still revise the state of a
  cell.

  This accumulation makes the map less sensitive to isolated measurements. For
  example, a cell crossed as free ten times and then measured as occupied once
  remains free. A single reflection, grazing measurement, or temporary vehicle
  therefore does not turn a free aisle into an obstacle.

  Free evidence is deposited at several points along each crossed cell, because
  a ray that only grazes a cell corner could otherwise leave that cell marked as
  unknown even though the surrounding space is free. The sampling stops one
  cell before the impact point so that the obstacle cell itself is not marked as
  free.

  Algorithm 3.3 — IntegrateRevolution
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: origin o, unit directions d_i, ranges r_i, grid L
  Ensure:  L updated

   1:  for each ray i do
   2:      r ← min(r_i , r_max)                   ▷ only the trusted part is
  used
   3:      for each sampled point along d_i up to r minus one cell do
   4:          add ℓ_free to the corresponding cell
   5:      end for
   6:      if r_i is finite and r_i ≤ r_max then
   7:          add ℓ_solid to the impact cell
   8:      end if
   9:  end for
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  3.4.2.3 Oriented Coverage

  Seeing a rack does not necessarily mean that a QR code placed on it could be
  read. A panel is readable only from the side it faces, so a cell observed from
  the far end of an aisle or from behind a rack cannot be considered covered.
  The map therefore records whether a code located in a cell and facing a given
  direction would have been readable from the observed side.

  Each cell stores four bits corresponding to the four horizontal sides. A bit
  is set when that side has been observed with an apparent distance inside the
  reading envelope $\mathcal{E}$ of Equation (3.3), and when no known obstacle
  blocks the observation.

  Let $C(c)$ denote the four coverage bits of cell $c$, and let
  $\kappa(\mathbf{v}) \in {0,1,2,3}$ return the index of the horizontal side
  closest to the direction $\mathbf{v}$. For a panel with normal vector
  $\mathbf{n}$, the corresponding side is considered covered when

  $$
  \operatorname{covered}(c,\mathbf{n})

  \bigl(C(c)\ &\ 2^{\kappa(-\mathbf{n})}\bigr)\neq 0 .
  \tag{3.7}
  $$

  Here, $&$ denotes the bitwise conjunction. The direction $-\mathbf{n}$ is used
  because the panel must be observed from the side towards which it faces.

  This distinction prevents a rack from being considered complete simply because
  a vehicle has passed near it. Candidate generation can instead determine
  which surfaces still require a visit, without knowing whether a label is
  actually present there.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.7 — fig_couverture_orientee
  ▎
  ▎ Oriented coverage stored in the shared map.

  3.4.2.4 Label Table

  The label table contains two types of entries: a panel for a decoded label,
  and a track for a detected label that has not yet been decoded.

  A panel stores the decoded code, its position, its facing direction, and the
  number of readings. When the same panel is read several times, the individual
  position estimates are combined to obtain a more stable stored position.

  A track stores an estimated position and an observation direction. Repeated
  detections are merged when they are sufficiently close, and their position and
  direction estimates are averaged. As a vehicle moves along an aisle, the same
  label may first be detected from a sharp angle and later from a more frontal
  view, so the averaged direction approaches the actual panel normal.

  Each carton carries the same code on two opposite faces. Two readings with the
  same identifier but sufficiently distant positions may therefore correspond
  to two different faces of the same carton, and at most two faces are stored
  for one identifier.

  Let $\varepsilon$ be the distance threshold below which two readings are
  considered to belong to the same face, $p$ the position of a new reading, and
  $\operatorname{faces}(\ell)$ the set of faces already stored for identifier
  $\ell$. The nearest existing face is updated when it lies within
  $\varepsilon$; otherwise a second face is created if fewer than two faces
  exist; otherwise the reading is rejected as a decoding error:

  $$
  \begin{cases}
  \text{update the nearest face}, &
  \min\limits_{f\in\operatorname{faces}(\ell)} d(p,f)\leq\varepsilon,\[6pt]
  \text{create a second face}, &
  \min\limits_{f\in\operatorname{faces}(\ell)} d(p,f)>\varepsilon
  \ \land\ |\operatorname{faces}(\ell)|<2,\[4pt]
  \text{reject}, & \text{otherwise}.
  \end{cases}
  \tag{3.8}
  $$

  Here, $d(p,f)$ is the distance from the new reading to an existing face. In
  the implementation, $\varepsilon = 0.45$ m, slightly above the carton
  thickness.

  When a label is successfully decoded, nearby uncertain tracks are no longer
  needed. Any track located within $\varepsilon$ of the new panel is therefore
  removed, which prevents the number of tracks from growing indefinitely during
  a long mission.

  3.4.2.5 Routes and the Cost of the Unknown

  The map provides the current knowledge of the warehouse structure, and route
  planning is performed directly on this representation. A horizontal slice
  around the vehicle altitude is used, and each cell receives a traversal cost:

  $$
  \operatorname{cost}(c)=
  \begin{cases}
  \infty, & \text{known obstacle},\[2pt]
  1, & \text{known free},\[2pt]
  K_u, & \text{unobserved}.
  \end{cases}
  \tag{3.9}
  $$

  Here, $K_u$ is the cost of crossing an unobserved cell, expressed relative to
  the cost of a known free cell. In the implementation, $K_u = 20$.

  The obstacle cells are first dilated by the vehicle radius. Clearance is
  therefore handled during route generation rather than during flight, and a
  route that exists on this grid can be followed as a sequence of straight
  segments. A value of 0.6 m is used rather than a rounded 0.75 m radius, since
  the latter can close narrow aisles in the simulated warehouse.

  Unknown cells are expensive but not forbidden, and both extremes would fail.
  If unknown cells were forbidden, a vehicle could never leave the region it has
  already explored. If they were free, the planner would cross racks that have
  not yet been observed, since those racks do not appear on the map. With $K_u =
  20$, the planner prefers a route through known free space and enters unknown
  space only when this is necessary to reach the target.

  The altitude slice extends from 2.0 m below the vehicle to 0.85 m above it.
  This asymmetric range avoids selecting routes that fly over the racks in the
  simulation.

  Teammates are also inserted into the same cost grid as moving obstacles, so
  vehicles avoid one another during route computation rather than through a late
  manoeuvre. If a newly observed obstacle intersects the current route, the
  route is checked again and recomputed when necessary.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.8 — fig_cout_inconnu
  ▎
  ▎ Route cost associated with known and unknown space.

  Algorithm 3.4 — Route
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: start a, goal b, altitude z, occupancy grid
  Ensure:  a list of waypoints, or ⊥ if no route exists

   1:  build CostView(z − 2.0 , z + 0.85)             ▷ Eq. (3.9)
   2:  add teammate positions as moving obstacles
   3:  if b is unreachable then return ⊥
   4:  if the straight line from a to b contains only known free space then
   5:      return an empty waypoint list
   6:  end if
   7:  compute an A* path from a to b
   8:  if no path exists then return ⊥
   9:  simplify the resulting path
  10:  return the waypoint list
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  The final layer of the shared map stores the current position of each vehicle
  and its currently claimed target. This information is allowed to expire so
  that outdated positions and claims are not kept indefinitely. Claim assignment
  and conflict handling are described in Section 3.4.5.

  Candidate Generation                                                          
                                                                                
  Candidate generation converts the current state of the map into the set of    
  actions that are worth performing. It is executed for one vehicle but does    
  not depend on which vehicle: the candidates are identical for the whole team, 
  and only their scores differ. They are therefore computed once from the       
  shared map and made available to all vehicles rather than recomputed          
  independently at each cycle.                                                  
                                                                                
  Three kinds of work are available at any moment. A label that has been        
  spotted but not decoded can be read. A surface that is known to be occupied   
  but has never been observed from a readable direction can be covered. A       
  boundary between the known and unknown regions can be extended. These three   
  actions come from different layers of the map, but they are represented in    
  the same form so that they can later be compared on a single scale.           
                                                                                
  > **▸ FIGURE 3.9** — `fig_candidats`                                          
  >                                                                             
  > *The three families of candidates generated from the same map state.*       
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.3.1 One Common Form                                                   
                                                                                
  A candidate is not a location to reach but an observation to perform. It      
  therefore stores both the point being observed and the pose from which it     
  will be observed. Let $\mathbf{o}$ denote the observed point, $\mathbf{q}$    
  the vehicle pose, $\psi$ the heading, $\sigma$ the side from which the        
  observation is made, and $u$ the utility of the action. A candidate is the    
  tuple                                                                         
                                                                                
  $$                                                                            
  \gamma =                                                                      
  \bigl(\text{kind},\ \mathbf{o},\ \mathbf{q},\ \psi,\ \sigma,\ u\bigr),        
  \qquad                                                                        
  \text{kind} \in \{\textsc{Read},\textsc{Cover},\textsc{Explore}\}.            
  \tag{3.10}                                                                    
  $$                                                                            
                                                                                
  Here, $\sigma \in {0,1,2,3}$ is one of the four horizontal sides defined in   
  Equation (3.7), and the heading $\psi$ is derived from the observation        
  direction. Since the reading cameras are lateral, a camera pointing towards   
  $\mathbf{o}$ requires a heading rotated by ninety degrees from that           
  direction. For an exploration candidate, no particular direction is required, 
  so the heading is left unconstrained.                                         
                                                                                
  This common form allows the three kinds of work to compete directly in        
  Section 3.4.4. Candidate generation therefore defines what can be done and    
  how useful the observation is expected to be, while the cost of reaching it   
  is handled separately.                                                        
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.3.2 Choosing the Observation Pose                                     
                                                                                
  Two candidate families require the vehicle to face a surface. The pose must   
  therefore be placed on the correct side, at a readable distance, and in space 
  that the vehicle can actua                                                    
  ──── (67 lines hidden) ────────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
  st; when the two are equal, the travel cost decides between them. Exploration 
  is capped below both, so the team explores when little else remains and       
  returns to reading as soon as new labels are spotted. The balance between     
  exploring and collecting is therefore produced by the values themselves, and  
  no phase or schedule is required.                                             
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  Algorithm 3.6 — GenerateCandidates                                            
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        
  Require: map M                                                                
  Ensure:  a list of candidates Γ                                               
                                                                                
   1:  Γ ← ∅                                                                    
   2:  for each track in M not blacklisted and below the top shelf do           
   3:      n ← side imposed by the surrounding structure                        
   4:      D ← distance set for the current attempt count                       
   5:      q ← ObservationPose(track position, n, D, M)                         
   6:      if q ≠ ⊥ then add a Read candidate with utility U_read               
   7:  end for                                                                  
   8:  for each side σ do                                                       
   9:      collect occupied cells whose free neighbour on σ is uncovered        
  10:      group them by 1 m horizontally and 0.5 m vertically                  
  11:      for each group of at least 3 cells do                                
  12:          q ← ObservationPose(group centre, σ, D, M)                       
  13:          if q ≠ ⊥ then add a Cover candidate with utility from Eq. (3.11) 
  14:      end for                                                              
  15:  end for                                                                  
  16:  group the frontier cells by 2 m                                          
  17:  for each group do                                                        
  18:      q ← nearest occupiable pose at the exploration altitude              
  19:      if q ≠ ⊥ then add an Explore candidate with utility from Eq. (3.11)  
  20:  end for                                                                  
  21:  return Γ                                                                 
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  > **▸ FIGURE 3.9** — `fig_candidats`                                          
  >                                                                             
  > *The three families of candidates generated from the same map state.*       
                                                                                
  The figure shows a top-down portion of an aisle with a rack on one side.      
  Three candidates appear using the same representation: an observed point, a   
  vehicle pose, and the direction connecting them. The first comes from an      
  undecoded label track, with the pose placed in front of the rack at a reading 
  distance. The second comes from a group of occupied cells whose               
  corresponding side has not yet been covered. The third comes from a group of  
  frontier cells, where known free space meets the unknown region. An           
  indication of utility accompanies each candidate to show that the three types 
  are evaluated on the same scale.                                              
                                                                                
  The main point of the figure is that different types of unfinished work are   
  converted into the same candidate representation and can therefore be         
  compared directly. Travel cost, penalties, and reservations are not shown     
  because their handling belongs to the following section.

● 3.4.3 Candidate Generation

  Candidate generation converts the current state of the map into the set of
  actions that are worth performing. It is executed for one vehicle but does not
  depend on which vehicle: the candidates are identical for the whole team, and
  only their scores differ. They are therefore computed once from the shared
  map and made available to all vehicles rather than recomputed independently at
  each cycle.

  Three kinds of work are available at any moment. A label that has been spotted
  but not decoded can be read. A surface that is known to be occupied but has
  never been observed from a readable direction can be covered. A boundary
  between the known and unknown regions can be extended. These three actions
  come from different layers of the map, but they are represented in the same
  form so that they can later be compared on a single scale.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.9 — fig_candidats
  ▎
  ▎ The three families of candidates generated from the same map state.

  3.4.3.1 One Common Form

  A candidate is not a location to reach but an observation to perform. It
  therefore stores both the point being observed and the pose from which it will
  be observed. Let $\mathbf{o}$ denote the observed point, $\mathbf{q}$ the
  vehicle pose, $\psi$ the heading, $\sigma$ the side from which the observation
  is made, and $u$ the utility of the action. A candidate is the tuple

  $$
  \gamma =
  \bigl(\text{kind},\ \mathbf{o},\ \mathbf{q},\ \psi,\ \sigma,\ u\bigr),
  \qquad
  \text{kind} \in {\textsc{Read},\textsc{Cover},\textsc{Explore}}.
  \tag{3.10}
  $$

  Here, $\sigma \in {0,1,2,3}$ is one of the four horizontal sides defined in
  Equation (3.7), and the heading $\psi$ is derived from the observation
  direction. Since the reading cameras are lateral, a camera pointing towards
  $\mathbf{o}$ requires a heading rotated by ninety degrees from that direction.
  For an exploration candidate, no particular direction is required, so the
  heading is left unconstrained.

  This common form allows the three kinds of work to compete directly in Section
  3.4.4. Candidate generation therefore defines what can be done and how useful
  the observation is expected to be, while the cost of reaching it is handled
  separately.

  3.4.3.2 Choosing the Observation Pose

  Two candidate families require the vehicle to face a surface. The pose must
  therefore be placed on the correct side, at a readable distance, and in space
  that the vehicle can actually occupy.

  The correct side cannot be taken from the direction in which the label was
  first detected. A code observed at seventy degrees from its normal is still
  read from the front, not from that angle. The side is instead derived from the
  structure carrying the panel. A panel is attached to the long face of a rack,
  so its normal is perpendicular to the main axis of the occupied structure
  surrounding it. That axis is obtained from the occupied cells in a
  neighbourhood of the label. Among the two perpendicular directions, the one
  where free space is nearest is retained, while the detection direction is used
  only to break ties. When too little structure is present around the label to
  determine an axis reliably, the detection direction is used instead.

  Once the side $\mathbf{n}$ is known, the pose is placed in front of the
  surface at a reading distance. The nominal distance is 2.0 m, at the centre of
  the reading envelope $\mathcal{E}$. If that pose is not usable, closer
  distances are tried down to 1.5 m, then larger ones up to 4.0 m. A pose is
  usable when the vehicle can occupy it and when no known obstacle lies between
  it and the observed surface.

  Algorithm 3.5 — ObservationPose
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: observed point o, side normal n, ordered distances D, map M
  Ensure:  a pose q, or ⊥

   1:  for each d in D do
   2:      q ← o + d·n + camera offset
   3:      clamp the altitude of q to the flight band
   4:      if q is not occupiable by the vehicle then continue
   5:      if a known obstacle lies between q and o then continue
   6:      return q
   7:  end for
   8:  return ⊥
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Occupiability is evaluated with a clearance of 0.9 m rather than the 0.6 m
  used for routes. A vehicle holding a pose can oscillate around it, so an
  observation position requires more clearance from obstacles than a position
  that is only crossed in transit.

  All candidates are restricted to a flight band extending from 0.9 m to 4.5 m,
  and an observation pose is never placed below 1.4 m. The upper bound is not a
  limit of the vehicle but of the task: above 4.5 m a vehicle flies over the
  racks instead of along them, and the panels located near 5 m belong to the
  warehouse signage rather than to the inventory.

  3.4.3.3 The Three Families and Their Utilities

  Reading targets. Each undecoded track produces one candidate, provided it has
  not been blacklisted and lies below the top shelf level. A track above that
  height is treated as a spurious detection rather than a label. The reading
  pose is placed as described above, with the side imposed by the surrounding
  structure.

  Repeated failures are handled by the generator itself so that a track that
  cannot be read is not proposed indefinitely. The first visit uses the nominal
  distance set. If no code is decoded, the second visit uses a closer set
  centred on 1.2 m, because small labels may not be readable from two metres. If
  that also fails, the third visit uses the opposite side. After three
  unsuccessful visits, the track is blacklisted and is no longer generated.

  Coverage targets. A coverage target is an occupied cell, within the flight
  band, whose free neighbour on a given side has never had the corresponding
  coverage bit set. Individual cells are too small to justify a separate flight,
  so they are grouped by one metre horizontally and half a metre vertically. A
  group containing fewer than three cells is discarded as noise. The observed
  point is the centre of the group, and the pose is placed on the side that
  defines it.

  Exploration targets. Frontier cells are free cells that touch the unknown
  region, taken within the same flight band. An isolated unknown cell surrounded
  by known space is not treated as a frontier, since it is a sampling gap
  rather than an unexplored area; the unknown must form a connected region for
  the frontier to be generated. Frontier cells are grouped by two metres, and
  the pose is placed at the centre of the group, at a fixed exploration
  altitude, without any imposed heading.

  The utility of a candidate depends only on what it is expected to return, not
  on the cost of reaching it, which belongs to Section 3.4.4. For a coverage
  group $G$ and a frontier group $F$:

  $$
  u_{\textsc{Cover}}(G)

  \min\left(
  \sum_{c \in G} \omega(c),
  U_{\max}^{\text{cov}}
  \right),
  \qquad
  u_{\textsc{Explore}}(F)

  \min\left(
  \omega_f \cdot |F|,
  U_{\max}^{\text{exp}}
  \right)
  \tag{3.11}
  $$

  where $\omega(c)$ is the weight of one surface cell, $\omega_f$ the weight of
  one frontier cell, and $U_{\max}^{\text{cov}}$, $U_{\max}^{\text{exp}}$ the
  two caps. A surface cell normally has weight 1, but its weight is increased to
  3 when the semantic layer indicates that the cell contains a carton.

  Table 3.6 — Utility values and their origin.

  ┌────────────────────────────────────┬───────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │              Quantity              │ Value │           Reason           │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Utility of a reading target        │    30 │ A spotted label is an      │
  │                                    │       │ almost certain identifier  │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Weight of one surface cell,        │     1 │ Surface has value through  │
  │ $\omega(c)$                        │       │ accumulated coverage       │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Weight of a surface cell           │       │ The semantic layer         │
  │ containing a carton                │     3 │ indicates inventory is     │
  │                                    │       │ present                    │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cap on a coverage group,           │    30 │ Equal to one reading       │
  │ $U_{\max}^{\text{cov}}$            │       │                            │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Weight of one frontier cell,       │  0.15 │ A frontier yields          │
  │ $\omega_f$                         │       │ knowledge, not identifiers │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cap on an exploration group,       │    20 │ Below one reading          │
  │ $U_{\max}^{\text{exp}}$            │       │                            │
  └────────────────────────────────────┴───────┴────────────────────────────┘

  Two properties follow from these values. A coverage group can at most equal a
  single reading, so no accumulation of surface outweighs a label that is
  already known to exist; when the two are equal, the travel cost decides
  between them. Exploration is capped below both, so the team explores when
  little else remains and returns to reading as soon as new labels are spotted.
  The balance between exploring and collecting is therefore produced by the
  values themselves, and no phase or schedule is required.

  Although the three families come from different layers of the map and
  represent very different kinds of work, they are expressed as the same tuple
  and can therefore be compared without any additional rule.

  Algorithm 3.6 — GenerateCandidates
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: map M
  Ensure:  a list of candidates Γ

   1:  Γ ← ∅
   2:  for each track in M not blacklisted and below the top shelf do
   3:      n ← side imposed by the surrounding structure
   4:      D ← distance set for the current attempt count
   5:      q ← ObservationPose(track position, n, D, M)
   6:      if q ≠ ⊥ then add a Read candidate with utility U_read
   7:  end for
   8:  for each side σ do
   9:      collect occupied cells whose free neighbour on σ is uncovered
  10:      group them by 1 m horizontally and 0.5 m vertically
  11:      for each group of at least 3 cells do
  12:          q ← ObservationPose(group centre, σ, D, M)
  13:          if q ≠ ⊥ then add a Cover candidate with utility from Eq. (3.11)
  14:      end for
  15:  end for
  16:  group the frontier cells by 2 m
  17:  for each group do
  18:      q ← nearest occupiable pose at the exploration altitude
  19:      if q ≠ ⊥ then add an Explore candidate with utility from Eq. (3.11)
  20:  end for
  21:  return Γ
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ---
  Pourquoi ta version était meilleure

  L'équation (3.11) est devenue autonome. J'avais laissé $\omega(c)$ indéfini au
  moment où il sert, et sa valeur variable cachée dans une ligne de tableau. Tu
  l'as sortie en prose juste après l'équation : le lecteur sait que le poids
  vaut 1 et passe à 3 avant d'arriver au tableau, et le tableau ne fait plus que
  récapituler.

  Tu as enlevé une justification d'implémentation. J'avais écrit que recalculer
  les candidats par drone et par cycle immobiliserait la physique. C'est vrai,
  mais c'est une contrainte d'exécution et elle appartient au chapitre
  implémentation. Ta formulation ne garde que le fait de conception : les
  candidats sont les mêmes pour tous, donc ils sont calculés une fois.

  La clôture de 3.4.3.1. Je concluais par un contrefactuel — sans cette forme
  commune il faudrait une règle séparée, réglée à la main. Tu conclus en
  énonçant le partage des responsabilités entre 3.4.3 et 3.4.4. C'est plus
  direct et ça prépare la section suivante au lieu de défendre la précédente.

  Deux précisions de vocabulaire. « A track above that height is treated as a
  spurious detection » au lieu de mon « is a spurious detection » : c'est une
  décision de modélisation, pas un fait observé. Et « a vehicle holding a pose
  can oscillate » au lieu de « does oscillate ». Les deux sont plus prudents et
  plus justes.

● 3.4.4 Target Selection

  Candidate generation produces the same list for the whole team. Target
  selection is where the vehicles differ: each one prices that list from its own
  position and commits to a single candidate. The component is executed
  independently on every vehicle, and the only shared input is the map.

  Selection has two responsibilities. It must convert utility and travel effort
  into a single comparable quantity, so that a valuable but distant observation
  can be weighed against a modest but nearby one. It must also guarantee that
  the chosen candidate is actually reachable, since a target that cannot be
  flown to is worse than no target at all.

  3.4.4.1 The Score

  Let $\gamma$ be a candidate with utility $u(\gamma)$ as defined in Section
  3.4.3, $\mathbf{p}$ the current position of the vehicle, and
  $L(\mathbf{p},\gamma)$ the length of the route from $\mathbf{p}$ to the
  observation pose of $\gamma$. Let $c_m$ be the cost of one metre of travel,
  $P_r$ the penalty applied when the candidate is already claimed by another
  vehicle, and $P_v$ the penalty applied for each teammate flying near it.
  Finally, let $\lambda$ be the weight given to the semantic guide and
  $\beta(\gamma)$ the bonus it contributes. The score is

  $$
  s(\gamma)

  u(\gamma)
  - c_m, L(\mathbf{p},\gamma)
  - P_r,\mathbb{1}!\left[\gamma \text{ is claimed}\right]
  - P_v, n_v(\gamma)
  - \lambda,\beta(\gamma),
  \tag{3.12}
  $$

  where $\mathbb{1}[\cdot]$ is the indicator function and $n_v(\gamma)$ is the
  number of teammates currently flying within the neighbour radius of the
  candidate.

  The cost of travel is set to $c_m = 1$, which fixes the unit of the whole
  scale: one point of utility is exactly one metre of flight. A reading target,
  worth 30, is therefore worth flying up to thirty metres for, and a full
  coverage group is worth the same. This is the only place in the system where
  the exchange rate between knowledge and effort is defined, and expressing it
  in metres makes every other value in Table 3.6 interpretable.

  Table 3.7 — Terms of the score and their values.

  ┌───────────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       Term        │    Value     │                 Role                 │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Travel cost,      │  1 per metre │ Defines the unit of the scale        │
  │ $c_m$             │              │                                      │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Claim penalty,    │         1000 │ Excludes a candidate already claimed │
  │ $P_r$             │              │  by another vehicle                  │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Claim radius      │          4 m │ Distance below which two targets are │
  │                   │              │  considered the same                 │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Neighbour         │      40 per  │ Keeps vehicles from converging on    │
  │ penalty, $P_v$    │     teammate │ the same area                        │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Neighbour radius  │          4 m │ Distance below which a teammate is   │
  │                   │              │ considered near                      │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Guide bonus, zone │           10 │ Applied when the observed point lies │
  │                   │              │  in the advised area                 │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Guide bonus, side │            5 │ Applied when the approach side       │
  │                   │              │ matches the advised one              │
  └───────────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  3.4.4.2 Keeping Vehicles Apart

  Two terms of the score exist only to separate the vehicles, and they act at
  different moments.

  The claim penalty applies to a target that another vehicle has already
  announced. Its value is large enough to remove the candidate from
  consideration in practice, but it remains a penalty rather than a hard
  constraint. If every remaining target has been claimed, a vehicle still
  selects one instead of stopping, and the resulting duplicated effort is
  preferable to an idle vehicle at the end of a mission.

  The neighbour penalty applies to a target that is simply close to a teammate
  in flight, whether or not anything has been claimed there. Its value is 40,
  which is larger than the utility of a reading target. A label located within
  four metres of another vehicle therefore receives a negative score and loses
  to almost any alternative. The penalty is applied once per teammate, so an
  area occupied by two vehicles is penalised twice. This term is what spreads
  the team over the building without any explicit partition of space: each
  vehicle is repelled by the others through the score alone.

  The guide bonus is the only term that can be disabled. It is added when the
  observed point falls inside the area advised by the semantic guide, with an
  additional bonus when the approach side also matches the advice. Exploration
  candidates receive the zone bonus but not the side bonus, since they have no
  imposed approach direction. The weight $\lambda$ controls how much the advice
  is allowed to influence the ranking, and $\lambda = 0$ removes the term
  entirely, leaving a purely geometric score. The guide itself is described in
  Section 3.4.6.

  3.4.4.3 Feasibility and the Two-Stage Evaluation

  Equation (3.12) requires the route length $L(\mathbf{p},\gamma)$, which is
  obtained from the map as described in Section 3.4.2.5. Computing a route for
  every candidate at every decision would be far more expensive than the
  decision itself, since a mission produces hundreds of candidates at a time.

  The evaluation is therefore performed in two stages. In the first stage, the
  route length is replaced by the straight-line distance $\lVert
  \mathbf{q}\gamma - \mathbf{p} \rVert$, which gives a provisional score 
  $\bar{s}(\gamma)$. Since no route can be shorter than the straight line, 
  $L(\mathbf{p},\gamma) \geq \lVert \mathbf{q}\gamma - \mathbf{p} \rVert$ and
  therefore

  $$
  \bar{s}(\gamma) ;\geq; s(\gamma)
  \qquad \text{for every candidate } \gamma .
  \tag{3.13}
  $$

  The provisional score is consequently an upper bound on the final score, and a
  candidate whose upper bound is already low cannot become competitive once its
  true route is known. In the second stage, only the six best candidates by
  provisional score receive an actual route, and the best of those is selected.
  This is a bounded approximation of the exact choice rather than an exact
  procedure, but the bound is what makes it defensible: the candidates that are
  discarded are discarded on an optimistic estimate, not on a pessimistic one.

  Feasibility is resolved in the same stage. If the map contains no route to a
  candidate, that candidate is removed instead of being penalised. The system
  therefore never commits to an observation it cannot reach, and the
  reachability test uses the map as it is known at that moment, not a plan of
  the building.

  Two filters are applied before scoring. Candidates whose observed point has
  already been visited three times without success are removed, as described in
  Section 3.4.3.3. Candidates whose observed point is blacklisted are removed as
  well.

  Once a candidate is selected, the vehicle announces it in the map before
  starting to fly. That announcement, its expiry, and its release are the
  subject of Section 3.4.5.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.10 — fig_selection
  ▎
  ▎ Two-stage evaluation of the candidates.

  Algorithm 3.7 — SelectTarget
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: vehicle position p, candidate list Γ, map M, guide advice A, weight λ
  Ensure:  the selected candidate, or ⊥

   1:  Γ ← { γ ∈ Γ : γ not blacklisted and visited fewer than 3 times }
   2:  for each γ ∈ Γ do
   3:      s̄(γ) ← score with the straight-line distance          ▷ Eq. (3.12),
  (3.13)
   4:  end for
   5:  sort Γ by decreasing s̄
   6:  best ← ⊥
   7:  for each γ among the six first do
   8:      route ← Route(p, pose of γ, altitude of γ)             ▷ Alg. 3.4
   9:      if route = ⊥ then continue                             ▷ unreachable,
  discarded
  10:      L ← length of the route
  11:      s(γ) ← score with L                                    ▷ Eq. (3.12)
  12:      if best = ⊥ or s(γ) > s(best) then best ← γ
  13:  end for
  14:  return best
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ---
  Description de la figure

  Figure 3.10 — fig_selection

  Sujet. Comment un drone choisit une cible parmi tous les candidats
  disponibles.

  Ce qui est représenté. Le passage d'une liste complète de candidats à une
  seule cible retenue, en deux étapes.

  À l'entrée, la liste des candidats produite par la section précédente,
  identique pour tous les drones. Une première étape leur attribue à chacun une
  note provisoire, calculée avec la distance à vol d'oiseau, et les classe. Une
  seconde étape ne conserve que les six premiers et calcule pour chacun un vrai
  chemin sur la carte ; certains sont éliminés parce qu'aucun chemin n'existe,
  les autres reçoivent leur note définitive, qui est toujours inférieure ou
  égale à la provisoire. Le meilleur des survivants est retenu.

  La différence entre les deux notes d'un même candidat doit être visible, pour
  montrer que la première étape est optimiste et la seconde exacte.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Que la première étape ne sert pas à choisir
  mais à éliminer, et qu'elle peut le faire sans risque parce qu'elle surestime
  toujours la note.

  Ce qui n'y figure pas. Aucun temps de calcul, aucune structure de données,
  aucun nombre de candidats en dehors des six vérifiés.
