❯ what do ut hink about this version if there is a problem in here correct it   
  and give me the corrected version ### 3.4.4 Target Selection                  
                                                                                
  Candidate generation produces the same list for the whole team. Target        
  selection is where the vehicles differ: each vehicle evaluates this list from 
  its own position and selects one candidate. The selection process is          
  therefore executed independently on every vehicle, while the shared map is    
  the only common input.                                                        
                                                                                
  Selection has two main goals. First, it must combine the expected utility of  
  a candidate with the effort required to reach it. This allows the system to   
  compare, for example, a valuable target that is far away with a less valuable 
  target that is nearby. Second, it must make sure that the selected candidate  
  is reachable. A target that cannot be reached should not be selected.         
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.4.1 The Score                                                         
                                                                                
  Let $\gamma$ be a candidate with utility $u(\gamma)$ as defined in Section    
  3.4.3. Let $\mathbf{p}$ be the current position of the vehicle, and let       
  $L(\mathbf{p},\gamma)$ be the length of the route from $\mathbf{p}$ to the    
  observation pose of $\gamma$. The travel cost per metre is $c_m$. The term    
  $P_r$ is the penalty applied when another vehicle has already claimed the     
  candidate, while $P_v$ is the penalty applied for each teammate flying near   
  it. Finally, $\lambda$ controls the influence of the semantic guide, and      
  $\beta(\gamma)$ is the bonus provided by the guide.                           
                                                                                
  The score of a candidate is                                                   
                                                                                
  $$                                                                            
  s(\gamma)                                                                     
  =                                                                             
  u(\gamma)                                                                     
  - c_m L(\mathbf{p},\gamma)                                                    
  - P_r\,\mathbb{1}\!\left[\gamma \text{ is claimed}\right]                     
  - P_v\,n_v(\gamma)                                                            
  + \lambda\,\beta(\gamma),                                                     
  \tag{3.12}                                                                    
  $$                                                                            
                                                                                
  where $\mathbb{1}[\cdot]$ is the indicator function and $n_v(\gamma)$ is the  
  number of teammates currently flying within the neighbour radius of the       
  candidate.                                                                    
                                                                                
  The travel cost is set to $c_m=1$. This defines the unit of the score: one    
  point of utility corresponds to one metre of travel. A reading target with a  
  utility of 30 is therefore worth travelling up to thirty metres for, before   
  the other penalties and bonuses are considered. The same applies to a         
  coverage group whose utility reaches the value 30. This gives a direct        
  meaning to the utility values introduced in Table 3.6.                        
                                                                                
  **Table 3.7 — Terms of the score and their values.**                          
                                                                                
  | Term                     |           Value | Role                           
                                      |                                         
  | ------------------------ | --------------: |                                
  ------------------------------------------------------------------ |          
  | Travel cost, $c_m$       |     1 per metre | Defines the unit of the score  
                                      |                                         
  | Claim penalty, $P_r$     |            1000 | Effectively removes a          
  candidate already claimed by another vehicle |                                
  | Claim radius             |             4 m | Distance below which two       
  targets are considered the same           |                                   
  | Neighbour penalty, $P_v$ | 40 per teammate | Keeps vehicles from converging 
  on the same area                    |                                         
  | Neighbour radius         |             4 m | Distance below which a         
  teammate is considered near                 |                                 
  | Guide bonus, zone        |              10 | Applied when the observed      
  point is inside the advised area         |                                    
  | Guide bonus, side        |               5 | Applied when the approach side 
  matches the advice                  |                                         
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.4.2 Keeping Vehicles Apart                                            
                                                                                
  Two terms in the score are used to keep the vehicles separated. They act at   
  different levels.                                                             
                                                                                
  The claim penalty applies when another vehicle has already announced that it  
  is going to a candidate. Its value is large enough to remove that candidate   
  from consideration in normal situations, but it is still implemented as a     
  penalty rather than as a hard constraint. If all remaining candidates have    
  already been claimed, the vehicle can still select one. This avoids leaving a 
  vehicle idle simply because no unclaimed target remains.                      
                                                                                
  The neighbour penalty is different. It applies when a candidate is close to a 
  teammate, even if that candidate has not been claimed. Its value is 40,       
  which is higher than the utility of a reading target. A target located within 
  four metres of another vehicle therefore receives a negative contribution     
  and will normally lose to an alternative target. The penalty is applied once  
  for each nearby teammate, so a candidate close to two vehicles receives the   
  penalty twice. In this way, the vehicles naturally spread through the         
  warehouse without requiring the space to be explicitly divided between them.  
                                                                                
  The guide bonus is the only optional term in the score. It is added when the  
  observed point is inside the area recommended by the semantic guide. An       
  additional bonus is given when the approach side also matches the             
  recommendation. Exploration candidates receive the zone bonus but not the     
  side bonus because they do not have a required approach direction. The weight 
  $\lambda$ controls how strongly the guide affects the ranking. Setting        
  $\lambda=0$ removes the guide completely and leaves a purely geometric score. 
  The semantic guide itself is described in Section 3.4.6.                      
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.4.3 Feasibility and the Two-Stage Evaluation                          
                                                                                
  Equation (3.12) requires the route length $L(\mathbf{p},\gamma)$, which is    
  computed from the map as described in Section 3.4.2.5. Computing a complete   
  route for every candidate at every decision step would be unnecessarily       
  expensive, especially when hundreds of candidates can be generated during a   
  mission.                                                                      
                                                                                
  The system therefore evaluates candidates in two stages.                      
                                                                                
  In the first stage, the true route length is replaced by the straight-line    
  distance                                                                      
                                                                                
  $$                                                                            
  \left\lVert \mathbf{q}_{\gamma}-\mathbf{p}\right\rVert,                       
  $$                                                                            
                                                                                
  where $\mathbf{q}_{\gamma}$ is the observation pose of $\gamma$. This gives a 
  provisional score $\bar{s}(\gamma)$. Because a real route cannot be shorter   
  than the straight-line distance,                                              
                                                                                
  $$                                                                            
  L(\mathbf{p},\gamma)                                                          
  \geq                                                                          
  \left\lVert \mathbf{q}_{\gamma}-\mathbf{p}\right\rVert,                       
  $$                                                                            
                                                                                
  and therefore                                                                 
                                                                                
  $$                                                                            
  \bar{s}(\gamma) \geq s(\gamma)                                                
  \qquad \text{for every candidate } \gamma.                                    
  \tag{3.13}                                                                    
  $$                                                                            
                                                                                
  The provisional score is thus an upper bound on the final score. A candidate  
  with a low provisional score cannot become better after its real route is     
  computed. This allows the system to discard most candidates before performing 
  the more expensive route computation.                                         
                                                                                
  In the second stage, only the six candidates with the highest provisional     
  scores are evaluated with an actual route. The candidate with the highest     
  final score is then selected. This is an approximation of the exact           
  selection, but the approximation is controlled by the upper-bound property    
  above: candidates are discarded using an optimistic estimate of their final   
  score rather than an underestimated one.                                      
                                                                                
  Reachability is checked during the same second stage. If the map contains no  
  valid route to a candidate, that candidate is discarded instead of simply     
  receiving a lower score. The system therefore does not commit to an           
  observation that it cannot reach. The reachability test is based on the map   
  available at that moment, rather than on a predefined plan of the warehouse.  
                                                                                
  Two simple filters are applied before scoring. Candidates whose observed      
  point has already been visited three times without success are removed, as    
  described in Section 3.4.3.3. Candidates whose observed point is blacklisted  
  are also removed.                                                             
                                                                                
  Once a candidate has been selected, the vehicle announces it in the shared    
  map before starting the flight. The announcement, its expiration, and its     
  release are described in Section 3.4.5.                                       
                                                                                
  > **▸ FIGURE 3.10** — `fig_selection`                                         
  >                                                                             
  > *Two-stage evaluation of the generated candidates.*                         
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  Algorithm 3.7 — SelectTarget                                                  
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        
  Require: vehicle position p, candidate list Γ, map M, guide advice A, weight  
  λ                                                                             
  Ensure:  the selected candidate, or ⊥                                         
                                                                                
   1:  Γ ← { γ ∈ Γ : γ not blacklisted and visited fewer than 3 times }         
   2:  for each γ ∈ Γ do                                                        
   3:      s̄(γ) ← score with the straight-line distance          ▷ Eq. (3.12),  
  (3.13)                                                                        
   4:  end for                                                                  
   5:  sort Γ by decreasing s̄                                                   
   6:  best ← ⊥                                                                 
   7:  for each γ among the six first do                                        
   8:      route ← Route(p, pose of γ, altitude of γ)             ▷ Alg. 3.4    
   9:      if route = ⊥ then continue                             ▷             
  unreachable, discarded                                                        
  10:      L ← length of the route                                              
  11:      s(γ) ← score with L                                    ▷ Eq. (3.12)  
  12:      if best = ⊥ or s(γ) > s(best) then best ← γ                          
  13:  end for                                                                  
  14:  return best                                                              
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  > **▸ FIGURE 3.10** — `fig_selection`                                         
  >                                                                             
  > *The selection process first ranks candidates using their straight-line     
  distance, then evaluates the six best candidates using their actual routes    
  before selecting the highest-scoring reachable target.*

  3.4.4 Target Selection

  Candidate generation produces the same list for the whole team. Target
  selection is where the vehicles differ: each vehicle evaluates this list from
  its own position and selects one candidate. The selection process is therefore
  executed independently on every vehicle, while the shared map is the only
  common input.

  Selection has two main goals. First, it must combine the expected utility of a
  candidate with the effort required to reach it. This allows the system to
  compare, for example, a valuable target that is far away with a less valuable
  target that is nearby. Second, it must make sure that the selected candidate
  is reachable. A target that cannot be reached should not be selected.

  3.4.4.1 The Score

  Let $\gamma$ be a candidate with utility $u(\gamma)$ as defined in Section
  3.4.3. Let $\mathbf{p}$ be the current position of the vehicle, and let
  $L(\mathbf{p},\gamma)$ be the length of the route from $\mathbf{p}$ to the
  observation pose of $\gamma$. The travel cost per metre is $c_m$. The term
  $P_r$ is the penalty applied when another vehicle has already claimed the
  candidate, while $P_v$ is the penalty applied for each teammate flying near
  it. Finally, $\lambda$ controls the influence of the semantic guide, and
  $\beta(\gamma)$ is the bonus provided by the guide.

  The score of a candidate is

  $$
  s(\gamma)

  u(\gamma)
  - c_m L(\mathbf{p},\gamma)
  - P_r,\mathbb{1}!\left[\gamma \text{ is claimed}\right]
  - P_v,n_v(\gamma)
  - \lambda,\beta(\gamma),
  \tag{3.12}
  $$

  where $\mathbb{1}[\cdot]$ is the indicator function and $n_v(\gamma)$ is the
  number of teammates currently flying within the neighbour radius of the
  candidate.

  The claim and neighbour terms are evaluated at the observation pose of the
  candidate, whereas the guide bonus is evaluated at its observed point. Both
  distances are measured horizontally, so two vehicles at different shelf levels
  above one another are still treated as neighbours.

  The travel cost is set to $c_m=1$. This defines the unit of the score: one
  point of utility corresponds to one metre of travel. A reading target with a
  utility of 30 is therefore worth travelling up to thirty metres for, before
  the other penalties and bonuses are considered. The same applies to a coverage
  group whose utility reaches the value 30. This gives a direct meaning to the
  utility values introduced in Table 3.6.

  Table 3.7 — Terms of the score and their values.

  ┌──────────────────┬─────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       Term       │    Value    │                  Role                  │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Travel cost,     │ 1 per metre │ Defines the unit of the score          │
  │ $c_m$            │             │                                        │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Claim penalty,   │        1000 │ Effectively removes a candidate        │
  │ $P_r$            │             │ already claimed by another vehicle     │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Claim radius     │         4 m │ Distance below which two targets are   │
  │                  │             │ considered the same                    │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Neighbour        │     40 per  │ Keeps vehicles from converging on the  │
  │ penalty, $P_v$   │    teammate │ same area                              │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Neighbour radius │         4 m │ Horizontal distance below which a      │
  │                  │             │ teammate is considered near            │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Guide bonus,     │          10 │ Applied when the observed point is     │
  │ zone             │             │ inside the advised area                │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Guide bonus,     │           5 │ Applied when the approach side matches │
  │ side             │             │  the advice                            │
  └──────────────────┴─────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  3.4.4.2 Keeping Vehicles Apart

  Two terms in the score are used to keep the vehicles separated. They act at
  different levels.

  The claim penalty applies when another vehicle has already announced that it
  is going to a candidate. Its value is large enough to remove that candidate
  from consideration in normal situations, but it is still implemented as a
  penalty rather than as a hard constraint. If all remaining candidates have
  already been claimed, the vehicle can still select one. This avoids leaving a
  vehicle idle simply because no unclaimed target remains.

  The neighbour penalty is different. It applies when a candidate is close to a
  teammate, even if that candidate has not been claimed. Its value is 40, which
  is higher than the utility of a reading target. A target located within four
  metres of another vehicle therefore receives a negative contribution and will
  normally lose to an alternative target. The penalty is applied once for each
  nearby teammate, so a candidate close to two vehicles receives the penalty
  twice. In this way, the vehicles naturally spread through the warehouse
  without requiring the space to be explicitly divided between them.

  The guide bonus is the only optional term in the score. It is added when the
  observed point is inside the area recommended by the semantic guide. An
  additional bonus is given when the approach side also matches the
  recommendation. Exploration candidates receive the zone bonus but not the side
  bonus because they do not have a required approach direction. The weight
  $\lambda$ controls how strongly the guide affects the ranking. Setting
  $\lambda=0$ removes the guide completely and leaves a purely geometric score.
  The semantic guide itself is described in Section 3.4.6.

  3.4.4.3 Feasibility and the Two-Stage Evaluation

  Equation (3.12) requires the route length $L(\mathbf{p},\gamma)$, which is
  computed from the map as described in Section 3.4.2.5. Computing a complete
  route for every candidate would be unnecessarily expensive, because several
  hundred candidates are available at each decision.

  The system therefore evaluates candidates in two stages.

  In the first stage, the true route length is replaced by the straight-line
  distance $\lVert \mathbf{q}{\gamma}-\mathbf{p}\rVert$, where 
  $\mathbf{q}{\gamma}$ is the observation pose of $\gamma$. This gives a
  provisional score $\bar{s}(\gamma)$. A real route cannot be shorter than the
  straight-line distance, so $L(\mathbf{p},\gamma) \geq \lVert
  \mathbf{q}_{\gamma}-\mathbf{p}\rVert$ and therefore

  $$
  \bar{s}(\gamma) \geq s(\gamma)
  \qquad \text{for every candidate } \gamma.
  \tag{3.13}
  $$

  The provisional score is thus an upper bound on the final score. A candidate
  with a low provisional score cannot become better once its real route is
  computed. This allows the system to discard most candidates before performing
  the more expensive route computation.

  In the second stage, only the six candidates with the highest provisional
  scores are evaluated with an actual route. The candidate with the highest
  final score is then selected. This is an approximation of the exact selection,
  but the approximation is controlled by the upper-bound property above:
  candidates are discarded using an optimistic estimate of their final score
  rather than an underestimated one.

  Reachability is checked during the same second stage. If the map contains no
  valid route to a candidate, that candidate is discarded instead of simply
  receiving a lower score. The system therefore does not commit to an
  observation that it cannot reach. The reachability test is based on the map
  available at that moment, rather than on a predefined plan of the warehouse.

  Two simple filters are applied before scoring. Candidates whose observed point
  has already been visited three times without success are removed, as
  described in Section 3.4.3.3. Candidates whose observed point is blacklisted
  are also removed.

  It is possible for no candidate to survive these stages, either because the
  list is empty or because none of the six best candidates is reachable. The
  vehicle then holds its current position, and this situation is recorded. If
  every vehicle is simultaneously without a target, the mission is considered
  complete, as described in Section 3.5.

  Selection is not repeated at every control cycle. Once a candidate has been
  selected, the vehicle keeps it until the observation has been performed, the
  target has been abandoned, or the vehicle has been unable to make progress
  towards it. Only then is a new selection made.

  Finally, once a candidate has been selected, the vehicle announces it in the
  shared map before starting the flight. The announcement, its expiration, and
  its release are described in Section 3.4.5.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.10 — fig_selection
  ▎
  ▎ Two-stage evaluation of the generated candidates.

  Algorithm 3.7 — SelectTarget
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: vehicle position p, candidate list Γ, map M, guide advice A, weight λ
  Ensure:  the selected candidate, or ⊥

   1:  Γ ← { γ ∈ Γ : γ not blacklisted and visited fewer than 3 times }
   2:  for each γ ∈ Γ do
   3:      s̄(γ) ← score with the straight-line distance          ▷ Eq. (3.12),
  (3.13)
   4:  end for
   5:  sort Γ by decreasing s̄
   6:  best ← ⊥
   7:  for each γ among the six first do
   8:      route ← Route(p, pose of γ, altitude of γ)             ▷ Alg. 3.4
   9:      if route = ⊥ then continue                             ▷ unreachable,
  discarded
  10:      L ← length of the route
  11:      s(γ) ← score with L                                    ▷ Eq. (3.12)
  12:      if best = ⊥ or s(γ) > s(best) then best ← γ
  13:  end for
  14:  return best
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

● 3.4.5 Coordination

  Coordination keeps the vehicles from duplicating work and from colliding,
  without any direct communication between them. No message is sent and no
  agreement is negotiated: every vehicle writes what it is doing into the shared
  map, and reads what the others have written. All the rules described in this
  section operate on that single structure.

  Separation is handled at three different scales, because the problems are not
  the same. Two vehicles may choose the same target, which is a question of task
  allocation. Two vehicles may plan routes that cross, which is a question of
  path planning. Two vehicles may simply come too close, which is a question of
  immediate safety. Each scale has its own mechanism.

  Table 3.8 — The three scales of separation.

  ┌────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │ Scale  │                 Mechanism                 │      Acts on       │
  ├────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Task   │ Claim announced in the map, penalised in  │ The choice of a    │
  │        │ the score                                 │ target             │
  ├────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Route  │ Teammates inserted as moving obstacles of │ The computed path  │
  │        │  radius 2 m                               │                    │
  ├────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Flight │ Right of way below 2.5 m, full stop below │ The current motion │
  │        │  1.5 m                                    │                    │
  └────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.11 — fig_coordination
  ▎
  ▎ The three scales at which vehicles are kept apart.

  3.4.5.1 Claims and Their Expiry

  When a vehicle selects a target, it writes a claim into the map before
  starting to fly. A claim associates the vehicle with the position of the
  target and carries an expiry time. Other vehicles do not read the claim as a
  prohibition: they apply the penalty $P_r$ of Equation (3.12) to any candidate
  within four metres of a claimed position.

  A claim is not permanent. Let $e_i$ be the expiry time of the claim held by
  vehicle $i$, let $\tau_i$ be the last time that vehicle published its position
  in the map, and let $T_s$ be the tolerated silence. At time $t$, the claim is
  effective only if

  $$
  \operatorname{active}(i,t)
  \iff
  \bigl(t < e_i\bigr)
  \ \wedge
  \bigl(t - \tau_i \leq T_s\bigr).
  \tag{3.14}
  $$

  The first condition makes a claim expire unless it is renewed. Its lifetime is
  45 s, which is longer than a normal observation but shorter than the time a
  vehicle would need to become permanently stuck. The second condition removes
  the claims of a vehicle that has stopped reporting its position, with $T_s =
  5$ s.

  The same expiry applies to the blacklist. A target that has been abandoned is
  excluded for 120 s rather than permanently, because the reason for the
  abandonment may itself disappear: an obstacle may be removed, or a route may
  open once more of the building is known. A track that has failed three reading
  attempts is the exception and is excluded permanently, since three failures
  indicate that the detection was wrong rather than that the approach was
  difficult.

  Algorithm 3.8 — AgeSharedState
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: map M, current time t
  Ensure:  expired entries removed, silent vehicles reported

   1:  remove every claim whose expiry time is earlier than t
   2:  remove every blacklist entry whose expiry time is earlier than t
   3:  S ← { i : t − τ_i > T_s }                    ▷ vehicles no longer
  reporting
   4:  for each i ∈ S do
   5:      remove the claim held by i
   6:  end for
   7:  return S
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  3.4.5.2 Separation in Space

  Claims prevent two vehicles from choosing the same work, but they say nothing
  about the space between the vehicles. Two targets four metres apart are
  distinct, yet the routes leading to them may still cross.

  Separation during planning is obtained by writing the teammates into the cost
  grid of Section 3.4.2.5 as moving obstacles of radius 2 m. A vehicle computing
  a route therefore treats the others as structure, and avoids them before it
  is anywhere near them. This is preferable to a late correction, because a
  route that already goes around a teammate does not need to be interrupted.

  Separation during flight is the last resort and is deliberately simple. When
  two vehicles are closer than 2.5 m horizontally and their altitudes differ by
  less than 1.2 m, the vehicle with the higher index stops and lets the other
  pass. Below 1.5 m, both vehicles stop regardless of priority. Priority by
  index is arbitrary, but it defines a total order over the team, which is what
  guarantees that two vehicles facing each other never both yield or both
  proceed.

  The vertical condition matters as much as the horizontal one. Vehicles
  frequently work on different shelf levels of the same rack, and treating them
  as conflicting whenever their horizontal distance is small would stop the team
  far more often than necessary.

  3.4.5.3 Releasing Work

  A claim is released as soon as the vehicle stops working on it. Four
  situations produce a release, and they differ in what happens to the target
  afterwards.

  Table 3.9 — Events that release a claim.

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬─────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │            Event             │    Claim    │          Target           │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ The observation has been     │             │ Removed from the          │
  │ performed                    │ Released    │ candidates, since the map │
  │                              │             │  has changed              │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ The flight controller        │ Released    │ Blacklisted for 120 s     │
  │ abandons the target          │             │                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ The vehicle has not moved 30 │             │                           │
  │  cm in 15 s while holding a  │ Released    │ Blacklisted for 120 s     │
  │ target                       │             │                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ The vehicle stops reporting  │ Released by │ Left available to the     │
  │ for more than 5 s            │  expiry     │ others                    │
  └──────────────────────────────┴─────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  The third case deserves attention because it covers several different failures
  with one rule. A vehicle that has held a target for fifteen seconds without
  moving thirty centimetres is stuck, but the cause may be a teammate blocking
  the aisle, a structure the map does not yet contain, or a sequence of route
  recomputations that cancel each other. The system does not attempt to
  distinguish these causes. It abandons the target, blacklists it so that no
  vehicle immediately tries again, and makes a new selection.

  The fourth case is the one that makes the loss of a vehicle harmless. Nothing
  in the system observes the health of a vehicle, and no component is
  responsible for detecting a failure. A vehicle that stops flying simply stops
  writing its position into the map, and Equation (3.14) then invalidates its
  claims. The work it was holding becomes available to the others within the
  silence tolerance, and the mission continues. The same mechanism covers a
  simulated failure, a crash, and a vehicle that has become unresponsive,
  because none of them is treated as a special case.

  Description de la figure

  Figure 3.11 — fig_coordination

  Sujet. Les trois échelles auxquelles les drones sont tenus à l'écart les uns
  des autres.

  Ce qui est représenté. Une même portion d'entrepôt vue de dessus, montrée
  trois fois, une par échelle.

  À l'échelle de la tâche, deux drones et l'ensemble des candidats disponibles.
  L'un a revendiqué une cible ; les candidats situés à moins de quatre mètres de
  cette revendication sont marqués comme fortement pénalisés pour l'autre
  drone, qui en choisit donc une ailleurs.

  À l'échelle du chemin, les deux drones ont des cibles distinctes, mais leurs
  trajets pourraient se croiser. Chacun est représenté comme un obstacle de deux
  mètres de rayon dans la carte de coût de l'autre, et les deux chemins
  calculés se contournent au lieu de se croiser.

  À l'échelle du vol, les deux drones sont proches malgré tout. Deux seuils
  apparaissent : à deux mètres et demi en horizontal, avec moins d'un mètre
  vingt d'écart en altitude, celui qui porte le plus grand indice s'arrête ; à
  un mètre et demi, les deux s'arrêtent. L'écart vertical doit être visible,
  pour montrer que deux drones travaillant à des étages différents du même rack
  ne sont pas en conflit.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Que les trois mécanismes ne se substituent
  pas les uns aux autres mais se complètent, et qu'aucun des trois ne repose sur
  un échange de messages : tout passe par la carte partagée.

  Ce qui n'y figure pas. Aucune durée, aucun protocole, aucun nom de composant
  logiciel.

  the same shared structure.

  planning problem. Two vehicles may simply get too close during flight, which

  ### 3.4.5.1 Claims and Their Expiry                                           
                                                                                
  When a vehicle selects a target, it writes a claim into the map before        
  starting to fly. The claim associates the vehicle with the target position    
  and includes an expiry time. Other vehicles do not treat this claim as a      
  strict prohibition. Instead, they apply the penalty $P_r$ from Equation       
  (3.12) to candidates located within four metres of the claimed position.      
                                                                                
  A claim is not permanent. Let $e_i$ be the expiry time of the claim held by   
  vehicle $i$, $\tau_i$ the last time that vehicle published its position in    
  the map, and $T_s$ the tolerated silence period. At time $t$, the claim is    
  active only if                                                                
                                                                                
  $$                                                                            
  \operatorname{active}(i,t)                                                    
  \iff                                                                          
  \bigl(t < e_i\bigr)                                                           
  \ \wedge\                                                                     
  \bigl(t-\tau_i \leq T_s\bigr).                                                
  \tag{3.14}                                                                    
  $$                                                                            
                                                                                
  The first condition makes the claim expire if it is not renewed. Its lifetime 
  is 45 s, which is longer than a normal observation but short enough to        
  prevent a vehicle from keeping a target indefinitely. The second condition    
  removes the claim when the vehicle has stopped reporting its position, with   
  $T_s=5$ s.                                                                    
                                                                                
  The same expiry mechanism is used for temporary blacklist entries. A target   
  that has been abandoned is excluded for 120 s rather than permanently,        
  because the reason for the abandonment may disappear. For example, an         
  obstacle may be removed, or a valid route may become available after more of  
  the building has been mapped. A track that has failed three reading attempts  
  is treated differently and is excluded permanently, because repeated failure  
  is considered evidence that the detection itself was incorrect rather than    
  that the approach was temporarily difficult.                                  
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  Algorithm 3.8 — AgeSharedState                                                
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        
  Require: map M, current time t                                                
  Ensure:  expired entries removed, silent vehicles reported                    
                                                                                
   1:  remove every claim whose expiry time is earlier than t                   
   2:  remove every blacklist entry whose expiry time is earlier than t         
   3:  S ← { i : t − τ_i > T_s }                    ▷ vehicles no longer        
  reporting                                                                     
   4:  for each i ∈ S do                                                        
   5:      remove the claim held by i                                           
   6:  end for                                                                  
   7:  return S                                                                 
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.5.2 Separation in Space                                               
                                                                                
  Claims prevent two vehicles from selecting the same work, but they do not     
  control the physical distance between them. Two targets may be four metres    
  apart and still have routes that cross.                                       
                                                                                
  During route planning, teammates are therefore added to the cost grid of      
  Section 3.4.2.5 as moving obstacles with a radius of 2 m. A vehicle computing 
  a route consequently treats the other vehicles as obstacles and tries to      
  avoid them from the beginning. This is preferable to correcting the           
  trajectory only after the vehicles have already come close.                   
                                                                                
  During flight, a simpler rule provides a final safety layer. When two         
  vehicles are closer than 2.5 m horizontally and their altitude difference is  
  less than 1.2 m, the vehicle with the higher index stops and gives way to the 
  other. When the horizontal distance falls below 1.5 m, both vehicles stop,    
  regardless of their priority.                                                 
                                                                                
  The priority based on the vehicle index is arbitrary, but it gives the team a 
  fixed order. This guarantees that two vehicles facing each other do not both  
  yield or both continue.                                                       
                                                                                
  The vertical condition is also important. Vehicles can work on different      
  shelf levels of the same rack, so a small horizontal distance does not        
  necessarily mean that they are in conflict. Considering altitude prevents     
  unnecessary stops when the vehicles are safely separated vertically.          
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.5.3 Releasing Work                                                    
                                                                                
  A claim is released as soon as the vehicle stops working on its target. Four  
  situations can cause this release, and each one leads to a different state    
  for the target afterwards.                                                    
                                                                                
  **Table 3.9 — Events that release a claim.**                                  
                                                                                
  | Event                                                          | Claim      
          | Target                                                  |           
  | -------------------------------------------------------------- |            
  ------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------  
  |                                                                             
  | The observation has been performed                             | Released   
          | Removed from the candidates because the map has changed |           
  | The flight controller abandons the target                      | Released   
          | Blacklisted for 120 s                                   |           
  | The vehicle has not moved 30 cm in 15 s while holding a target | Released   
          | Blacklisted for 120 s                                   |           
  | The vehicle stops reporting for more than 5 s                  | Released   
  by expiry | Made available to the other vehicles                    |         
                                                                                
  The third case is important because one simple rule covers several possible   
  failures. If a vehicle has held a target for fifteen seconds without moving   
  at least thirty centimetres, it is considered stuck. The cause may be another 
  vehicle blocking the aisle, a structure that is not yet present in the map,   
  or repeated route recomputations. The system does not try to identify the     
  exact cause. Instead, it releases the target, blacklists it temporarily, and  
  starts a new selection.                                                       
                                                                                
  The fourth case allows the team to continue after the loss of a vehicle. No   
  separate component is responsible for detecting a vehicle failure. A vehicle  
  that stops flying simply stops publishing its position in the map. Equation   
  (3.14) then invalidates its claims after the silence tolerance is reached.    
  The targets it was holding become available to the other vehicles, and the    
  mission can continue.                                                         
                                                                                
  The same mechanism also covers a simulated failure, a crash, or an            
  unresponsive vehicle. None of these situations requires a special recovery    
  procedure: the absence of new state information is enough for the shared map  
  to release the corresponding work.

● Deux erreurs de fond, dont une était déjà dans ma version.

  La revendication n'est jamais renouvelée. Tu écris qu'elle est « short enough
  to prevent a vehicle from keeping a target indefinitely », et moi j'avais
  écrit « expires unless it is renewed ». Ni l'un ni l'autre n'est exact : dans
  le code, reserve est appelé une seule fois, au moment où la cible est
  attribuée, et rien ne la prolonge. C'est un bail de 45 s à durée fixe,
  remplacé seulement quand le drone choisit une cible suivante.

  « No separate component is responsible for detecting a vehicle failure » est 
  trop fort. La boucle de mission détecte bien une chute : altitude sous 0,3 m
  ou inclinaison au-delà de 70°, et elle libère les revendications
  immédiatement. La règle de silence reste le mécanisme général, mais elle n'est
  pas seule.

  Troisième point, mineur : les lignes 2 et 3 de la table 3.9 sont le même
  mécanisme déclenché de deux façons. Le contrôleur et le test d'immobilité
  placent tous deux la cible dans le même état abandonné, et la libération comme
  la mise en liste noire ont lieu à la décision suivante.

  Voilà la version complète corrigée.

  3.4.5 Coordination

  Coordination prevents the vehicles from duplicating work and helps them avoid
  collisions, without requiring direct communication between them. No message is
  exchanged and no agreement is negotiated. Instead, each vehicle writes its
  current state into the shared map and reads the information written by the
  others. All coordination rules described in this section therefore operate on
  the same shared structure.

  Separation is handled at three different scales because the problems are
  different. Two vehicles may select the same target, which is a task allocation
  problem. Two vehicles may plan routes that cross, which is a path planning
  problem. Two vehicles may simply get too close during flight, which is an
  immediate safety problem. Each case is handled by a different mechanism.

  Table 3.8 — The three scales of separation.

  ┌────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │ Scale  │                 Mechanism                  │      Acts on      │
  ├────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Task   │ Claim announced in the map and penalised   │ The choice of a   │
  │        │ in the score                               │ target            │
  ├────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Route  │ Teammates inserted as moving obstacles     │ The computed path │
  │        │ with a radius of 2 m                       │                   │
  ├────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Flight │ Right of way below 2.5 m, full stop below  │ The current       │
  │        │ 1.5 m                                      │ motion            │
  └────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────┘

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.11 — fig_coordination
  ▎
  ▎ The three scales used to keep the vehicles separated.

  3.4.5.1 Claims and Their Expiry

  When a vehicle selects a target, it writes a claim into the map before
  starting to fly. The claim associates the vehicle with the target position and
  includes an expiry time. Other vehicles do not treat this claim as a strict
  prohibition. Instead, they apply the penalty $P_r$ from Equation (3.12) to
  candidates located within four metres of the claimed position.

  A claim is not permanent. Let $e_i$ be the expiry time of the claim held by
  vehicle $i$, $\tau_i$ the last time that vehicle published its position in the
  map, and $T_s$ the tolerated silence period. At time $t$, the claim is active
  only if

  $$
  \operatorname{active}(i,t)
  \iff
  \bigl(t < e_i\bigr)
  \ \wedge
  \bigl(t-\tau_i \leq T_s\bigr).
  \tag{3.14}
  $$

  The first condition gives the claim a fixed lifetime of 45 s, counted from the
  moment the target is assigned. The claim is not extended while the vehicle
  works on the target; it is simply replaced when the vehicle selects its next
  one. The duration must therefore be long enough to cover a complete
  observation, including the transit, the approach, and the time spent holding
  the pose, and short enough that a vehicle which stops making progress does not
  reserve a target for much longer than it can use it.

  The second condition removes the claim when the vehicle has stopped reporting
  its position, with $T_s=5$ s. A vehicle publishes its position at every cycle,
  so this condition is satisfied continuously in normal operation.

  The same expiry mechanism is used for temporary blacklist entries. A target
  that has been abandoned is excluded for 120 s rather than permanently, because
  the reason for the abandonment may disappear. For example, an obstacle may be
  removed, or a valid route may become available after more of the building has
  been mapped. A track that has failed three reading attempts is treated
  differently and is excluded permanently, because repeated failure is
  considered evidence that the detection itself was incorrect rather than that
  the approach was temporarily difficult.

  Algorithm 3.8 — AgeSharedState
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: map M, current time t
  Ensure:  expired entries removed, silent vehicles reported

   1:  remove every claim whose expiry time is earlier than t
   2:  remove every blacklist entry whose expiry time is earlier than t
   3:  S ← { i : t − τ_i > T_s }                    ▷ vehicles no longer
  reporting
   4:  for each i ∈ S do
   5:      remove the claim held by i
   6:  end for
   7:  return S
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  3.4.5.2 Separation in Space

  Claims prevent two vehicles from selecting the same work, but they do not
  control the physical distance between them. Two targets may be four metres
  apart and still have routes that cross.

  During route planning, teammates are therefore added to the cost grid of
  Section 3.4.2.5 as moving obstacles with a radius of 2 m. A vehicle computing
  a route consequently treats the other vehicles as obstacles and tries to avoid
  them from the beginning. This is preferable to correcting the trajectory only
  after the vehicles have already come close.

  During flight, a simpler rule provides a final safety layer. When two vehicles
  are closer than 2.5 m horizontally and their altitude difference is less than
  1.2 m, the vehicle with the higher index stops and gives way to the other.
  When the horizontal distance falls below 1.5 m, both vehicles stop, regardless
  of their priority.

  The priority based on the vehicle index is arbitrary, but it gives the team a
  fixed order. This guarantees that two vehicles facing each other do not both
  yield or both continue.

  The vertical condition is also important. Vehicles can work on different shelf
  levels of the same rack, so a small horizontal distance does not necessarily
  mean that they are in conflict. Considering altitude prevents unnecessary
  stops when the vehicles are safely separated vertically.

  3.4.5.3 Releasing Work

  A claim is released as soon as the vehicle stops working on its target. Four
  situations can cause this release, and each one leads to a different state for
  the target afterwards.

  Table 3.9 — Events that release a claim.

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬─────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │            Event             │    Claim    │           Target           │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ The observation has been     │             │ Removed from the           │
  │ performed                    │ Released    │ candidates because the map │
  │                              │             │  has changed               │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ The flight controller        │ Released    │ Blacklisted for 120 s      │
  │ abandons the target          │             │                            │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ The vehicle has not moved 30 │             │                            │
  │  cm in 15 s while holding a  │ Released    │ Blacklisted for 120 s      │
  │ target                       │             │                            │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ The vehicle stops reporting  │ Released by │ Made available to the      │
  │ for more than 5 s            │  expiry     │ other vehicles             │
  └──────────────────────────────┴─────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  The second and third rows describe the same mechanism activated by two
  different triggers. In both cases the target is placed in an abandoned state,
  and the release together with the blacklist entry are applied when the vehicle
  performs its next selection.

  The third case is important because one simple rule covers several possible
  failures. If a vehicle has held a target for fifteen seconds without moving at
  least thirty centimetres, it is considered stuck. The cause may be another
  vehicle blocking the aisle, a structure that is not yet present in the map, or
  repeated route recomputations. The system does not try to identify the exact
  cause. Instead, it releases the target, blacklists it temporarily, and starts
  a new selection.

  The fourth case allows the team to continue after the loss of a vehicle. A
  vehicle that stops flying also stops publishing its position in the map, and
  Equation (3.14) then invalidates its claims once the silence tolerance is
  reached. The targets it was holding become available to the other vehicles,
  and the mission continues.

  This mechanism requires no diagnosis, since the absence of new state
  information is sufficient whatever the cause. Two situations are nevertheless
  detected explicitly, because they can be recognised without ambiguity and
  because releasing the work immediately is preferable to waiting for the
  silence delay: a vehicle whose altitude falls below 0.3 m, and a vehicle whose
  tilt exceeds 70 degrees. In both cases the vehicle is marked as lost and its
  claims are released at once. The silence rule remains the general mechanism
  and covers every other case, including a vehicle that has become unresponsive
  without falling.

● 3.4.6 The Semantic Guide

  The semantic guide is the only component of the system that is not geometric.
  It is a pre-trained vision-language model that is shown the current state of
  the search and asked where the team should look next. Its answer does not
  decide anything: it is converted into a bonus that enters the score of
  Equation (3.12), weighted by $\lambda$.

  The component is optional by construction. Setting $\lambda = 0$ removes its
  contribution from the score, and the system then behaves exactly as if the
  guide were absent. Everything described in the previous sections continues to
  work, because no other component reads the advice.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.12 — fig_guide
  ▎
  ▎ What the guide is shown, what it is asked, and how its answer reaches the 
  ▎ score.

  3.4.6.1 What the Guide Is Asked

  A pre-trained model cannot be asked an open question about the mission,
  because its answer would have to be interpreted before it could be used. The
  two questions put to the guide are therefore closed, and both can be answered
  with a single word.

  The first question is which area the vehicle should visit next. To make this
  answerable, the candidates produced by Section 3.4.3 are grouped on a
  three-metre grid, the resulting groups are sorted by total utility, and at
  most six of them are retained and numbered from one. The guide answers with a
  number, which removes any need to interpret free text.

  The second question is from which side the shelf in that area should be
  approached. The answer is one of the four horizontal sides, expressed as a
  cardinal direction. This second question exists because choosing the correct
  side is a recurring difficulty for the geometric part: the side is derived
  from the structure surrounding a label, as described in Section 3.4.3.2, and
  that inference fails when the structure is not yet well mapped.

  3.4.6.2 What the Guide Is Given

  Two rules govern what the guide receives, and both are design decisions rather
  than practical constraints.

  The first rule is that the guide is given only what the map already knows. It
  never receives the position of an unread code, the layout of the building, or
  any other information that the team has not established by observation.
  Without this rule the guide would answer from the simulator rather than from
  the mission, and any improvement it produced would be meaningless.

  The second rule is that the guide is given facts, never scores. For each zone
  it is told how many codes have been spotted but not read, how many shelf
  sections holding boxes have never been looked at, whether unexplored space is
  present, which way the shelf faces there, and how far the zone is from the
  vehicle. It is never told the utility or the score that the geometric part has
  already computed for that zone. If it were, it would learn to reproduce the
  geometric ranking, and the advice would add nothing to the decision it is
  supposed to inform.

  Table 3.10 — Information available to the guide.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │                  Given                  │           Not given           │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Number of spotted but unread codes per  │ Position of any unread code   │
  │ zone                                    │                               │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Number of shelf sections with boxes     │ Layout of the building        │
  │ never observed                          │                               │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Presence of unexplored space            │ Utility or score of a zone    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Facing direction of the shelves in the  │ The choice made by the        │
  │ zone                                    │ geometric part                │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Distance from the vehicle to the zone   │ Ground truth of any kind      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  The state can be presented to the guide in two forms. The first is visual: the
  model is shown the current camera image together with a top view of the map
  on which the candidate zones are drawn and numbered. The second is textual:
  the same information is written as a structured description of each zone,
  without any image. The two forms carry the same facts and differ only in how
  they are expressed, which allows models that accept images and models that do
  not to be compared on the same task.

  3.4.6.3 How the Advice Enters the Decision

  The advice is a zone, defined by a centre and a radius, and optionally a side.
  Let $\mathbf{o}\gamma$ be the observed point of a candidate, $\sigma\gamma$
  its approach side, $(\mathbf{z}, \rho)$ the advised zone, and $\sigma_a$ the
  advised side. The bonus used in Equation (3.12) is

  $$
  \beta(\gamma)

  B_z,\mathbb{1}!\left[\lVert \mathbf{o}\gamma - \mathbf{z}\rVert \leq 
  \rho\right]
  +
  B_s,\mathbb{1}!\left[\sigma\gamma = \sigma_a \ \wedge\ \text{kind}(\gamma)
  \neq \textsc{Explore}\right],
  \tag{3.15}
  $$

  where $B_z = 10$ is the bonus for being inside the advised zone and $B_s = 5$
  the additional bonus for matching the advised side. Both distances are
  measured horizontally. Exploration candidates receive the zone bonus but not
  the side bonus, since they have no imposed approach direction.

  The guide is never waited for. A request is sent in the background while the
  vehicle continues to fly, and the answer is collected at a later cycle and
  used from the next decision onwards. A minimum interval separates two
  consecutive requests from the same vehicle, and a new request is only sent
  once the previous one has returned. If no advice has been received yet, or if
  the model returns an answer that does not correspond to any proposed zone, the
  term $\beta(\gamma)$ is simply zero and the decision is made on geometry
  alone.

  This asynchronous arrangement is what makes the component safe to add. The
  decision loop never blocks on the model, so a slow answer costs nothing, and a
  missing answer costs nothing either.

  Algorithm 3.9 — RequestAdvice
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: vehicle i, map M, candidate list Γ, minimum interval Δ
  Ensure:  the advice held by vehicle i is updated when one is available

   1:  if the last request from i is more recent than Δ then return
   2:  if a request from i is still running then
   3:      if it has not completed then return
   4:      A ← its result
   5:      if A ≠ ⊥ then store A as the advice of vehicle i
   6:  end if
   7:  Z ← group Γ on a 3 m grid, sort by total utility, keep at most 6, number
  them
   8:  if Z = ∅ then return
   9:  build the state description from M and Z                ▷ facts only, no
  scores
  10:  send the two questions in the background
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  The weight $\lambda$ used in each reported mission, and the effect of the
  guide on the final result, are given in Chapter 5.

  ---
  Description de la figure

  Figure 3.12 — fig_guide

  Sujet. Ce que le guide reçoit, ce qu'on lui demande, et par où sa réponse
  entre dans la décision.

  Ce qui est représenté. Trois moments, de gauche à droite.

  À gauche, ce que le guide reçoit : la vue de dessus de la carte à cet instant,
  avec les zones candidates entourées et numérotées de un à six, et la
  description en mots de ce que la carte sait de chacune — des faits seulement,
  jamais la note. L'image de la caméra latérale accompagne la vue de dessus dans
  la variante visuelle.

  Au centre, les deux questions et la forme de leur réponse : un numéro de zone,
  et une direction cardinale.

  À droite, l'entrée dans le score : la zone conseillée reçoit un bonus, et les
  candidats dont le côté d'abordage correspond au côté conseillé en reçoivent un
  second. Le poids λ est visible sur ce chemin, ainsi que le fait qu'à zéro il
  l'annule entièrement.

  Une indication doit montrer que la demande part en arrière-plan et que la
  réponse arrive plus tard, pendant que le drone continue de voler.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Que le guide conseille sans décider, qu'il
  ne voit que ce que l'équipe a elle-même observé, et que le système fonctionne
  inchangé si sa réponse n'arrive jamais.

  Ce qui n'y figure pas. Aucun nom de modèle, aucun texte de prompt, aucune
  latence.

  content.   == ### 3.4.6 The Semantic Guide                                    
                                                                                
  The semantic guide is the only component of the system that does not rely     
  only on geometric information. It is a pre-trained vision-language model that 
  receives the current state of the search and suggests where the team should   
  look next. Its answer does not directly select a target. Instead, it is       
  converted into a bonus that is added to the score in Equation (3.12), with a  
  weight $\lambda$.                                                             
                                                                                
  The guide is optional by design. Setting $\lambda=0$ removes its contribution 
  from the score, so the system behaves exactly as if the guide were not        
  present. All other components continue to work normally because none of them  
  depends on the guide's advice.                                                
                                                                                
  > **▸ FIGURE 3.12** — `fig_guide`                                             
  >                                                                             
  > *What the guide receives, what it is asked, and how its answer affects the  
  target score.*                                                                
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.6.1 What the Guide Is Asked                                           
                                                                                
  The guide is not asked an open question about the mission. Such a question    
  would produce free text that would then have to be interpreted before it      
  could be used. Instead, the two questions are designed so that each answer    
  can be given with a single word or number.                                    
                                                                                
  The first question asks which area the vehicle should visit next. To make     
  this possible, the candidates generated in Section 3.4.3 are grouped on a     
  three-metre grid. The groups are then sorted by their total utility, and at   
  most six groups are kept and numbered. The guide only needs to return one of  
  these numbers, so no interpretation of free text is required.                 
                                                                                
  The second question asks from which side the shelf in the selected area       
  should be approached. The answer is one of the four horizontal sides,         
  expressed as a cardinal direction. This question is useful because            
  determining the correct observation side is a recurring difficulty for the    
  geometric part. As described in Section 3.4.3.2, the side is inferred from    
  the structure surrounding a label, but this inference can be unreliable when  
  the surrounding structure has not yet been sufficiently mapped.               
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.6.2 What the Guide Is Given                                           
                                                                                
  Two rules define the information given to the guide. Both are deliberate      
  design choices.                                                               
                                                                                
  First, the guide receives only information that is already known from the     
  map. It never receives the position of an unread code, the complete layout of 
  the building, or any other information that the team has not discovered       
  through observation. Otherwise, the guide could answer using information that 
  is unavailable to the vehicles, and its contribution would not reflect the    
  actual search process.                                                        
                                                                                
  Second, the guide receives facts, not scores. For each zone, it is given the  
  number of codes that have been detected but not yet read, the number of shelf 
  sections containing boxes that have not yet been observed, whether            
  unexplored space is present, the facing direction of the shelves, and the     
  distance from the vehicle to the zone. It is not given the utility or score   
  already computed by the geometric selector. This prevents the guide from      
  simply reproducing the geometric ranking and allows its advice to provide an  
  additional source of information.                                             
                                                                                
  **Table 3.10 — Information available to the guide.**                          
                                                                                
  | Given                                                             | Not     
  given                             |                                           
  | ----------------------------------------------------------------- |         
  ------------------------------------- |                                       
  | Number of spotted but unread codes per zone                       |         
  Position of any unread code           |                                       
  | Number of shelf sections with boxes that have never been observed | Layout  
  of the building                |                                              
  | Presence of unexplored space                                      | Utility 
  or score of a zone            |                                               
  | Facing direction of the shelves in the zone                       | Choice  
  made by the geometric selector |                                              
  | Distance from the vehicle to the zone                             | Ground  
  truth of any kind              |                                              
                                                                                
  The state can be presented to the guide in two forms. The first is visual:    
  the model receives the current camera image together with a top view of the   
  map showing the candidate zones and their numbers. The second is textual: the 
  same information is provided as a structured description of each zone,        
  without an image. Both forms contain the same facts and differ only in how    
  those facts are presented. This allows models that accept images and models   
  that do not to be evaluated on the same task.                                 
                                                                                
  ### 3.4.6.3 How the Advice Enters the Decision                                
                                                                                
  The guide returns a zone defined by a centre and a radius, and optionally an  
  approach side. Let $\mathbf{o}*\gamma$ be the observed point of candidate     
  $\gamma$, $\sigma*\gamma$ its approach side, $(\mathbf{z},\rho)$ the advised  
  zone, and $\sigma_a$ the advised side. The bonus used in Equation (3.12) is   
                                                                                
  $$                                                                            
  \beta(\gamma)                                                                 
  =                                                                             
  B_z\,                                                                         
  \mathbb{1}\!\left[                                                            
  \lVert \mathbf{o}_\gamma-\mathbf{z}\rVert \leq \rho                           
  \right]                                                                       
  +                                                                             
  B_s\,                                                                         
  \mathbb{1}\!\left[                                                            
  \sigma_\gamma=\sigma_a                                                        
  \ \wedge\                                                                     
  \operatorname{kind}(\gamma)\neq\textsc{Explore}                               
  \right],                                                                      
  \tag{3.15}                                                                    
  $$                                                                            
                                                                                
  where $B_z=10$ is the bonus for being inside the advised zone and $B_s=5$ is  
  the additional bonus for matching the advised side. Both distances are        
  measured horizontally. Exploration candidates receive the zone bonus but not  
  the side bonus because they have no imposed approach direction.               
                                                                                
  The guide is never allowed to block the vehicle. A request is sent in the     
  background while the vehicle continues its current task. When the answer      
  becomes available, it is stored and used from the next decision cycle. A      
  minimum interval separates two consecutive requests from the same vehicle,    
  and a new request is sent only after the previous request has finished.       
                                                                                
  If no advice has been received, or if the model returns an answer that does   
  not correspond to any of the proposed zones, $\beta(\gamma)$ is set to zero.  
  The vehicle then makes its decision using the geometric score alone.          
                                                                                
  This asynchronous design makes the guide safe to add to the system. The       
  decision loop never waits for the model. A slow response therefore does not   
  stop the vehicle, and a missing or invalid response has no effect on the rest 
  of the system.                                                                
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  Algorithm 3.9 — RequestAdvice                                                 
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        
  Require: vehicle i, map M, candidate list Γ, minimum interval Δ               
  Ensure:  the advice held by vehicle i is updated when one is available        
                                                                                
   1:  if the last request from i is more recent than Δ then return             
   2:  if a request from i is still running then                                
   3:      if it has not completed then return                                  
   4:      A ← its result                                                       
   5:      if A ≠ ⊥ then store A as the advice of vehicle i                     
   6:  end if                                                                   
   7:  Z ← group Γ on a 3 m grid, sort by total utility, keep at most 6, number 
  them                                                                          
   8:  if Z = ∅ then return                                                     
   9:  build the state description from M and Z                ▷ facts only, no 
  scores                                                                        
  10:  send the two questions in the background                                 
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────        
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  The value of $\lambda$ used in each reported mission, and the effect of the   
  guide on the final results, are presented in Chapter 5.
