  ---
  3.4.6 The Semantic Guide

  The semantic guide is the only component of the system that does not rely only
  on geometric information. It is a pre-trained vision-language model that
  receives the current state of the search and suggests where the vehicle should
  look next. Its answer does not directly select a target. Instead, it is
  converted into a bonus that is added to the score in Equation (3.12), with a
  weight $\lambda$.

  The guide is optional by design. Setting $\lambda=0$ removes its contribution
  from the score, so the system behaves exactly as if the guide were not
  present. All other components continue to work normally because none of them
  depends on the guide's advice.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 3.12 — fig_guide
  ▎
  ▎ What the guide receives, what it is asked, and how its answer affects the 
  ▎ target score.

  3.4.6.1 What the Guide Is Asked

  The guide is not asked an open question about the mission. Such a question
  would produce free text that would then have to be interpreted before it could
  be used. Instead, the two questions are designed so that each answer can be
  given with a single word or number.

  The first question asks which area the vehicle should visit next. To make this
  possible, the candidates generated in Section 3.4.3 are grouped on a
  three-metre grid. The groups are then sorted by their total utility, and at
  most six groups are kept and numbered. The guide only needs to return one of
  these numbers, so no interpretation of free text is required.

  The second question asks from which side the shelf in the selected area should
  be approached. The answer is one of the four horizontal sides, expressed as a
  cardinal direction. This question is useful because determining the correct
  observation side is a recurring difficulty for the geometric part. As
  described in Section 3.4.3.2, the side is inferred from the structure
  surrounding a label, but this inference can be unreliable when the surrounding
  structure has not yet been sufficiently mapped.

  Each vehicle sends its own request and keeps its own advice. The distances
  given in the description are measured from the position of the vehicle that
  asked, so two vehicles in different parts of the building do not receive the
  same answer.

  3.4.6.2 What the Guide Is Given

  Two rules define the information given to the guide. Both are deliberate
  design choices.

  First, the guide receives only information that is already known from the map.
  It never receives the position of an unread code, the complete layout of the
  building, or any other information that the team has not discovered through
  observation. Otherwise, the guide could answer using information that is
  unavailable to the vehicles, and its contribution would not reflect the actual
  search process.

  Second, the guide receives facts, not scores. For each zone, it is given the
  number of codes that have been detected but not yet read, the number of shelf
  sections containing boxes that have not yet been observed, whether unexplored
  space is present, the facing direction of the shelves, and the distance from
  the vehicle to the zone. It also receives the number of codes read since the
  beginning of the mission. It is not given the utility or score already
  computed by the geometric selector. This prevents the guide from simply
  reproducing the geometric ranking and allows its advice to provide an
  additional source of information.

  Table 3.10 — Information available to the guide.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │                    Given                    │         Not given         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Number of spotted but unread codes per zone │ Position of any unread    │
  │                                             │ code                      │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Number of shelf sections with boxes that    │ Layout of the building    │
  │ have never been observed                    │                           │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Presence of unexplored space                │ Utility or score of a     │
  │                                             │ zone                      │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Facing direction of the shelves in the zone │ Choice made by the        │
  │                                             │ geometric selector        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Distance from the vehicle to the zone       │ Ground truth of any kind  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Number of codes read since the start of the │                           │
  │  mission                                    │                           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  The state can be presented to the guide in two forms. The first is visual: the
  model receives the current camera image together with a top view of the map
  showing the candidate zones and their numbers. The second is textual: the same
  information is provided as a structured description of each zone, without an
  image. Both forms contain the same facts and differ only in how those facts
  are presented. This allows models that accept images and models that do not to
  be evaluated on the same task.

  3.4.6.3 How the Advice Enters the Decision

  The guide returns a zone defined by a centre and a radius, and optionally an
  approach side. Let $\mathbf{o}\gamma$ be the observed point of candidate 
  $\gamma$, $\sigma\gamma$ its approach side, $(\mathbf{z},\rho)$ the advised
  zone, and $\sigma_a$ the advised side. The bonus used in Equation (3.12) is

  $$
  \beta(\gamma)

  B_z,
  \mathbb{1}!\left[
  \lVert \mathbf{o}\gamma-\mathbf{z}\rVert \leq \rho
  \right]
  +
  B_s,
  \mathbb{1}!\left[
  \sigma\gamma=\sigma_a
  \ \wedge
  \operatorname{kind}(\gamma)\neq\textsc{Explore}
  \right],
  \tag{3.15}
  $$

  where $B_z=10$ is the bonus for being inside the advised zone and $B_s=5$ is
  the additional bonus for matching the advised side. Both distances are
  measured horizontally. Exploration candidates receive the zone bonus but not
  the side bonus because they have no imposed approach direction.

  The guide is never allowed to block the vehicle. A request is sent in the
  background while the vehicle continues its current task. When the answer
  becomes available, it is stored and used from the next decision cycle. A
  minimum interval separates two consecutive requests from the same vehicle, and
  a new request is sent only after the previous request has finished.

  An advice is kept until a new one replaces it. It can therefore be applied to
  several successive decisions, and it may become slightly outdated while the
  map continues to change. This is acceptable because the advice only adds a
  bonus: an outdated suggestion can make a candidate less attractive than it
  should be, but it cannot make an unreachable or already completed target be
  selected.

  If no advice has been received, or if the model returns an answer that does
  not correspond to any of the proposed zones, $\beta(\gamma)$ is set to zero.
  The vehicle then makes its decision using the geometric score alone.

  This asynchronous design makes the guide safe to add to the system. The
  decision loop never waits for the model. A slow response therefore does not
  stop the vehicle, and a missing or invalid response has no effect on the rest
  of the system.

  Algorithm 3.9 — RequestAdvice
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Require: vehicle i, map M, candidate list Γ, minimum interval Δ
  Ensure:  the advice held by vehicle i is updated when one is available

   1:  if the last request from i is more recent than Δ then return
   2:  if a request from i is still running then
   3:      if it has not completed then return
   4:      A ← its result
   5:      if A ≠ ⊥ then store A as the advice of vehicle i
   6:  end if
   7:  Z ← group Γ on a 3 m grid, sort by total utility, keep at most 6, number
  them
   8:  if Z = ∅ then return
   9:  build the state description from M and Z                ▷ facts only, no
  scores
  10:  send the two questions in the background
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  The value of $\lambda$ used in each reported mission, and the effect of the
  guide on the final results, are presented in Chapter 5.

● Bouhadi construit sa conclusion de chapitre conception (sa 4.9) en trois
  mouvements.

  Un. Elle nomme l'idée organisatrice unique du chapitre — chez elle, séparer la
  décision sémantique de la production syntaxique — dit quelle section l'a
  établie, et rappelle la mesure qui a prouvé qu'elle était nécessaire. Elle
  l'appelle explicitement la décision porteuse du chapitre : tout le reste
  s'appuie dessus au lieu de la contourner.

  Deux. Elle parcourt les sections restantes en chaîne causale, une phrase
  chacune, où le problème laissé ouvert par une section motive la suivante. Pas
  un résumé section par section : un enchaînement. Chez elle, le jeu de données
  rend la reformulation possible, le choix des modèles sépare l'effet de la
  formulation de celui d'un modèle, l'entraînement supervisé révèle l'écart que
  le chapitre voulait traiter, cet écart motive la phase suivante, et ainsi de
  suite.

  Trois. Elle retourne les choix de conception en affirmations testables et
  passe la main au chapitre suivant. Sa formule : « les choix faits ici sont des
  affirmations testables, pas seulement plausibles », puis elle les liste, puis
  « le chapitre 5 reprend chacune d'elles ».

  C'est un gabarit directement réutilisable chez toi.

  ⎿  40:parser.add_argument("--part-arret", type=float, default=0.95, dest=
     "part_arret",
     241:               "arret": {"codes_attendus": args.codes_attendus or
     … +3 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

● 3.5 System Operation

  The previous sections described six components separately. This section shows
  how they run together during a mission. The flight controller that executes a
  chosen pose is not described here: it belongs to the vehicle platform rather
  than to the contribution of this work, and it is presented in Chapter 4.

  3.5.1 Three Rhythms

  The components do not run at the same rate, and this is what allows a slow
  component to be present without slowing the mission.

  Table 3.11 — Rates at which the components run.

  ┌──────────────────┬─────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Activity     │        Rate         │             Reason             │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Flight control   │ Vehicle rate        │ The aircraft must be commanded │
  │                  │                     │  continuously                  │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Observation      │ Every cycle, 0.2 s  │ Five observations per second   │
  │                  │                     │ for every vehicle              │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Route re-check   │ Every second cycle  │ Enough to detect a route that  │
  │                  │                     │ has been cut                   │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Decision         │ One vehicle per     │ A decision must never delay    │
  │                  │ cycle               │ the physics                    │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Advice from the  │ At most every 5 s   │ The answer is used when it     │
  │ guide            │ per vehicle         │ arrives                        │
  └──────────────────┴─────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

  A cycle lasts 0.2 s of simulated time. During each cycle, every vehicle that
  is still flying integrates its LiDAR revolution into the map, publishes its
  position, and decodes both camera images. The learned detector is more
  expensive, so it runs for one vehicle per cycle and on one of its two cameras,
  alternating. With three vehicles, each one therefore runs the detector once
  every three cycles.

  At most one vehicle makes a decision per cycle. This is the rule that keeps
  the decision out of the control loop: the other vehicles continue to fly on
  the target they already hold while one of them chooses its next.

  3.5.2 One Cycle

  A cycle follows the same order every time. The map is updated first, so that
  every decision taken afterwards uses the most recent state. Expired claims and
  blacklist entries are then removed. Vehicles that are flying towards a target
  check that their route is still valid, and recompute it if a newly observed
  obstacle has cut it. Finally, one vehicle without a current target selects a
  new one, announces it in the map, and starts flying.

  A vehicle that has reached a reading pose holds it for two seconds, which
  corresponds to ten images at the observation rate. A vehicle that has no
  target and cannot find one waits two seconds before trying again, so that a
  temporary absence of candidates does not consume the decision slot at every
  cycle.

  3.5.3 Ending the Mission

  The team decides for itself when the inventory is finished. Four conditions
  end the mission, and the first one to occur applies.

  Table 3.12 — Termination conditions.

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────┐
  │                         Condition                         │    Value    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ A given share of the expected inventory has been read,    │ 95 %, then  │
  │ followed by a grace period                                │ 60 s        │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ No new identifier has been read for a given delay         │ 120 s       │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Every vehicle still flying is simultaneously without a    │ 30 s        │
  │ target                                                    │             │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ No vehicle is still flying                                │ —           │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────┘

  The first condition assumes that the size of the inventory is known in
  advance, which is realistic for a warehouse where the number of stored items
  is recorded even though their positions are not. The grace period exists
  because the last few codes are often the most expensive: without it the
  mission would stop the instant the share is reached, and with an unlimited
  budget it would keep flying for a single unreachable label.

  The second and third conditions do not use that assumption. They end the
  mission when progress has stopped, either because no new identifier is being
  found or because no vehicle can find anything left to do.

  3.5.4 Behaviour When Conditions Change

  The three situations that a prepared plan cannot absorb are handled by the
  mechanisms already described. None of them is treated as a special case, and
  no component exists to detect them.

  An obstacle appears in an aisle. The LiDAR measures it, and the occupancy
  layer confirms it as solid after a few revolutions. Those cells become
  impassable in the cost grid, the route re-check finds that the current route
  is cut, and a new route is computed. If no route remains, the target is
  abandoned and blacklisted for 120 s, and the vehicle selects another one.

  The warehouse has changed since a previous mission. The map is built from
  nothing at every mission, so a building whose contents have moved presents no
  difficulty. The same mechanism also works within a mission: because occupancy
  accumulates evidence rather than storing the last measurement, an object that
  is removed stops receiving occupied evidence and returns to free once enough
  rays have crossed it.

  A vehicle is lost. It stops publishing its position, its claims expire, and
  the work it was holding becomes available to the others, as described in
  Section 3.4.5.3. The remaining vehicles continue with a larger share of the
  building each.

  ---
  3.6 Conclusion

  This chapter presented the design of a system that takes a complete QR-code
  inventory of a warehouse whose layout is not known in advance. It is built on
  a single organising decision: the plan is replaced by a description that the
  vehicles write themselves while they fly, and every action the system takes is
  derived from that description rather than prepared before take-off. Section
  3.2 established why this was necessary, since no coverage path, visiting order
  or target list can be written for a building that has not yet been described,
  and Section 3.3 stated the consequences that follow from it. Every later
  section builds on this decision rather than around it.

  The remaining sections turned that decision into a concrete system, and each
  one answered a difficulty left open by the previous one. Section 3.4.1
  established what can be known at all, and showed that detecting a label and
  reading it are two functions with ranges that differ by a factor of two; the
  intermediate state between them is what gives the mission something to
  collect. Section 3.4.2 organised those observations into four layers, among
  which oriented coverage is what makes remaining work definable without knowing
  where the labels are. Section 3.4.3 converted those layers into three kinds
  of work written in a single form, so that reading, covering and exploring
  could be compared at all. Section 3.4.4 priced them on one scale where one
  point of utility equals one metre of flight, which removed the need for any
  explicit arbitration between exploring and collecting. Section 3.4.5 kept the
  vehicles apart through the same shared structure, using claims that expire
  rather than a protocol, which is also what makes the loss of a vehicle
  harmless. Section 3.4.6 added the only non-geometric component, an advice that
  is weighted and removable. Section 3.5 showed the whole system running at
  three different rates and deciding for itself when the inventory is complete.

  The choices made here are testable claims rather than plausible arrangements.
  That a system with no pre-planning can complete an inventory in a building it
  has never seen. That a single score is sufficient to balance exploration
  against collection, with no phase and no schedule. That coordination through
  expiring claims keeps several vehicles apart and absorbs the loss of one
  without any component observing their health. That the range of the learned
  detector, rather than that of the decoder, governs how quickly the inventory
  is collected. And that a pre-trained vision-language model, given only what
  the team has itself observed, improves the result over pure geometry — a claim
  that is equally reportable if it turns out to be false. Chapter 4 describes
  how this design was implemented, and Chapter 5 tests each of these claims in
  turn.

● 4.1 Introduction

  Chapter 3 described what the system does and why each component is built the
  way it is. Translating that design into a system that actually flies requires
  choosing concrete tools, fixing the values that were deliberately left out of
  the design, and solving the practical problems that appear when a described
  mechanism meets a real execution environment.

  This chapter has two parts. The first part presents the implementation. It
  begins with the technology stack and the machine on which everything runs,
  then describes the runtime architecture and the simulated warehouse, and then
  covers each component in the same order as Section 3.4, so that a component
  can be found in the same position in both chapters. The second part presents
  the evaluation. It defines the scenarios, the baseline, and the metrics, and
  then reports the results for each of the claims stated in Section 3.6.

  4.2 Technology Stack and Execution Environment

  4.2.1 Technologies and Libraries

  The system is written in Python and runs entirely inside a physics simulator,
  with one autopilot process per vehicle. Table 4.1 lists the technologies used,
  grouped by the role they play.

  Table 4.1 — Technology stack, organised by role.

  ┌────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │          Role          │         Technology         │      Version      │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Physics and rendering  │ NVIDIA Isaac Sim           │ 5.1.0             │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Airframe model and     │ Pegasus Simulator, Iris    │ 5.1               │
  │ simulator bridge       │ quadrotor                  │                   │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │                        │ ArduPilot SITL, one        │                   │
  │ Flight stack           │ arducopter process per     │ 4.6.0-beta1       │
  │                        │ vehicle                    │                   │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Link to the autopilots │ MAVLink through pymavlink  │ 2.4.49            │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Implementation         │ Python                     │ 3.11              │
  │ language               │                            │                   │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Map, geometry and      │ numpy, scipy               │ 1.26.0, 1.15.3    │
  │ numerical work         │                            │                   │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Image handling and     │ OpenCV (headless)          │ 4.11.0            │
  │ camera geometry        │                            │                   │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ QR decoding            │ zxing-cpp, with ZBar as an │ 3.1.1, 0.1.9      │
  │                        │  alternative               │                   │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Learned detection      │ Ultralytics, YOLO11 nano   │ 8.3.253           │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Vision-language guide  │ PyTorch, Transformers,     │ 2.7.0, 5.12.1,    │
  │                        │ bitsandbytes, peft         │ 0.50.2, 0.20.0    │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Figures and mission    │ matplotlib, ffmpeg         │ 3.10.3, 4.4.2     │
  │ videos                 │                            │                   │
  └────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┴───────────────────┘

  Two choices in this table follow directly from the design rather than from
  convenience. ArduPilot runs as a separate process for each vehicle, which
  means the system commands real flight software over a real protocol and never
  writes a position directly into the simulator. And the decoder is a separate
  library from the detector, which keeps the two perception paths independent as
  required in Section 3.4.1.4.

  The versions are pinned. Isaac Sim links against a specific version of numpy,
  and an unconstrained installation of any package that depends on it is enough
  to leave the simulator unable to start. All installations are therefore
  performed against a fixed constraints file.

  4.2.2 Execution Environment

  Every result reported in this document was produced on a single machine,
  described in Table 4.2. The simulator, the three autopilot processes, and the
  system itself all run on it at the same time.

  Table 4.2 — Execution environment.

  ┌───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Component     │                    Specification                    │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CPU               │ Intel Core i9-10900, 10 cores and 20 threads at     │
  │                   │ 2.80 GHz                                            │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ RAM               │ 31 GiB                                              │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ GPU               │ NVIDIA GeForce RTX 2060 SUPER, 8 GB                 │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ GPU driver and    │ 535.288.01, CUDA 12.6                               │
  │ CUDA              │                                                     │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Operating system  │ Ubuntu 22.04.5 LTS, kernel 6.8.0                    │
  └───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The graphics memory is the constraint that shaped the most implementation
  decisions. Eight gigabytes must be shared between the renderer, which produces
  the camera images and the LiDAR returns, the learned detector, and the
  vision-language guide when it is enabled. Three consequences follow, and each
  is discussed in the corresponding section. The detector is the smallest
  variant of its family and runs in half precision, as described in Section
  4.5.3. The guide is quantised to four bits and is never loaded at the same
  time as a second model, as described in Section 4.8. And the detector does not
  run for every vehicle at every cycle, which was already stated as a design
  rule in Section 3.4.1.4 and is what keeps the renderer and the detector from
  competing for the same memory in the same instant.

  The CPU is shared as well, between the simulator, the three autopilots, and
  the decision code. Because the autopilots are real processes with their own
  timing, a delay on this machine is not neutral: an autopilot that stops
  receiving commands does not wait, it continues to fly on its last instruction.
  For this reason no other workload is run during a flight, and every
  measurement reported in Part B was collected on an otherwise idle machine.

  ---
  4.1 Introduction

  Chapter 3 described the system: what each component does, what it produces,
  and why it is built that way. This chapter describes how that design was
  actually built, and how it was tested.

  The first part covers the implementation. It presents the tools that were used
  and the machine everything runs on, then the way the simulator and the flight
  software are connected, then the warehouse in which the missions take place,
  and finally the concrete realisation of each component, including the values
  that Chapter 3 deliberately left aside.

  The second part covers the evaluation. It defines the situations the system
  was placed in, the reference method it is compared against, the quantities
  that were measured, and the results obtained.

  4.2 Technology Stack and Execution Environment

  4.2.1 Technologies and Libraries

  The system runs entirely in simulation. Nothing in it is a model of a drone
  written for this work: the vehicle is a physical body inside a physics engine,
  and it is flown by the same autopilot software that flies a real aircraft.
  This section presents the tools that make that possible, starting from the
  simulator and working outwards to the libraries used by each component.

  NVIDIA Isaac Sim[^1] is a robotics simulator built on the Omniverse platform.
  It provides three things that this work depends on. It runs the rigid-body
  physics at a fixed step, so that a vehicle has real inertia and a command has
  a real delay. It renders the camera images with ray tracing, which is what
  makes a printed QR code legible or illegible in an image depending on
  distance, angle and lighting. And it simulates a rotating LiDAR that returns a
  range for every ray, which is the sensor the shared map is built from. The
  scene itself, including the warehouse, the racks and the cartons, is described
  in the USD format, manipulated through the pxr library.

  Pegasus Simulator[^2] is an extension for Isaac Sim that adds aerial vehicles
  and connects them to real flight software. It contributes the airframe model —
  the rotors, the thrust curve, the drag — and the communication bridge that
  carries sensor readings out of the simulator and motor commands back in.

  The vehicle is the Iris quadrotor, a four-rotor airframe widely used as a
  reference model in drone simulation. Its rotors extend about 0.34 m from its
  centre, which is one of the two terms of the clearance radius used for route
  planning in Section 3.4.2.5, the other being the lateral oscillation measured
  while the vehicle holds a position. Its body is a compact shell, and the two
  reading cameras are mounted below it so that the hull never appears in a
  reading image. The same airframe is declared on both sides of the simulation,
  in Pegasus and in the autopilot, so that the vehicle being flown and the
  vehicle being simulated have identical characteristics.

  ArduPilot[^3] is the autopilot software. It is used here in SITL mode, which
  stands for software in the loop: the complete flight stack runs as an ordinary
  program on the same machine, receiving simulated sensor data instead of real
  ones and producing motor commands as if it were on board. One such process
  runs for each vehicle. This is a deliberate choice rather than a convenience.
  The system never writes a position into the simulator; it sends velocity
  commands to an autopilot, which decides how to achieve them, and the vehicle
  therefore behaves with the delays, the overshoot and the attitude dynamics of
  a real aircraft. The autopilots are used in GUIDED mode, in which an external
  program supplies targets while the autopilot retains attitude control.

  MAVLink is the protocol spoken between the system and the autopilots, through
  the pymavlink[^4] library. Every take-off, every velocity command, and every
  position reading passes through it.

  NumPy[^5] and SciPy[^6] carry all the numerical work. The occupancy grid, the
  coverage layer, the LiDAR point clouds and the candidate scoring are all array
  operations, which is what allows a full LiDAR revolution to be integrated
  into the map in a few milliseconds. SciPy is used in particular for the
  rotation conversions between the several frames the system works in.

  OpenCV[^7] handles everything related to images and camera geometry: colour
  conversion, the intrinsic matrix, the pose of a planar square from its four
  corners, and the drawing of the annotated views used in the figures and the
  mission videos. The headless build is used, since no window is ever opened.

  zxing-cpp[^8] is the QR decoder retained for flight. ZBar[^9] and BoofCV[^10]
  were evaluated on the same images and are kept as alternatives; the comparison
  that led to this choice is reported in Section 4.5.2.

  Ultralytics[^11] provides the training and inference framework for the learned
  detector described in Section 3.4.1.3, in the YOLO11 family. It is used both
  to train the detector on the automatically annotated images and to run it
  during flight.

  PyTorch[^12] is the underlying deep-learning framework for both the detector
  and the vision-language guide. Transformers[^13] loads the guide and its
  processor. bitsandbytes[^14] quantises it to four bits, which is what allows
  it to share eight gigabytes of graphics memory with the renderer. PEFT[^15]
  applies the low-rank adapter used by the trained variant of the guide.

  qrcode and Pillow generate the QR images that are pasted onto the carton faces
  when a warehouse is built, so that the codes read during a mission are
  genuine QR symbols rendered in the scene rather than markers placed by hand.

  Matplotlib[^16] produces the measurement figures, and FFmpeg[^17] encodes the
  mission videos from the images captured during flight. pytest[^18] runs the
  unit tests that check each component in isolation, without starting the
  simulator.

  Table 4.1 — Technology stack, organised by the part of the system it serves.

  ┌──────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │  Part of the system  │         Technology         │       Version       │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Physics, rendering   │ Isaac Sim, USD (pxr)       │ 5.1.0               │
  │ and LiDAR            │                            │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Airframe and         │ Pegasus Simulator, Iris    │ 5.1                 │
  │ simulator bridge     │ quadrotor                  │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Flight software      │ ArduPilot SITL, one        │ 4.6.0-beta1         │
  │                      │ process per vehicle        │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Link to the          │ MAVLink, pymavlink         │ 2.4.49              │
  │ autopilots           │                            │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Implementation       │ Python                     │ 3.11                │
  │ language             │                            │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Map, scoring and     │ NumPy, SciPy               │ 1.26.0, 1.15.3      │
  │ geometry             │                            │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Images and camera    │ OpenCV (headless)          │ 4.11.0              │
  │ geometry             │                            │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ QR decoding          │ zxing-cpp; ZBar and BoofCV │ 3.1.1; 0.1.9        │
  │                      │  as alternatives           │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Learned detection    │ Ultralytics, YOLO11 nano   │ 8.3.253             │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Semantic guide       │ PyTorch, Transformers,     │ 2.7.0, 5.12.1,      │
  │                      │ bitsandbytes, PEFT         │ 0.50.2, 0.20.0      │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Scene generation     │ qrcode, Pillow             │ —                   │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Figures and videos   │ Matplotlib, FFmpeg         │ 3.10.3, 4.4.2       │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Tests                │ pytest                     │ —                   │
  └──────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  The versions in this table are pinned rather than left open. Isaac Sim links
  against a specific version of NumPy, and installing any package that silently
  upgrades it is enough to leave the simulator unable to start. All
  installations are therefore performed against a fixed constraints file.

  4.2.2 Execution Environment

  Every result reported in this document was produced on a single machine,
  described in Table 4.2. The simulator, the autopilot processes and the system
  itself all run on it at the same time.

  Table 4.2 — Execution environment.

  ┌───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Component     │                    Specification                    │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CPU               │ Intel Core i9-10900, 10 cores and 20 threads at     │
  │                   │ 2.80 GHz                                            │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ RAM               │ 31 GiB                                              │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ GPU               │ NVIDIA GeForce RTX 2060 SUPER, 8 GB                 │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ GPU driver and    │ 535.288.01, CUDA 12.6                               │
  │ CUDA              │                                                     │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Operating system  │ Ubuntu 22.04.5 LTS, kernel 6.8.0                    │
  └───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The graphics memory is the constraint that shaped the most implementation
  decisions. Eight gigabytes must be shared between the renderer, which produces
  the camera images and the LiDAR returns, the learned detector, and the
  vision-language guide when it is enabled. Three consequences follow. The
  detector is the smallest variant of its family and runs in half precision, as
  described in Section 4.5.3. The guide is quantised to four bits and is never
  loaded together with a second model, as described in Section 4.8. And the
  detector does not run for every vehicle at every cycle, which keeps the
  renderer and the detector from competing for the same memory at the same
  instant.

  The processor is shared as well, between the simulator, the autopilots and the
  decision code. This has a consequence that does not exist in a purely offline
  system: the autopilots are independent processes with their own timing, so a
  vehicle whose commands arrive late does not wait, it continues on its last
  instruction. For this reason no other workload was run during a flight, and
  every measurement reported in the second part of this chapter was collected on
  an otherwise idle machine.

  [^1]: https://developer.nvidia.com/isaac/sim
  [^2]: https://pegasussimulator.github.io/PegasusSimulator/
  [^3]: https://ardupilot.org
  [^4]: https://github.com/ArduPilot/pymavlink
  [^5]: https://numpy.org
  [^6]: https://scipy.org
  [^7]: https://opencv.org
  [^8]: https://github.com/zxing-cpp/zxing-cpp
  [^9]: https://github.com/mchehab/zbar
  [^10]: https://boofcv.org
  [^11]: https://docs.ultralytics.com
  [^12]: https://pytorch.org
  [^13]: https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers
  [^14]: https://github.com/bitsandbytes-foundation/bitsandbytes
  [^15]: https://huggingface.co/docs/peft
  [^16]: https://matplotlib.org
  [^17]: https://ffmpeg.org
  [^18]: https://pytest.org

  Thought for 52s (ctrl+o to expand)

● 4.1 Introduction

  Chapter 3 described the system: what each component does, what it produces,
  and why it is built that way. This chapter describes how that design was
  actually built, and how it was tested.

  The first part covers the implementation. It presents the tools that were used
  and the machine everything runs on, then the way the simulator and the flight
  software are connected, then the warehouse in which the missions take place,
  and finally the concrete realisation of each component, including the values
  that Chapter 3 deliberately left aside.

  The second part covers the evaluation. It defines the situations the system
  was placed in, the reference method it is compared against, the quantities
  that were measured, and the results obtained.

  4.2 Technology Stack and Execution Environment

  4.2.1 Technologies and Libraries

  The system runs entirely in simulation. Nothing in it is a model of a drone
  written for this work: the vehicle is a physical body inside a physics engine,
  and it is flown by the same autopilot software that flies a real aircraft.
  This section presents the tools that make that possible, starting from the
  simulator and working outwards to the libraries used by each component.

  NVIDIA Isaac Sim[^1] is a robotics simulator built on the Omniverse platform.
  It provides three things that this work depends on. It runs the rigid-body
  physics at a fixed step, so that a vehicle has real inertia and a command has
  a real delay. It renders the camera images with ray tracing, which is what
  makes a printed QR code legible or illegible in an image depending on
  distance, angle and lighting. And it simulates a rotating LiDAR that returns a
  range for every ray, which is the sensor the shared map is built from. The
  scene itself, including the warehouse, the racks and the cartons, is described
  in the USD format, manipulated through the pxr library.

  Pegasus Simulator[^2] is an extension for Isaac Sim that adds aerial vehicles
  and connects them to real flight software. It contributes the airframe model —
  the rotors, the thrust curve, the drag — and the communication bridge that
  carries sensor readings out of the simulator and motor commands back in.

  The vehicle is the Iris quadrotor, a four-rotor airframe widely used as a
  reference model in drone simulation. Its rotors extend about 0.34 m from its
  centre, which is one of the two terms of the clearance radius used for route
  planning in Section 3.4.2.5, the other being the lateral oscillation measured
  while the vehicle holds a position. Its body is a compact shell, and the two
  reading cameras are mounted below it so that the hull never appears in a
  reading image. The same airframe is declared on both sides of the simulation,
  in Pegasus and in the autopilot, so that the vehicle being flown and the
  vehicle being simulated have identical characteristics.

  ArduPilot[^3] is the autopilot software. It is used here in SITL mode, which
  stands for software in the loop: the complete flight stack runs as an ordinary
  program on the same machine, receiving simulated sensor data instead of real
  ones and producing motor commands as if it were on board. One such process
  runs for each vehicle. This is a deliberate choice rather than a convenience.
  The system never writes a position into the simulator; it sends velocity
  commands to an autopilot, which decides how to achieve them, and the vehicle
  therefore behaves with the delays, the overshoot and the attitude dynamics of
  a real aircraft. The autopilots are used in GUIDED mode, in which an external
  program supplies targets while the autopilot retains attitude control.

  MAVLink is the protocol spoken between the system and the autopilots, through
  the pymavlink[^4] library. Every take-off, every velocity command, and every
  position reading passes through it.

  NumPy[^5] and SciPy[^6] carry all the numerical work. The occupancy grid, the
  coverage layer, the LiDAR point clouds and the candidate scoring are all array
  operations, which is what allows a full LiDAR revolution to be integrated
  into the map in a few milliseconds. SciPy is used in particular for the
  rotation conversions between the several frames the system works in.

  OpenCV[^7] handles everything related to images and camera geometry: colour
  conversion, the intrinsic matrix, the pose of a planar square from its four
  corners, and the drawing of the annotated views used in the figures and the
  mission videos. The headless build is used, since no window is ever opened.

  zxing-cpp[^8] is the QR decoder retained for flight. ZBar[^9] and BoofCV[^10]
  were evaluated on the same images and are kept as alternatives; the comparison
  that led to this choice is reported in Section 4.5.2.

  Ultralytics[^11] provides the training and inference framework for the learned
  detector described in Section 3.4.1.3, in the YOLO11 family. It is used both
  to train the detector on the automatically annotated images and to run it
  during flight.

  PyTorch[^12] is the underlying deep-learning framework for both the detector
  and the vision-language guide. Transformers[^13] loads the guide and its
  processor. bitsandbytes[^14] provides the four-bit quantisation used for the
  guide. PEFT[^15] applies the low-rank adapter used by the trained variant of
  the guide.

  qrcode and Pillow generate the QR images that are pasted onto the carton faces
  when a warehouse is built, so that the codes read during a mission are
  genuine QR symbols rendered in the scene rather than markers placed by hand.

  Matplotlib[^16] produces the measurement figures, and FFmpeg[^17] encodes the
  mission videos from the images captured during flight. pytest[^18] runs the
  unit tests that check each component in isolation, without starting the
  simulator.

  Table 4.1 — Technology stack, organised by the part of the system it serves.

  ┌──────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │  Part of the system  │         Technology         │       Version       │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Physics, rendering   │ Isaac Sim, USD (pxr)       │ 5.1.0               │
  │ and LiDAR            │                            │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Airframe and         │ Pegasus Simulator, Iris    │ 5.1                 │
  │ simulator bridge     │ quadrotor                  │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Flight software      │ ArduPilot SITL, one        │ 4.6.0-beta1         │
  │                      │ process per vehicle        │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Link to the          │ MAVLink, pymavlink         │ 2.4.49              │
  │ autopilots           │                            │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Implementation       │ Python                     │ 3.11                │
  │ language             │                            │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Map, scoring and     │ NumPy, SciPy               │ 1.26.0, 1.15.3      │
  │ geometry             │                            │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Images and camera    │ OpenCV (headless)          │ 4.11.0              │
  │ geometry             │                            │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ QR decoding          │ zxing-cpp; ZBar and BoofCV │ 3.1.1; 0.1.9        │
  │                      │  as alternatives           │                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Learned detection    │ Ultralytics, YOLO11 nano   │ 8.3.253             │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Semantic guide       │ PyTorch, Transformers,     │ 2.7.0, 5.12.1,      │
  │                      │ bitsandbytes, PEFT         │ 0.50.2, 0.20.0      │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Scene generation     │ qrcode, Pillow             │ —                   │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Figures and videos   │ Matplotlib, FFmpeg         │ 3.10.3, 4.4.2       │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Tests                │ pytest                     │ —                   │
  └──────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  Package versions are pinned through a constraints file, since Isaac Sim
  requires a specific version of NumPy.

  4.2.2 Execution Environment

  The simulator, the autopilot processes and the system itself run on a single
  machine, whose specifications are given in Table 4.2.

  Table 4.2 — Execution environment.

  4.2.2 Execution Environment

  machine, whose specifications are given in Table 4.2.

  Table 4.2 — Execution environment.

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

● 4.3 Runtime Architecture

  4.3.1 Processes and the Link Between Them

  A mission is not one program. It runs as a set of processes on the same
  machine, and the way they are connected determines how the whole system
  behaves in time.

  The simulator process holds almost everything. It contains the scene, the
  physics, the rendering, and the Python code implementing the components of
  Chapter 3: perception, the shared map, candidate generation, target selection,
  coordination, and the flight controller. It is a single process running a
  single loop.

  The autopilot processes are separate. One instance of the ArduPilot flight
  stack runs for each vehicle, launched automatically when the scene is built.
  Each one has its own internal state, its own control loops, and its own
  timing, and none of them is aware that it is being simulated.

  The two sides exchange information over MAVLink, on a UDP port assigned per
  vehicle from its index. Two different kinds of information cross this
  boundary, in opposite directions and at different rates. From the simulator to
  the autopilot go the simulated sensor readings — attitude, angular rates,
  accelerations, position — which the autopilot treats exactly as it would treat
  those of a real aircraft. From the system to the autopilot go the mission
  commands: arm, take off, and then a stream of velocity setpoints. From the
  autopilot back to the simulator come the motor commands, which Pegasus
  converts into forces applied to the rigid body.

  This separation has a consequence that shapes the rest of the architecture.
  The autopilots are independent processes that run continuously, whereas the
  system is a sequential loop. When the loop is busy computing a route or
  running the detector, the autopilots do not wait: they keep flying the
  aircraft on the last command they received. The system is therefore designed
  so that no single step of the loop can take long, which is the reason for the
  scheduling rules described below.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 4.1 — fig_architecture_execution
  ▎
  ▎ Processes of a mission and the information exchanged between them.

  4.3.2 The Simulation Loop

  The loop advances the world in fixed increments called cycles. A cycle is the
  unit in which everything above the autopilot is expressed: one cycle produces
  one observation and at most one decision.

  Table 4.3 — Timing of the simulation.

  ┌───────────────────┬───────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Quantity      │       Value       │             Origin             │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Physics step      │ 1/800 s           │ Required by the ArduPilot      │
  │                   │                   │ backend to exchange at 250 Hz  │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Physics steps per │ 160               │ —                              │
  │  cycle            │                   │                                │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cycle duration    │ 0.2 s of          │ 160 steps at 1/800 s           │
  │                   │ simulated time    │                                │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cycles per second │ 5                 │ —                              │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Rendered images   │ 1                 │ The last step of the cycle is  │
  │ per cycle         │                   │ the rendered one               │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Observation rate  │ 5 per second and  │ One image per camera per cycle │
  │                   │ per vehicle       │                                │
  └───────────────────┴───────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

  The physics step is not a free parameter. The autopilot backend must exchange
  with the flight software at 250 Hz, and the simulator must therefore advance
  in steps small enough to sustain that rate. Rendering, on the other hand, is
  not performed at every physics step: only the last step of each cycle is
  rendered, which fixes the observation rate at five images per second for each
  camera.

  Each cycle executes the same sequence of phases, always in the same order.

  1. Command. Each vehicle checks the right-of-way rule of Section 3.4.5.2. A
  vehicle that must give way stores its current target aside and holds its
  position; the others send their next velocity setpoint to their autopilot.
  2. Physics. The world advances by 159 steps without rendering, followed by one
  step with rendering.
  3. Scene events. If the scenario includes an obstacle appearing at a given
  time, it is added to the scene at this point, exactly like any other object.
  4. Observation. Each vehicle that is still flying integrates its LiDAR
  revolution into the map, publishes its position, and decodes both camera
  images. The learned detector runs for one vehicle only, selected by the cycle
  index.
  5. Ageing. Expired claims and expired blacklist entries are removed from the
  map, and vehicles that have stopped reporting are identified.
  6. Loss detection. A vehicle whose altitude or attitude indicates that it is
  no longer flying is marked as lost and its claims are released.
  7. Decision. At most one vehicle selects a new target, announces it, and
  receives its route.
  8. Termination. The conditions of Section 3.5.3 are evaluated, and the journal
  is updated.

  Two of these phases are deliberately limited. Only one vehicle decides per
  cycle, so that the cost of a decision is never multiplied by the size of the
  team. And the detector runs for one vehicle per cycle, so that with three
  vehicles each one obtains a detection every three cycles, that is roughly
  twice per second.

  4.3.3 Time

  Two clocks coexist during a mission and must not be confused.

  Simulated time is the time experienced by the vehicles. It advances by exactly
  0.2 s per cycle, regardless of how long that cycle actually takes to compute.
  Every duration reported in this document — mission length, time of a failure,
  time an obstacle appears, expiry delays — is expressed in simulated time.

  Wall-clock time is the time the machine takes. It is recorded for each mission
  but is never used by the system, since no component reads it. The ratio
  between the two depends on the number of vehicles and on whether the detector
  and the guide are enabled.

  A third timing detail belongs here because it crosses the boundary between the
  simulator and the system. The renderer delivers an image one step behind the
  physics. The perception component therefore pairs each image with the pose and
  the point cloud of the previous instant, as described in Section 3.4.1.4.
  This correction is made in the system, not in the simulator, because it is a
  property of the rendering pipeline rather than of the vehicle.

  4.3.4 Concurrency

  The loop is sequential. Every phase listed above completes before the next one
  begins, and the components never run in parallel with one another. This is a
  deliberate simplification: with a single loop, the state of the map at the
  moment of a decision is unambiguous, and no locking is needed anywhere.

  One exception exists. The semantic guide runs in a background thread, so that
  the loop can continue while the model produces its answer. The result is
  collected at a later cycle, as described in Section 3.4.6.3. This is the only
  concurrency in the system, and it is confined to a component that can be
  removed entirely.

  Finally, the shared map is a single Python object held in the simulator
  process, and the vehicles access it directly. No network is simulated between
  them, and no message is serialised. This is an assumption of the present work
  rather than a property of the design: the map is written and read through a
  narrow interface — observations in, state out — so that replacing it by a
  distributed structure would not change any of the components described in
  Chapter 3. Nothing in the evaluation, however, tests that replacement.

  ---
  Description de la figure

  Figure 4.1 — fig_architecture_execution

  Sujet. Les processus qui composent une mission, et ce qui circule entre eux.

  Ce qui est représenté. Deux blocs de nature différente.

  Le premier est le processus du simulateur. Il contient la scène et la
  physique, le rendu des caméras et du lidar, et l'ensemble du code du système :
  la perception, la carte partagée, la génération de candidats, la sélection,
  la coordination et le contrôleur de vol. Un seul processus, une seule boucle.

  Le second est répété autant de fois qu'il y a de drones : un processus
  d'autopilote par véhicule, chacun avec ses propres boucles de commande et son
  propre rythme.

  Entre les deux, le lien MAVLink, avec les trois flux distincts qui
  l'empruntent. Du simulateur vers l'autopilote, les mesures des capteurs
  simulés. Du système vers l'autopilote, les commandes de mission et les
  consignes de vitesse. De l'autopilote vers le simulateur, les commandes
  moteur, converties en forces appliquées au corps rigide.

  Le guide sémantique apparaît à part, relié au processus du simulateur par un
  lien marqué comme asynchrone, puisqu'il est le seul élément qui ne s'exécute
  pas dans la boucle.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Que le système ne déplace jamais un drone
  directement : il commande un autopilote qui vole l'appareil. Et que les
  autopilotes tournent en continu, indépendamment de la boucle, ce qui explique
  pourquoi cette boucle est conçue pour qu'aucune de ses étapes ne soit longue.

  Ce qui n'y figure pas. Aucun numéro de port, aucun nom de fichier, aucune
  durée.

  ---
  4.3 Runtime Architecture

  A mission does not run as a single program. It runs as a stack of layers
  distributed over two kinds of process: one simulator process that hosts the
  scene and the system, and one flight-software process per vehicle. Figure 4.1
  shows the complete infrastructure, and the layers are described below from the
  physical world upwards.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 4.1 — fig_architecture_execution
  ▎
  ▎ Runtime architecture of the system.

  4.3.1 The Layers

  Layer 1 — Simulation. Isaac Sim holds the world. The building, the racks, the
  cartons and the QR panels are described in a USD scene, the rigid-body physics
  advances at a fixed step, and the renderer produces the camera images and the
  LiDAR ranges. This layer is the source of every measurement the system will
  ever receive, and the destination of every force produced by the vehicles.

  Layer 2 — Vehicles. Pegasus Simulator instantiates one Iris quadrotor for each
  vehicle and attaches its sensors: two lateral cameras for reading, one
  forward camera, and one rotating LiDAR. It also provides the bridge that
  carries simulated sensor readings out to the flight layer and motor commands
  back into the physics.

  Layer 3 — Flight. One ArduPilot SITL process runs for each vehicle, connected
  to the bridge by MAVLink over a UDP port assigned from the vehicle index. Each
  process receives the simulated sensors and the mission commands, and returns
  motor commands. The vehicles are flown in GUIDED mode, in which the system
  supplies velocity setpoints while the autopilot retains attitude control.

  Layer 4 — System. This layer contains the components described in Chapter 3,
  and it is the only layer written for this work. Perception converts images and
  LiDAR revolutions into facts, the shared map stores them, candidate
  generation turns them into available actions, target selection chooses one,
  coordination keeps the vehicles apart, and the flight controller converts a
  chosen pose into velocity setpoints sent down to Layer 3. All of it runs
  inside the simulator process.

  Layer 5 — Models. The two learned elements are loaded on the graphics
  processor and called by Layer 4: the detector that finds labels and cartons in
  an image, and the vision-language guide that advises on the next area. The
  detector is called during the observation phase; the guide is called
  separately and its answer is collected when it becomes available. This layer
  is the only one that can be removed without changing the others, since the
  guide is optional and the geometric system remains complete without it.

  Layer 6 — Outputs. Everything a mission produces is written to disk: the
  mission journal with its events and its reading counts over time, the final
  state of the shared map, the trajectories of the vehicles, the images captured
  by the fixed cameras, the videos assembled from them, and the measurement
  figures. Nothing in this layer is read back by the system during the mission.

  4.3.2 Execution Flow

  The numbered steps below follow one cycle of a mission through the layers, and
  correspond to the numbers in Figure 4.1.

  1. The system sends a velocity setpoint to the autopilot of each vehicle that
  is free to move.
  2. The autopilot converts that setpoint into motor commands.
  3. Pegasus applies the resulting forces to the rigid body of the vehicle.
  4. Isaac Sim advances the physics and renders the last step of the cycle.
  5. The new sensor readings are returned to the autopilots, which close their
  own control loops.
  6. Perception reads the two camera images and the LiDAR revolution of the
  vehicles that are flying.
  7. The detector is invoked on one image, and the decoder on both.
  8. The resulting observations are written into the shared map.
  9. Expired claims and blacklist entries are removed from the map.
  10. One vehicle generates its candidates from the map, scores them, selects
  one, and announces its claim.
  11. The flight controller receives the chosen pose and its route, and produces
  the setpoints used at step 1 of the next cycle.
  12. The guide, when enabled, is queried in the background and its answer is
  stored for a later decision.
  13. The journal, the map snapshots and the captured images are written to the
  output layer.

  A cycle covers 0.2 s of simulated time and produces one rendered image per
  camera, which fixes the observation rate at five per second for each vehicle.
  At most one vehicle performs step 10 in a given cycle, so the cost of a
  decision does not grow with the size of the team.

  ---

  Figure 4.1 — fig_architecture_execution

  sorties en haut, avec les numéros du flux d'exécution portés sur les liens.

  La couche de simulation contient la scène USD, la physique et le rendu des
  caméras et du lidar. La couche des véhicules contient les quadrirotors avec
  leurs capteurs. La couche de vol contient un processus d'autopilote par
  véhicule, reliés à la couche des véhicules par MAVLink. La couche système

  doit être visible : elle englobe la couche de simulation, la couche des
  véhicules et la couche système, mais pas les processus d'autopilote. La couche
  des modèles contient le détecteur et le guide, sur le processeur graphique,
  le guide étant marqué comme optionnel et asynchrone. La couche des sorties
  contient le journal, la carte finale, les trajectoires et les vidéos.

  Les treize étapes du flux d'exécution sont numérotées sur les liens qu'elles
  empruntent, de la consigne de vitesse envoyée à l'autopilote jusqu'à
  l'écriture des sorties.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Que le système ne déplace jamais un drone
  directement, et que la seule couche écrite pour ce travail est la couche
  système ; tout ce qui est en dessous existait déjà.

  Ce qui n'y figure pas. Aucun numéro de port, aucun nom de fichier, aucune

● 4.3 Runtime Architecture

  A mission does not run as a single program. It runs as a stack of layers
  distributed over two kinds of process: one simulator process that hosts the
  scene and the system itself, and one flight-software process for each vehicle.
  Figure 4.1 shows the complete infrastructure. The layers are described below
  from the physical world upwards, each one presented together with what it
  receives and what it passes on.

  Simulation. Isaac Sim holds the world. The building, the racks, the cartons
  and the QR panels are described in a USD scene, the rigid-body physics
  advances at a fixed step, and the renderer produces the camera images and the
  LiDAR ranges. This layer receives forces from the vehicles above it and
  returns measurements to them. It is the source of everything the system will
  ever know, and nothing bypasses it.

  Vehicles. Pegasus Simulator instantiates one Iris quadrotor for each vehicle
  and attaches its sensors: two lateral cameras for reading, one forward camera,
  and one rotating LiDAR. It passes the simulated sensor readings up to the
  flight layer and converts the motor commands it receives back into forces
  applied to the rigid body.

  Flight. One ArduPilot SITL process runs for each vehicle, connected to Pegasus
  by MAVLink over a UDP port assigned from the vehicle index. It receives the
  simulated sensors and closes its own attitude loops, and it receives the
  mission commands from the system above: arm, take off, and then a stream of
  velocity setpoints. What it returns downwards is motor commands. The vehicles
  are flown in GUIDED mode, in which the system supplies targets while the
  autopilot keeps control of the attitude.

  System. This layer holds the components described in Chapter 3, and it is the
  only layer written for this work. Perception reads the camera images and the
  LiDAR revolutions and turns them into facts; the shared map stores those
  facts; candidate generation turns the map into the actions currently
  available; target selection picks one; coordination keeps the vehicles from
  choosing the same one; and the flight controller converts the chosen pose into
  the velocity setpoints sent down to the flight layer. All of it runs inside
  the simulator process, in a single loop.

  Models. The two learned elements are loaded on the graphics processor and
  called by the system layer. The detector is invoked on one camera image while
  an observation is being processed, and returns the labels and cartons it
  finds. The semantic guide is invoked separately and in the background, and its
  answer is collected at a later moment and applied to the next decision. This
  is the only layer that can be removed without altering the others, since the
  geometric system is complete without the guide.

  Outputs. Everything a mission produces is written to disk: the journal of
  events and reading counts over time, the final state of the shared map, the
  trajectories, the images captured by the fixed cameras and the videos
  assembled from them. Nothing in this layer is read back by the system while
  the mission runs.

  The loop that drives this stack advances the world in cycles of 0.2 s of
  simulated time. Within one cycle the system sends its setpoints, the physics
  advances, a single image is rendered for each camera, the vehicles that are
  flying observe and write into the map, expired claims are removed, and one
  vehicle — never more — selects its next target. The observation rate is
  therefore five per second for each vehicle, and the cost of a decision does
  not grow with the size of the team.

  La couche de simulation contient la scène, la physique et le rendu des caméras
  et du lidar. La couche des véhicules contient les quadrirotors avec leurs
  capteurs. La couche de vol contient un processus d'autopilote par véhicule. La
  couche système contient les composants du chapitre 3. La couche des modèles
  contient le détecteur et le guide, sur le processeur graphique. La couche des
  sorties contient le journal, la carte finale, les trajectoires et les vidéos.

  La frontière du processus simulateur doit être visible : elle englobe la
  simulation, les véhicules, le système et les modèles, mais pas les processus
  d'autopilote, qui sont dehors et reliés par MAVLink.

  Chaque lien entre deux couches porte ce qui y circule, dans le sens où il
  circule : les forces vers le bas et les mesures vers le haut entre la
  simulation et les véhicules ; les capteurs simulés vers le haut et les
  commandes moteur vers le bas entre les véhicules et le vol ; les consignes de
  vitesse vers le bas entre le système et le vol ; les images et les tours de
  lidar vers le haut entre les véhicules et le système. Le lien vers le guide
  porte une marque distincte, parce qu'il est asynchrone.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Que le système ne déplace jamais un drone
  directement, et que la seule couche écrite pour ce travail est la couche
