● 4.3 Runtime Architecture

  A mission does not run as a single program. It runs as a stack of layers
  distributed over two kinds of process: one simulator process that hosts the
  world and the system itself, and one flight-software process for each vehicle.
  Figure 4.1 shows the complete infrastructure. The layers are described below
  from the physical world upwards, each one presented together with what it
  receives and what it passes on.

  Simulation. Isaac Sim holds the world. The scene is described in the USD
  format, the rigid-body physics advances at a fixed step, the renderer produces
  the camera images, and a rotating LiDAR sensor returns a range for every ray.
  This layer receives forces from the vehicles above it and returns
  measurements to them. It is the source of everything the system will ever
  know, and nothing bypasses it.

  The building itself is generated rather than fixed, and this is what makes an
  unseen warehouse possible. A single integer, the seed, determines a complete
  warehouse. The interior is 19.34 m wide and 29.80 m long, with a ceiling at
  9.00 m and a usable flight height of 5.80 m. It contains three racks, each
  1.40 m deep, 17.62 m long and 6.00 m high, with three shelf levels at 1.325 m,
  2.825 m and 4.125 m. From the seed are drawn the lateral position of each
  rack, subject to a minimum aisle width of 3.00 m and a margin of 0.60 m from
  the walls, the longitudinal offset at which the racks begin, the fraction of
  shelf positions that actually hold a carton, drawn between one half and the
  whole, and the take-off positions of the vehicles. Cartons that are not
  retained are disabled entirely: they are neither rendered nor physically
  present, so the LiDAR does not see an object the cameras cannot.

  Each retained carton receives a QR panel on two opposite faces, generated as a
  real QR symbol and applied as a textured quad 0.01 m in front of the carton
  surface, with a side of 0.40 m. The same identifier therefore appears twice in
  the building, which is the property the label table of Section 3.4.2.4 is
  built to handle. In the warehouse used for the nominal scenario, this produces
  228 panels carrying 114 distinct identifiers.

  Table 4.3 — The building, and what the seed varies.

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │               Quantity               │          Value or range          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Interior width                       │ 19.34 m                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Interior length                      │ 29.80 m                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Ceiling height                       │ 9.00 m                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Usable flight height                 │ 5.80 m                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Number of racks                      │ 3                                │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Rack dimensions                      │ 1.40 m deep, 17.62 m long, 6.00  │
  │                                      │ m high                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Shelf levels                         │ 1.325 m, 2.825 m, 4.125 m        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Minimum aisle width                  │ 3.00 m                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Lateral position of each rack        │ Drawn from the seed              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Longitudinal offset of the racks     │ Drawn from the seed              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Fraction of shelf positions holding  │ Drawn from the seed, between 0.5 │
  │ a carton                             │  and 1.0                         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Take-off positions                   │ Drawn from the seed              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ QR panel side                        │ 0.40 m                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Panels per carton                    │ 2, on opposite faces             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  Vehicles. Pegasus Simulator instantiates one Iris quadrotor for each vehicle
  and attaches its sensors: two lateral cameras of 1024 × 768 pixels with a 60°
  field of view, one low-resolution forward camera, and one rotating LiDAR. It
  passes the simulated sensor readings up to the flight layer and converts the
  motor commands it receives back into forces applied to the rigid body.

  Flight. One ArduPilot SITL process runs for each vehicle, connected to Pegasus
  by MAVLink over a UDP port assigned from the vehicle index. It receives the
  simulated sensors and closes its own attitude loops, and it receives the
  mission commands from the system above: arm, take off, and then a stream of
  velocity setpoints. What it returns downwards is motor commands. The vehicles
  are flown in GUIDED mode, in which the system supplies targets while the
  autopilot keeps control of the attitude.

  System. This layer holds the components described in Chapter 3, and it is the
  only layer written for this work. Perception reads the camera images and the
  LiDAR revolutions and turns them into facts; the shared map stores those
  facts; candidate generation turns the map into the actions currently
  available; target selection picks one; coordination keeps the vehicles from
  choosing the same one; and the flight controller converts the chosen pose into
  the velocity setpoints sent down to the flight layer. All of it runs inside
  the simulator process, in a single loop.

  Models. The two learned elements are loaded on the graphics processor and
  called by the system layer. The detector is invoked on one camera image while
  an observation is being processed, and returns the labels and cartons it
  finds. The semantic guide is invoked separately and in the background, and its
  answer is collected at a later moment and applied to the next decision. This
  is the only layer that can be removed without altering the others, since the
  geometric system is complete without the guide.

  Outputs. Everything a mission produces is written to disk: the journal of
  events and reading counts over time, the final state of the shared map, the
  trajectories, the images captured by the fixed cameras and the videos
  assembled from them. Nothing in this layer is read back by the system while
  the mission runs.

  The loop that drives this stack advances the world in cycles of 0.2 s of
  simulated time. Within one cycle the system sends its setpoints, the physics
  advances, a single image is rendered for each camera, the vehicles that are
  flying observe and write into the map, expired claims are removed, and one
  vehicle — never more — selects its next target. The observation rate is
  therefore five per second for each vehicle, and the cost of a decision does
  not grow with the size of the team.

  ---
  Description de la figure

  Figure 4.1 — fig_architecture_execution

  Sujet. L'infrastructure complète sur laquelle une mission s'exécute,
  décomposée jusqu'aux éléments réels et non en gros blocs.

  Les éléments à faire apparaître, couche par couche.

  Simulation : la scène USD, le générateur d'entrepôt piloté par une graine, le
  moteur physique, le moteur de rendu, et le capteur lidar. Le générateur doit
  être distinct de la scène, puisqu'il la produit avant la mission et
  n'intervient plus ensuite.

  Véhicules : pour un drone, séparément, la cellule Iris, la caméra latérale
  gauche, la caméra latérale droite, la caméra frontale, le lidar, et le pont
  Pegasus. Le drone est répété N fois.

  Vol : un processus autopilote par drone, avec à l'intérieur la boucle
  d'attitude et le mode GUIDED. Les processus sont répétés N fois et se trouvent
  hors de la frontière du processus simulateur.

  Système : les six composants séparément, pas un seul bloc — la perception, la
  carte partagée, la génération de candidats, la sélection de cible, la
  coordination, le contrôleur de vol. La carte partagée est unique, les cinq
  autres existent par drone.

  Modèles : le détecteur appris et le guide sémantique, distincts l'un de
  l'autre.

  Sorties : le journal de mission, la carte finale, les trajectoires, les images
  et les vidéos, séparément.

  Les liens à porter, avec ce qui y circule.

  Générateur → scène : le bâtiment, les racks, les cartons retenus, les panneaux
  QR. Physique ↔ cellule : forces vers le bas, état du corps vers le haut.
  Rendu → caméras et lidar → perception : images et tours de lidar. Pegasus ↔
  autopilote : capteurs simulés vers le haut, commandes moteur vers le bas.
  Contrôleur de vol → autopilote : consignes de vitesse. Perception → carte.
  Carte → génération de candidats, et carte → coordination. Génération →
  sélection → contrôleur de vol. Sélection → carte : la revendication. Détecteur
  ↔ perception. Guide ↔ sélection, ce lien étant le seul marqué asynchrone.
  Système → sorties.

  La frontière à montrer. Le processus simulateur englobe la simulation, les
  véhicules, le système et les modèles. Les autopilotes sont dehors, reliés par
  MAVLink.

  Ce que le lecteur doit comprendre. Que le système ne déplace jamais un drone
  directement mais commande un autopilote, que la carte partagée est le seul
  élément unique là où tout le reste est répété par drone, et que la seule
  couche écrite pour ce travail est la couche système.

● 4.4 Implementation of the Perception Component

  4.4.1 Sensor Configuration

  Each vehicle carries three cameras and one LiDAR. The two lateral cameras are
  the reading instruments: they are mounted 0.11 m below the body so that the
  hull never enters the frame, and they point ninety degrees to the left and to
  the right of the heading, which is what allows a single pass along an aisle to
  serve both racks. The forward camera is low-resolution and is never used for
  decoding; it provides the image shown to the semantic guide.

  The intrinsic matrix is read from the simulator rather than computed from the
  field of view. The two agree — a focal length of 886.81 pixels for a 60° field
  of view at 1024 pixels wide — but the simulator is the authority, since it is
  the renderer that determines how a panel is projected, and every distance
  recovered from four corners depends on that matrix.

  The LiDAR is configured as a full revolution produced at each render rather
  than as a mechanically rotating sensor, because it is used to build a map and
  not to imitate a physical instrument. The angular resolution is 2° in both
  directions, which gives 3420 rays per revolution over 360° horizontally and
  36° vertically.

  That resolution is a measured choice rather than a default. At 4°, the top
  surface of a shelf 24 cm below the vehicle was only touched 1.4 m ahead of it,
  which was enough for a vehicle to fly over a shelf board in an empty rack
  before the map contained it. At 2°, the same board is seen from 3.4 m.

  Table 4.4 — Sensor configuration.

  ┌────────────────┬────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Sensor     │     Parameter      │               Value               │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Lateral        │ Number             │ 2, pointing left and right        │
  │ cameras        │                    │                                   │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Resolution         │ 1024 × 768                        │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Field of view      │ 60°                               │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Focal length       │ 886.81 px                         │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Mounting           │ 0.11 m below the body             │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Forward camera │ Resolution         │ 160 × 120                         │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Use                │ Image supplied to the semantic    │
  │                │                    │ guide                             │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ LiDAR          │ Horizontal field   │ 360°                              │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Vertical field     │ 36°                               │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Angular resolution │ 2° horizontally and vertically    │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Rays per           │ 3420                              │
  │                │ revolution         │                                   │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Range              │ 0.4 m to 25.0 m                   │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Revolutions        │ One per rendered frame            │
  └────────────────┴────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  Two of these values are bounds rather than capabilities. The minimum range of
  0.4 m excludes the propellers, without which a vehicle measures itself. And
  although the sensor reaches 25 m, only the first 8 m of each ray is written
  into the map, for the reason given in Section 3.4.1.2: beyond that distance
  two neighbouring rays are further apart than one map cell, and the free space
  they carve out becomes perforated.

  4.4.2 Choice of the QR Decoder

  Section 3.4.1 states that the reading envelope is the most structural quantity
  in the system. That envelope is a property of the decoder as much as of the
  camera, so the decoder was chosen by measurement rather than by reputation.

  The bench recorded 3481 images, each stored with the exact pose of the camera,
  across five campaigns: 2000 poses drawn at random with a free camera, 300
  poses in a warehouse emptied of all its codes, 400 poses in a second
  warehouse, 12 positions held by an actual vehicle in flight, and four
  traversals of a rack at different speeds. Six decoders then analysed exactly
  the same grayscale images.

  Table 4.5 — Decoders compared on the same 3481 images.

  ┌───────────────────────┬───────────────┬─────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │        Decoder        │  Successful   │  Apparent   │ Median distance  │
  │                       │     reads     │    range    │      error       │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ ZBar                  │        91.2 % │      4.50 m │           0.4 cm │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ zxing-cpp             │        90.2 % │      3.65 m │           0.8 cm │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ BoofCV                │        87.2 % │      4.50 m │           1.0 cm │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ OpenCV QRCodeDetector │        80.8 % │      1.65 m │           1.0 cm │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ OpenCV ArUco, ×3      │        79.8 % │           — │           1.6 cm │
  │ upscaling             │               │             │                  │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ OpenCV ArUco          │        79.3 % │           — │           1.8 cm │
  └───────────────────────┴───────────────┴─────────────┴──────────────────┘

  zxing-cpp is retained, at 17 ms per image, with ZBar kept as an immediate
  alternative; the two are separated by one point. The OpenCV family, which is
  the usual default in robotics code, loses eleven points and more than half the
  apparent range, which would have reduced the envelope from 4.0 m to 1.65 m
  and changed the geometry of every reading pose in the system.

  Two restrictions are applied to the decoder's output. It is limited to the QR
  format alone, because the warehouse decor carries barcodes that a general
  reader would enter into the inventory. And every decoded string is matched
  against the expected identifier pattern, since the building also carries QR
  codes on its signage. On the 300 images of the emptied warehouse, none of the
  six decoders ever reported a read, which is what allows a decoded identifier
  to be written into the map without any confirmation step.

  4.4.3 The Learned Detector

  The detector is a YOLO11 nano model with 2.58 million parameters, trained at
  an input resolution of 1024 pixels and run in half precision, with two
  classes: qr and carton.

  The training set is produced without manual annotation. In simulation the
  position of every carton and every panel is known, so each object is projected
  into the image from the exact pose of the camera. Projection alone would
  label a hidden object as visible, so visibility is established physically: 25
  points are sampled on each surface, a ray is cast towards each of them, and a
  point is accepted only when the first object the ray reaches is the one that
  owns it. The annotation box encloses the accepted points rather than the whole
  object. An object less than 30 % visible, or 20 % for a carton, is marked as
  ignored and counts neither as an example nor as an error.

  Table 4.6 — Composition of the annotated sets.

  ┌──────────────┬─────────────┬────────┬───────────┬───────────┬─────────┐
  │     Set      │ Warehouses  │ Images │    QR     │ Cartons   │ Ignored │
  │              │             │        │ annotated │ annotated │         │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────┼────────┼───────────┼───────────┼─────────┤
  │ Training     │ 6 (seeds 0  │   3000 │    23 016 │    26 304 │  13 325 │
  │              │ to 5)       │        │           │           │         │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────┼────────┼───────────┼───────────┼─────────┤
  │              │ 2 (seeds    │        │           │           │         │
  │ Sealed test  │ 9033 and    │    800 │     5 917 │     6 495 │   2 580 │
  │              │ 9019)       │        │           │           │         │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────┼────────┼───────────┼───────────┼─────────┤
  │ Re-annotated │ Campaigns   │        │           │           │         │
  │  bench       │ of Section  │   3577 │    19 615 │    24 625 │  14 949 │
  │              │ 4.4.2       │        │           │           │         │
  └──────────────┴─────────────┴────────┴───────────┴───────────┴─────────┘

  The third set is what makes the comparison against the geometric detector
  exact. The images of the decoder bench were re-annotated by the same procedure
  from their recorded poses, so the two detectors are measured on the same
  photographs, at known distances, including the 300 images that contain no code
  at all.

  Two variants were trained and judged at the same threshold, the lowest one
  keeping false alarms below one percent of the empty images.

  Table 4.7 — The two variants, and the geometric detector on the same images.

  ┌───────────────────────────────┬───────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┐
  │          Measurement          │ Geometric │  Learned,    │ Learned, 640 │
  │                               │           │   1024 px    │      px      │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Target panel detected at 6–8  │      47 % │         98 % │         98 % │
  │ m                             │           │              │              │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Range held at 90 % detection  │     4.0 m │        8.0 m │        8.0 m │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Images with no code where one │    27.3 % │        0.7 % │        0.3 % │
  │  is reported                  │           │              │              │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Visible codes found, sealed   │         — │       98.3 % │       94.4 % │
  │ warehouses                    │           │              │              │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Visible cartons found, sealed │         — │       94.1 % │       91.4 % │
  │  warehouses                   │           │              │              │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ False codes per image, sealed │         — │         0.02 │         0.02 │
  │  warehouses                   │           │              │              │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Time per image                │         — │        12 ms │        12 ms │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Graphics memory               │         — │        63 MB │        49 MB │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Training time                 │         — │       48 min │       21 min │
  └───────────────────────────────┴───────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┘

  The 1024 variant is retained. Both hold the same range and the same time per
  image, since at one image per observation the cost is dominated by the
  transfer rather than by the computation, and the larger input finds four more
  points of codes and five more points of cartons.

  The confidence threshold is 0.5 rather than the usual 0.25, and the trade-off
  was measured directly. At 0.25 the detector finds 99.4 % of the visible codes
  but reports one on 2.33 % of the empty images; at 0.5 it finds 98.3 % for 0.67
  %. One point of recall is exchanged for three times fewer phantom targets,
  which is the correct direction when every phantom costs a flight.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 4.2 — fig_portees
  ▎
  ▎ Detection range of the two detectors, measured on the same images.

  ▎ ▸ FIGURE 4.3 — fig_annotation
  ▎
  ▎ Training examples produced without manual annotation.

  4.4.4 Placement Parameters and Filters

  Section 3.4.1.2 describes how an observation is placed in three dimensions and
  which filters reject it. Table 4.8 gives the values those rules use.

  Table 4.8 — Placement parameters.

  ┌─────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │        Parameter        │      Value       │           Role            │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │                         │                  │ Physical side of a panel  │
  │ Panel side, nominal     │ 0.40 m           │ in the generated          │
  │                         │                  │ warehouse                 │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Code side, $c$          │ 0.336 m          │ Panel side times 21/25,   │
  │                         │                  │ from Equation (3.4)       │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Pose from four corners  │ IPPE_SQUARE      │ Formulation dedicated to  │
  │                         │                  │ planar square markers     │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Maximum incidence,      │                  │ Beyond it the four        │
  │ $\alpha_{\max}$         │ 60°              │ corners give an           │
  │                         │                  │ unreliable normal         │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │                         │ $\min(0.25,\     │ Perpendicular distance    │
  │ LiDAR ray tolerance     │ 0.06 + 0.04,r)$  │ accepted between a ray    │
  │                         │ m                │ and a LiDAR point         │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Map takeover distance,  │ 8.0 m            │ Beyond it the range is    │
  │ $r_{\max}$              │                  │ taken from the map        │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Plausibility window,    │                  │ Accepted ratio to the     │
  │ $[\kappa_{lo},          │ $[0.45,\ 1.8]$   │ distance implied by the   │
  │ \kappa_{hi}]$           │                  │ region width              │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Fusion radius,          │                  │ Below it, two             │
  │ $\varepsilon$           │ 0.45 m           │ observations are the same │
  │                         │                  │  panel                    │
  └─────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  Two of these deserve a word. The pose formulation returns two solutions,
  because a planar square seen from a distance is ambiguous; the retained one is
  the solution whose normal agrees with the expected direction when it is
  known, and otherwise the one with the smaller reprojection error. And the
  plausibility window is deliberately wide, because the region returned by the
  detector is larger than the code and may be clipped by an obstruction: its
  purpose is not precision but the rejection of a point placed far behind the
  label by a ray that has passed through a gap in a rack.

  The measured cost of one observation is given in Table 4.9. These values are
  what the scheduling rules of Section 3.4.1.4 are built around.

  Table 4.9 — Measured cost of one observation, per vehicle.

  ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │           Operation            │        Cost        │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ LiDAR integration into the map │              22 ms │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Coverage integration           │               2 ms │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Decoding, both images          │              19 ms │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Learned detection, one image   │ 12 ms in isolation │
  └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  The accuracy that results from this configuration is the figure the shared map
  depends on: a code that has been read is placed with a median error of 0.8 cm
  on the bench, and 1.0 cm in flight.

● 4.5 Implementation of the Map, Planning and Decision

  4.5.1 The Grid and Its Layers

  The shared map covers a volume slightly larger than the building, so that a
  vehicle observing a wall from inside still writes into a valid cell. With a
  resolution of 0.25 m, this gives a grid of 84 × 128 × 24 cells, that is 258
  048 cells in total.

  Three of the four layers of Section 3.4.2 are stored in this grid, and the
  fourth is a list.

  Table 4.10 — Storage of the map.

  ┌──────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────┐
  │      Layer       │             Representation              │    Size    │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Occupancy        │ One 32-bit float per cell, the          │    1.03 MB │
  │                  │ accumulated value $L(c)$                │            │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Oriented         │ One byte per cell, four bits used       │    0.26 MB │
  │ coverage         │                                         │            │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Semantic channel │ One byte per cell                       │    0.26 MB │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Label table and  │ Lists of entries, one per panel, track, │ Negligible │
  │ claims           │  vehicle or claim                       │            │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Total            │                                         │    1.55 MB │
  └──────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────┘

  The coverage layer uses a byte for four bits, which wastes half of it. Packing
  them would save 0.13 MB and would make the test of Equation (3.7) more
  expensive, which is the wrong trade when the whole structure already fits in
  under two megabytes.

  4.5.2 Writing into the Map

  Occupancy. The accumulation of Equation (3.6) uses the values of Table 4.11. A
  free measurement subtracts less than an occupied measurement adds, but a
  single ray deposits free evidence in every cell it crosses and occupied
  evidence in only one, so free space still accumulates faster than obstacles
  along a ray.

  Table 4.11 — Accumulation values.

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │                  Quantity                  │   Value   │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Occupied evidence, $\ell_{\mathrm{solid}}$ │   $+0.85$ │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Free evidence, $\ell_{\mathrm{free}}$      │   $-0.40$ │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Bound applied by $\operatorname{clip}$     │ $\pm 5.0$ │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Occupied threshold, $\tau_{+}$             │    $+1.0$ │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Free threshold, $\tau_{-}$                 │    $-1.0$ │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────┘

  Free evidence is deposited at three points per cell along each ray, and stops
  one full cell before the impact. Both details are described in Section
  3.4.2.2; the values here are what makes them concrete. With a bound of $\pm
  5.0$, a cell needs six consecutive occupied measurements to move from fully
  free to occupied, which is a fraction of a second for a real obstacle and
  never happens for an isolated spurious return.

  Coverage. Integrating the coverage of one camera means answering, for every
  cell in its field of view, whether a code placed there would have been
  readable. The camera cone is sampled at 3° in both directions, and each
  direction is walked in steps of half a cell up to the upper bound of the
  reading envelope. For each sampled cell the apparent distance is computed as
  the distance along the ray divided by the cosine of the angle to the camera
  axis, which is exactly Equation (3.2) evaluated at the edge of the field of
  view rather than at its centre. A cell is marked only if that apparent
  distance falls inside the envelope and if no cell already known to be occupied
  lies in front of it along the same direction; occlusion is resolved in a
  single pass by a cumulative sum along each ray.

  4.5.3 Route Planning

  Planning runs on a horizontal view built from the altitude slice of Section
  3.4.2.5. Obstacles are dilated by a radius expressed in cells without rounding
  up, because rounding 0.6 m to three cells would give 0.75 m and would close
  the narrow aisles of the building. Two different radii are used: 0.6 m for a
  route, which is only crossed, and 0.9 m for an observation pose, which is held
  and around which the vehicle oscillates.

  The search itself is A* on that view, eight-connected, with diagonal moves
  costed at $\sqrt{2}$ and a Euclidean heuristic. Two shortcuts surround it.
  Before searching, the straight line from the start to the goal is tested, and
  if it crosses only cells known to be free the route is returned empty, with no
  waypoint at all. After searching, the resulting cell path is pruned so that
  only the turns are kept: a waypoint is removed whenever the segment joining
  its neighbours remains valid, which turns a staircase of cells into a short
  list of corners.

  One case is treated specially. A vehicle may find itself inside the dilated
  margin of an obstacle without being inside the obstacle, typically after
  holding a pose close to a rack. If the departure cell were simply impassable
  the vehicle could never leave it, so the margin is lifted at the departure
  cell alone, and only when the underlying cell is not itself occupied.

  4.5.4 Verification Before Use

  A map that is wrong does not announce itself. It produces plausible numbers,
  and every component above it inherits the error. The map was therefore
  verified against the physics engine before any measurement was taken, and the
  bench refuses to continue if a check fails.

  Table 4.12 — Checks performed before the map is used.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │                Check                │              Result               │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Every LiDAR ray recomputed by the   │ 0.0 cm median difference, 0 to 3  │
  │ physics engine, at three headings   │ % of rays disagreeing             │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Point cloud compared at 0° and 90°  │ 0.0 cm, the world does not move   │
  │ heading                             │ when the vehicle turns            │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Obstacles reported in open aisles   │ None: every point at flight       │
  │                                     │ height falls on a rack or a wall  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Memory occupied                     │ 1.5 MB for 258 048 cells          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  The first check is the one that earned its place. The LiDAR reports its
  vertical angles counted downwards, and the sensor only refreshes when a frame
  is rendered. Without a comparison against rays cast directly in the physics
  engine, the map would have filled upside down while producing entirely
  normal-looking statistics.

  4.5.5 Decision Scheduling

  Candidate generation, target selection and coordination required almost no
  implementation beyond what Chapter 3 specifies. Two scheduling decisions are
  worth recording.

  The candidate list is identical for the whole team, so it is computed once and
  cached on the map for two seconds of simulated time rather than recomputed by
  each vehicle at each cycle. A vehicle that decides within that window reads
  the cached list and scores it from its own position.

  At most one vehicle performs a decision in a given cycle, and a vehicle that
  has no target and finds none waits two seconds before trying again. Both rules
  exist for the same reason, stated in Section 4.3: the autopilots are
  independent processes that keep flying while the loop computes, so no single
  step of the loop may take long.
